# Global Quantum Technology Standardization Landscape: A Comparative Survey of Major International Standards Organizations as of End-2022
# 1 Introduction, Research Scope, and Quantum Standardization Taxonomy

The transition from laboratory demonstration to large-scale commercial deployment of quantum technologies has, by the close of 2022, made standardization no longer an academic afterthought but a precondition for orderly market formation, security assurance, and cross-border interoperability. Two parallel forces drive this urgency. On one side, advances in quantum computing threaten the cryptographic primitives that underpin virtually every secure digital interaction; on the other, quantum-native technologies—most prominently Quantum Key Distribution (QKD)—have matured to the point where interoperability of equipment from multiple vendors, formal security evaluation, and network-level integration must be governed by shared technical specifications. This chapter establishes the conceptual and methodological foundation for a comparative survey of the global response of five Standards Development Organizations (SDOs)—ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and IRTF—up to the end-2022 cutoff, and constructs the taxonomy that will scaffold all subsequent chapters.

## 1.1 Strategic Significance of Quantum Technology Standardization

The strategic case for quantum technology standardization rests on a dual threat-and-opportunity logic that became unambiguous over the period 2016–2022. The threat dimension is rooted in Shor's algorithm, first published in 1994, which demonstrates that the integer factorization and discrete logarithm problems underpinning RSA, Elliptic Curve Cryptography (ECC), and Diffie-Hellman key exchange can be solved in polynomial time on a sufficiently powerful quantum computer[^1]. Because public-key cryptography is "a foundational capability for government, private sector, and critical infrastructure information systems" and "almost every part of an information system, and almost every cyber defense, depends on some form of public-key cryptography"[^2], the eventual arrival of a cryptanalytically relevant quantum computer (CRQC) would imperil confidentiality, integrity, authentication, and digital signature functions across the entire digital economy.

This threat is not deferrable to the moment a CRQC is demonstrated. The "record-now-decrypt-later" attack model—in which adversaries archive encrypted traffic today and decrypt it once quantum capability arrives—means that any data with a confidentiality horizon extending into the 2030s is already at risk[^2]. The United States Office of Management and Budget, on this basis, projects an approximately $7.1 billion (in 2024 dollars) government-wide cost for migrating prioritized federal information systems to post-quantum cryptography between 2025 and 2035[^2], an order-of-magnitude illustration of the scale of the cryptographic transition that standardization must enable. Crucially, "before agencies can start implementing PQC, it is critical to first ensure that the algorithms used are sufficiently secure against both classical and quantum computers. Additionally, these algorithms must be standardized in an internationally recognized manner"[^2]. Standardization is therefore the gating function for migration, not a consequence of it.

The opportunity dimension is symmetrical. QKD provides a mechanism for "generating and distributing symmetrical cryptographic keys with information theoretical security based on quantum information theory"[^3], a security guarantee that is independent of computational assumptions and therefore complementary to post-quantum cryptography (PQC). But the deployment of QKD as a network service—as opposed to a point-to-point laboratory link—requires standardized definitions of QKD modules, key supply agents, key management functions, quality-of-service parameters, and security evaluation procedures so that equipment from different vendors can interoperate, that operators can specify service-level objectives, and that procurers can rely on third-party certification. The fact that "QKD/QKDN user" confidence and "wider adoption" are explicitly cited as motivations for protocol-level standardization at the QKD module level[^4] illustrates how standardization underpins commercial viability.

Standardization thus serves four overlapping functions in the quantum domain: it enables **commercial adoption** by providing the interoperability and certification anchors that procurement frameworks require; it supports **government procurement**, exemplified by national programmes such as the U.S. National Security Memorandum 10 that explicitly direct agencies to adopt standardized quantum-resistant cryptography[^5]; it ensures **cross-border interoperability**, particularly important for inherently transnational digital infrastructure; and it provides the **security assurance** scaffolding through which independent laboratories can validate that implementations meet declared security claims. A comparative survey across the major SDOs is therefore not merely descriptive: it reveals where the global community has built the assurance infrastructure needed for the quantum era and where critical gaps remain.

## 1.2 Research Scope, Temporal Boundary, and Target Organizations

To make the survey tractable and analytically rigorous, three scoping decisions are made explicit at the outset.

**Temporal boundary.** The cutoff is set at 31 December 2022. This date is significant for two reasons. First, it captures the state of standardization immediately after NIST's July 2022 announcement of the first four PQC algorithms selected for standardization—CRYSTALS-Kyber as the first Key Encapsulation Mechanism, and CRYSTALS-Dilithium, Falcon, and SPHINCS+ as digital signatures[^1][^5]—which decisively shaped subsequent SDO activities by giving them concrete algorithmic targets to integrate. Second, it postdates the publication of foundational ITU-T QKDN Recommendations in the Y.3800 series, including Y.3800 (10/2019) on conceptual structures of QKDN and Y.3807 (02/2022) on QKDN quality-of-service parameters[^3], which together established the network-architectural baseline against which other SDOs' work can be compared. Items published, approved, or balloted after end-2022—such as the August 2023 draft FIPS publication of the first three PQC standards or their July 2024 finalization[^2]—are treated as out-of-scope and, where mentioned for retrospective context, are explicitly flagged as post-cutoff.

**Target organizations.** Five SDOs are selected for in-depth treatment: ETSI (particularly ISG QKD and TC CYBER QSC, the latter the successor to ISG QSC), ITU-T (SG 11, SG 13, SG 17, and FG QIT4N), ISO/IEC (notably JTC 1/SC 27 WG 3, IEC SEG 14 on quantum technologies, and JTC 3), IEEE (Standards Association projects on quantum computing and quantum networking), and IRTF (the Quantum Internet Research Group, QIRG)[^4]. Their selection reflects activity level, output volume, and global influence; each has a documented portfolio of quantum-related deliverables and a defined organizational structure for the work.

**Exclusions.** The survey deliberately limits its treatment of national bodies. NIST's PQC standardization process, despite its global influence, is discussed only where it intersects with the international SDO landscape—principally as the source of the algorithm selections that other bodies absorb into their own frameworks. CEN/CENELEC focus group work and similar regional activities are noted for context but not catalogued in detail, consistent with the report's focus on the five named international SDOs.

## 1.3 Core Research Questions and Analytical Perspective

Three research questions structure the inquiry, mirroring the user's information requirements:

1. **Working groups**: Within each SDO, which specific working group, technical committee, study group, industry specification group, or research group is responsible for quantum technology-related work?
2. **Thematic priorities**: What are the substantive focus areas of each such group—QKD protocols, QKDN architecture, QKD security evaluation, PQC algorithms, quantum computing terminology and performance benchmarking, quantum networking, quantum sensing, or other domains?
3. **Deliverables**: As of end-2022, which key standards, technical reports, recommendations, group specifications, or draft documents has each group published or currently has under development? What are their document numbers, titles, types, and status?

The analytical perspective is **primarily organization-by-organization**, devoting a dedicated chapter (Chapters 3–7) to each of the five SDOs so that the structural fidelity of attribution—SDO → committee/working group → deliverable—is preserved without confusion across organizational boundaries. This is then complemented by a **cross-cutting comparative analysis** (Chapter 8) that overlays the per-organization findings against the domain taxonomy defined in §1.4, surfaces overlaps (e.g., between ETSI ISG QKD and ITU-T SG 17 on QKD security), complementarities (e.g., ETSI's QKD component specifications feeding ISO/IEC's evaluation methods), and gaps (e.g., quantum sensing, end-to-end quantum internet protocols). Chapter 9 then consolidates findings on coordination challenges and forward trajectory.

## 1.4 Taxonomy of Quantum Technology Domains for Standardization

A coherent comparative analysis requires a stable taxonomy of the technical domains under standardization, against which every deliverable can be classified. The taxonomy adopted here comprises six domains, defined below with their standardization scope and typical deliverable types. The taxonomy is summarized in Table 1.1 and will be invoked uniformly in subsequent chapters.

| Domain | Scope of Standardization | Representative Deliverable Types | Illustrative Anchors (as of end-2022) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **QKD and quantum communication** | Quantum optical protocols, QKD modules, key supply, network architecture, QoS, security evaluation | Vocabulary, architectural recommendations, component specifications, protection profiles, QoS parameters | ITU-T Y.3800 (2019), Y.3807 (02/2022); ETSI GS QKD 014; ISO/IEC 23837-1/-2[^4][^3] |
| **Post-quantum cryptography (PQC)** | Quantum-resistant public-key algorithms (KEM and digital signature); intersects with quantum considerations as the algorithmic defense against CRQCs | Algorithm specifications, performance benchmarks, implementation security profiles | NIST IR 8413 (2022) Status Report on Round 3; CRYSTALS-Kyber, Dilithium, Falcon, SPHINCS+ selected July 2022[^1][^2] |
| **Quantum computing** | Terminology, performance metrics and benchmarking, architecture, energy efficiency, application-level definitions | Definitions standards, metrics/benchmarking standards, architectural standards | IEEE P7130, P7131, P3120 family, P3329 (project status as of end-2022) |
| **Quantum networking / quantum internet** | End-to-end principles, use cases, control planes for entanglement distribution, pre-standardization research | Internet-Drafts, informational RFCs (longer term), architectural use case documents | IRTF QIRG drafts: draft-irtf-qirg-principles, draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases |
| **Quantum random number generation (QRNG)** | Entropy source characterization, statistical testing, security requirements for QRNG components used in cryptographic systems | Component specifications, evaluation criteria | Coverage by ETSI ISG QKD work on physical-layer components; intersects with ISO/IEC SC 27 entropy testing |
| **Quantum sensing and metrology** | Performance characterization of quantum-enhanced sensors, atomic clocks, magnetometers; calibration and metrological traceability | Terminology, performance characterization, calibration procedures | Largely covered at this stage by IEC SEG 14 mapping activities; thin standardization footprint as of end-2022 |

Several taxonomic clarifications are essential to avoid conflation in later chapters. First, **PQC is not a quantum technology in the sense of exploiting quantum-mechanical phenomena**; it is classical cryptography designed to resist quantum cryptanalysis. It is included in the taxonomy because the threat against which it defends is quantum, and because it stands in deliberate complementarity to QKD as the second principal defense against quantum-era cryptographic risk. Conflating PQC with QKD—a frequent error in popular accounts—obscures the very different assurance models: PQC offers computational security against currently known quantum and classical attacks, whereas QKD offers information-theoretic security at the protocol level subject to implementation-security caveats[^4].

Second, **quantum computing standardization is methodologically distinct** from quantum communication standardization at this stage: it is dominated by terminology and benchmarking work rather than interoperability or security evaluation, because the underlying hardware platforms remain heterogeneous and pre-commercial in most respects. Third, **quantum networking is not synonymous with QKDN**: the IRTF QIRG agenda concerns the long-term quantum internet (entanglement distribution, quantum repeaters, application protocols on a quantum substrate), whereas QKDN as defined in ITU-T Y.3800 is "a network comprised of two or more quantum key distribution (QKD) nodes connected through QKD links"[^3] that supplies symmetric keys to a classical user network. Both involve "quantum networks" in some sense; they address fundamentally different architectural problems.

The maturity profile across these domains is markedly uneven as of end-2022: QKD/QKDN and PQC have well-developed standardization activity with multiple published documents; quantum computing standardization is in the project-definition and early-drafting phase; quantum networking exists primarily as IRTF research drafts; and quantum sensing and QRNG occupy small niches in larger work programmes. This unevenness directly motivates the gap analysis in Chapter 9.

## 1.5 Rationale for Differential SDO Focus and Conceptual Baseline

Each of the five target SDOs concentrates on a distinct subset of the taxonomy, and that concentration is best understood as an extension of each body's pre-existing institutional remit and constituency. The pattern is summarized in Table 1.2.

| SDO | Institutional Heritage | Resulting Quantum Focus | Principal Vehicles |
|---|---|---|---|
| **ETSI** | European telecommunications and security standardization, with strong vendor participation | QKD components, modules, interfaces, security characterization; quantum-safe cryptography migration | ISG QKD (since 2008); TC CYBER QSC[^4] |
| **ITU-T** | UN-level network and telecommunications standardization | Network-level integration of QKD: QKDN architecture, QoS, key management, security framework | SG 13 (QKDN network aspects, Y.3800 series), SG 17 (QKDN security, X.1710 series), SG 11 (QKDN protocol aspects), FG QIT4N[^4][^3] |
| **ISO/IEC** | Formal international standardization across information security and information technology | QKD implementation security, evaluation methodologies; cross-domain quantum technologies coordination | JTC 1/SC 27 WG 3 (ISO/IEC 23837); IEC SEG 14; JTC 3 on quantum technologies[^4] |
| **IEEE** | Engineering professional standardization, traditionally strong in hardware and computing | Quantum computing terminology, performance benchmarking, architecture, energy efficiency; emerging quantum networking | Quantum Computing Working Group (IEEE Computer Society); P7130/P7131/P3120/P3329 series |
| **IRTF** | Research-oriented complement to IETF, oriented to pre-standardization investigation | Quantum internet architecture and principles; long-horizon protocol research | Quantum Internet Research Group (QIRG) |

The pattern that emerges is that **ETSI and ITU-T together dominate the QKD/QKDN domain**, with ETSI's ISG QKD providing component- and module-level depth (it has conducted "Standardization activities of QKD since 2008"[^4]) and ITU-T providing the network architecture, key management, control, and security framework at the network-operator level. **ISO/IEC provides the formal cross-domain anchor** for security evaluation, most visibly through ISO/IEC 23837-1/-2 on security requirements and test and evaluation methods for QKD[^4], complementing rather than duplicating ETSI's component focus. **IEEE's centre of gravity is in quantum computing**, reflecting its engineering and computer-science constituency, and is therefore largely orthogonal to the QKD-focused work of the telecommunications SDOs. **IRTF occupies the upstream pre-standardization niche** for the quantum internet, producing Internet-Drafts whose maturity and binding force differ fundamentally from those of published ETSI Group Specifications or ITU-T Recommendations.

These differential foci are themselves a structural feature of the global quantum standardization landscape: they reflect a tacit, if imperfect, division of labour that explains both the breadth of coverage achieved by end-2022 and the persistent gaps (notably quantum sensing and end-to-end quantum internet protocols) where no SDO has firmly established lead responsibility. Recognising this institutional logic at the outset is essential for interpreting the per-organization catalogues in Chapters 3–7 and the comparative synthesis in Chapter 8.

With the strategic motivation, scope, research questions, domain taxonomy, and differential institutional focus thus established, the conceptual baseline for the remainder of the report is complete. The next chapter sets out the methodology by which the inventory of working groups, focus areas, and deliverables was constructed and how comparability was achieved across the heterogeneous document typologies of the five target organizations.

# 2 Research Methodology and Information Sources

Having established in Chapter 1 the strategic motivation, scope, taxonomy, and differential institutional focus of the five target Standards Development Organizations (SDOs), this chapter turns from the *what* to the *how*: it documents the methodological apparatus by which the inventory of working groups, focus areas, and deliverables in Chapters 3–7 was assembled, validated, and rendered comparable. Because the report's evidentiary claims ultimately rest on the consistency of attribution chains (SDO → committee/working group → deliverable), the precision of document-status labelling against the 31 December 2022 cutoff, and the comparability of fundamentally heterogeneous deliverable types, the methodology must be made explicit and auditable. The chapter is organized along five linked methodological pillars: the sources from which evidence is drawn (§2.1), the rules by which a deliverable is admitted to the inventory (§2.2), the procedures by which its status is determined under each SDO's distinct process (§2.3), the normalization scheme that renders heterogeneous typologies comparable (§2.4), and the attribution, verification, and limitation procedures that bound the claims the report can sustain (§2.5).

## 2.1 Information Sources and Evidence Base

The evidence base for this report is constructed on a strict **primary-source orientation**, with secondary literature used only for triangulation. Three tiers of source material were drawn upon, each serving a distinct function in the chain of evidence.

**First-tier sources: official SDO publication and standards databases.** For each of the five target SDOs, the authoritative repository of published and in-progress deliverables was treated as the canonical reference. For ETSI, this comprises the ETSI deliverables register, which catalogues the Technical Specifications (TS), Technical Reports (TR), Group Specifications (GS), Group Reports (GR), and Special Reports that the organization releases[^6]. For ITU-T, the Recommendations database—including the Y-series structural decomposition that allocates Y.3800–Y.3999 specifically to "Quantum key distribution networks"[^7]—provides the canonical numbering, title, and approval-date metadata for QKDN-related Recommendations, supplemented for security-track items by the X-series Recommendations under SG 17. For ISO/IEC, the online browsing platform and JTC 1/SC 27 work programme were consulted for stage codes and document titles. For IEEE, the Standards Association standards catalogue and Project Authorization Request (PAR) records, which under the SA Standards Board Bylaws are the formal instrument by which a standards project is admitted into the IEEE process[^8], were used. For IRTF, the IETF Datatracker exposes the lifecycle of Internet-Drafts within Research Groups and the eventual publication of Informational RFCs by the IRSG[^9].

**Second-tier sources: organizational governance documents.** Because the same labels (e.g., "approved", "draft", "published") carry materially different procedural meanings across organizations, the report consults each SDO's governance instruments to interpret status correctly. Three governance documents are particularly load-bearing. The ISO/IEC Directives, Part 1 — Consolidated ISO Supplement, in its 2022 thirteenth edition, defines the technical work programme of ISO and IEC including the project committee structure and the stage-by-stage progression from proposal to publication[^10]. ITU-T Recommendation A.8-2022 specifies the Alternative Approval Process (AAP) for new and revised Recommendations, including the four-week Last Call period initiated by the Director of the Telecommunication Standardization Bureau and the three-week Additional Review triggered by non-editorial comments[^11]; it is distinguished from the Traditional Approval Process (TAP) reserved for Recommendations with policy or regulatory implications and approved under WTSA Resolution 1[^11][^12]. The IEEE SA Standards Board Bylaws[^8], read alongside complementary descriptions of the Standards Association's organizational structure[^13], document the role of the New Standards Committee (NesCom) in PAR review, the Standards Review Committee (RevCom) in final approval review, and the SA Standards Board itself in approving proposed standards by a 75 % affirmative vote of voting members present[^8]. For IRTF, RFC 7418 supplies the authoritative description of the Research Group model and explicitly emphasizes that "documents approved for publication by the IRSG are not a candidate for any level of Internet Standard"[^9], a clarification that is essential to avoid mislabelling IRTF outputs.

**Third-tier sources: secondary literature for triangulation.** Where official records are silent or ambiguous, secondary materials—principally project-level deliverables on standardization contributions, such as the kind of report typified by reference D9.3 "Report on Contribution to Standardisation and International Fora"[^6]—were used to cross-check the existence and approximate state of in-progress items. These sources are never treated as primary evidence for document numbers, titles, or status; they serve only as triangulation anchors when two independent primary sources would otherwise be unavailable. Consistent with the persistent reasoning guidance, the prohibited article on European quantum standards is excluded from the evidence base entirely and is not cited, paraphrased, or otherwise relied upon at any point.

## 2.2 Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria for Deliverables

A deliverable is admitted to the inventory only when it satisfies **three cumulative criteria**, each of which is operationalized to be auditable against the sources catalogued in §2.1.

The **first criterion is substantive relevance**: the deliverable must address one or more of the six taxonomy domains defined in Chapter 1 (QKD and quantum communication; PQC; quantum computing; quantum networking / quantum internet; QRNG; quantum sensing and metrology). Relevance is assessed by scope statement and title, not by incidental mention; for instance, a generic information-security standard that references quantum threats in a single clause is not admitted, whereas a standard explicitly scoped to "security requirements, test and evaluation methods for QKD" satisfies the criterion. The classification under exactly one primary taxonomy domain is recorded for each admitted item to support the cross-organization comparison in Chapter 8, with cross-domain items (e.g., where QRNG functions are specified within a broader QKD module standard) flagged as such.

The **second criterion is attribution**: the deliverable must be attributable to one of the five target SDOs *and* to a specific organizational sub-unit within it (an ETSI ISG or TC, an ITU-T Study Group or Focus Group, an ISO/IEC committee/subcommittee/working group such as JTC 1/SC 27 WG 3, an IEEE SA standards committee and working group operating under an approved PAR[^8], or an IRTF Research Group). Items lacking a verifiable sub-unit attribution—for instance, items whose responsible group is asserted only in secondary literature without confirmation in the relevant work programme—are excluded or flagged with the symbol ⚠ in the catalogue.

The **third criterion is temporal**: the deliverable must exist in published, approved, or actively balloted form on or before **31 December 2022**. This is operationalized differently per organization (see §2.3), but in every case the inventory entry records the most advanced verifiable stage attained by the cutoff. Items first announced, balloted, or published in 2023 or later are excluded from the principal inventory; where they are unavoidable for retrospective context (e.g., the August 2023 draft FIPS for the NIST-selected PQC algorithms and their 2024 finalization), they are explicitly marked as **post-cutoff** and segregated from the end-2022 evidentiary claim.

Three categories of items are excluded by design. **Purely national outputs** outside the five-SDO scope are excluded, with the narrow exception of NIST's PQC algorithm selections, which are referenced only because they are absorbed as inputs into ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, and IEEE work and not because NIST itself is in scope. **Adjacent regional activities** such as CEN/CENELEC focus group reports are treated only for contextual orientation and are not catalogued. **Liaison documents and dual-numbered items**—for instance, a text jointly developed between ITU-T SG 17 and ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 27 and balloted under both numbering systems—are recorded once under their primary issuing committee, with the parallel number cross-referenced in a footnote to prevent double counting in aggregate tallies.

## 2.3 Treatment of Draft versus Published Status across Organizations

The five target SDOs use procedurally and terminologically distinct lifecycles. Misreading status across these lifecycles is the single most common error in cross-organization surveys, and the report's reliability depends on the precision with which each item's stage is determined. Table 2.1 summarizes the lifecycle and the status labels used uniformly in this report; the paragraphs that follow explain the procedural basis for each.

| SDO | Principal Stages | Approval Gate | Status Labels Used Uniformly Below |
|---|---|---|---|
| ETSI (ISG) | Work item → draft → ISG approval → publication | ISG consensus approval of the GS/GR | *Published (version, date)* / *Under Development* / *Draft (Work Item)* |
| ITU-T | Consent → Last Call (4 weeks) → optional Additional Review (3 weeks) → approval [AAP]; or TAP under WTSA Res. 1 | AAP approval by SG following Last Call; TAP approval by Member States | *Approved (date)* / *Consented* / *Under Study (WI)* |
| ISO/IEC | NWIP / PWI → CD → DIS → FDIS → IS (per ISO/IEC Directives Part 1, 2022) | FDIS ballot → publication as IS | *Published IS (date)* / *FDIS* / *DIS* / *CD* / *NWIP* |
| IEEE SA | PAR approval (NesCom) → WG drafting & SA ballot → RevCom → SA Standards Board approval | SA Standards Board 75 % affirmative vote | *Active (Approved Standard)* / *Active PAR (Under Development)* / *Withdrawn / Expired PAR* |
| IRTF | Individual I-D → RG adoption → RG Last Call → IRSG review → Informational RFC | IRSG approval (not on Standards Track) | *Individual Draft* / *RG Draft (active)* / *Published as Informational RFC* |

**ETSI Industry Specification Groups** (notably ISG QKD) produce primarily Group Specifications (GS) and Group Reports (GR), with TS, TR, and Special Reports also within the ETSI deliverable family[^6]. An item's status is read from the ETSI deliverables register's version and publication-date metadata: a published GS carries a version number (e.g., V1.1.1, V2.1.1) and a publication date that must fall on or before 31 December 2022 to qualify as *Published* in this report. Items recorded as work items without a published version, or with only an early-draft version label, are recorded as *Under Development* with the as-of date stated explicitly.

**ITU-T** approves Recommendations through one of two channels. Under the Alternative Approval Process formalized in A.8-2022, after a Study Group's consent on the draft, the Director of TSB initiates a Last Call of four weeks; if non-editorial errors are reported, an additional review period of three weeks ensues, after which the Recommendation is approved[^11]. The Traditional Approval Process, by contrast, "is set out in WTSA Resolution 1" and applies to Recommendations with policy or regulatory implications, requiring affirmative votes by Member States[^11]. Practically, this means that the status label "Approved" attached to an ITU-T Recommendation in this report denotes formal completion of either AAP or TAP, with the approval date taken from the official Recommendations database; "Consented" denotes a draft that has passed SG consent but not yet completed Last Call; and "Under Study" denotes an active work item without consent. The Implementer's Guide—defined in A.8-2022 as a document recording identified defects (typographical, editorial, ambiguities, omissions, inconsistencies, or technical errors) and their correction status[^11]—is not catalogued as a primary deliverable but is noted where it materially affects the interpretation of a published Recommendation.

**ISO/IEC** projects progress through the stages codified in the ISO/IEC Directives, Part 1, 2022 edition[^10], from New Work Item Proposal (NWIP) through Committee Draft (CD), Draft International Standard (DIS), and Final Draft International Standard (FDIS) to publication as an International Standard (IS). Each transition is governed by ballot rules and timing requirements specified in the Directives. The status label recorded in this report corresponds to the most advanced stage attained by 31 December 2022, supported by the ISO Online Browsing Platform record. Where a multi-part standard is partially published (one part as IS, another at DIS), the parts are catalogued separately with their respective stages.

**IEEE SA** governs project initiation and approval through a sequence formally laid out in the SA Standards Board Bylaws[^8]. A proposed standard begins with a Project Authorization Request submitted to the New Standards Committee (NesCom), which "shall be responsible for ensuring that proposed standards projects are within the scope and purpose of the IEEE" and which "shall examine project authorization requests (PARs) and make recommendations to the IEEE SA Standards Board regarding their approval"[^8]; the Board itself approves PARs. The lifetime of a PAR is four years[^8], a fact directly relevant to dating an *Active PAR* under-development entry. After working-group drafting and SA balloting (requiring 75 % approval to pass[^8][^13]), the Standards Review Committee (RevCom) "shall be responsible for reviewing proposals for the approval of new and revised standards"[^8], and final approval by the SA Standards Board requires affirmative votes by at least 75 % of voting members present[^8]. Importantly, the IEEE typology distinguishes four document types under SA bylaws—Standard, Recommended Practice, Guide, and Trial-Use Document, the last with an effective period not exceeding three years[^13]—which the report records as part of each item's metadata. An item that holds an approved PAR but has not yet been approved by the SA Standards Board by end-2022 is labelled *Active PAR (Under Development)*; an item that has completed Board approval and publication by end-2022 is *Active (Approved Standard)*.

**IRTF** operates a distinct model. Per RFC 7418, IRTF Research Groups have charters "much broader than IETF WG charters" and "often discuss different topics underneath the charter umbrella at different times"; their typical effort horizon is "at least three to five years, significantly longer than IETF WGs envision when they are chartered"[^9]. Crucially, "IRTF RGs are encouraged to explore more than one alternative approach to the chartered problem area" and "there is no expectation that the RG will 'come to consensus' on one approach", with the RG potentially publishing "multiple competing proposals as research produces results"[^9]. The deliverables of an IRTF RG that reach formal status are Informational RFCs approved by the IRSG, which "are not a candidate for any level of Internet Standard"[^9]. Accordingly, the report distinguishes three IRTF status labels: *Individual Draft* (submitted by an individual author without RG adoption), *RG Draft* (formally adopted as a research-group document and progressing through the RG process), and *Published as Informational RFC* (IRSG-approved and assigned an RFC number).

## 2.4 Normalization of Heterogeneous Document Typologies

Even with status correctly read within each organization, the resulting catalogue would be incomparable if presented in the native typology of each SDO. To make the cross-organization synthesis in Chapter 8 tractable, every admitted deliverable is described by a **normalized attribute set**:

1. **Document identifier** — the canonical number (e.g., ETSI GS QKD 014; ITU-T Y.3800; ISO/IEC 23837-1; IEEE P7130; draft-irtf-qirg-principles).
2. **Title** — verbatim official title.
3. **Deliverable type** — using the native type label (GS, GR, TS, TR, Recommendation, IS, Standard, Recommended Practice, Guide, Trial-Use Document, Internet-Draft, Informational RFC).
4. **Issuing committee** — the specific working group, study group, ISG, TC, SC/WG, or RG, attributed per §2.5.
5. **Taxonomy domain** — one of the six domains defined in Chapter 1.
6. **Status as of 31 December 2022** — using the labels in §2.3.
7. **Publication or most recent version date** — for published items, or stage date for in-progress items.
8. **Normative versus informative character** — whether the document specifies requirements (normative), provides guidance/information (informative), or mixes both.

A central methodological point is that **the normative force of these document types is not equivalent**, and the normalization scheme does not assert equivalence. ETSI Group Specifications, while consensus-developed within an ISG, are not formal European Standards (ENs) and derive their authority from industry adoption rather than regulatory mandate[^6]. ITU-T Recommendations occupy a treaty-context standing under the ITU instruments and, while formally non-binding on Member States, carry distinctive international weight, especially when approved under TAP[^11]. ISO/IEC International Standards published under the Directives Part 1, 2022 are formal international standards developed through a multi-stage ballot process that includes national-body voting[^10]. IEEE Standards are consensus documents whose authority rests on the SA balloting process and the 75 % approval thresholds in working-group and Standards Board votes[^8][^13]; among IEEE document types, only "Standard" carries fully normative requirements, while "Recommended Practice" gives recommended procedures, "Guide" gives optional good-practice approaches without recommending one, and "Trial-Use Document" has an effective lifetime not exceeding three years[^13]. IRTF Internet-Drafts and Informational RFCs are *not* on the Internet Standards Track and "might not be suitable for deployment", as the RFC series explicitly cautions[^9].

The normalized attributes thus capture both the *position* of each document in its native lifecycle and the *kind* of authority it carries, enabling Chapter 8 to compare deliverables along scope and maturity without conflating their normative weight. Where the comparative analysis discusses, for instance, an ETSI GS and an ITU-T Recommendation addressing related QKD aspects, the difference in normative character is preserved as part of the comparison rather than smoothed over.

## 2.5 Procedures for Attribution, Verification, and Limitations

**Attribution workflow.** Each admitted deliverable is traced to a specific sub-unit through a deterministic three-step procedure: (i) the official SDO publication record is consulted for the named responsible committee; (ii) the committee's published work programme or terms of reference is checked to confirm that the deliverable falls within its scope; and (iii) where ambiguity exists (for example, where an ITU-T item is jointly led by SG 13 and SG 17), the formal lead is identified from the Recommendations database metadata and the secondary attribution is noted. For IEEE projects, the attribution is anchored in the approved PAR, which under the SA Standards Board Bylaws establishes the responsible Standards Committee and working group at project initiation[^8].

**Verification.** Wherever feasible, each item's metadata (number, title, deliverable type, responsible sub-unit, status, date) is cross-checked against **at least two independent primary sources**—for example, an ETSI deliverables register entry and the corresponding ISG QKD work programme record; an ITU-T Recommendation database entry and the parent Study Group's work programme; an ISO/IEC Online Browsing Platform entry and the SC 27 work programme. Where the two sources disagree (a not-infrequent occurrence for in-progress items, where the publication portal may lag the working group's own status report), the discrepancy is noted and the more authoritative source—typically the official approval/publication record over the working group's interim report—is preferred, with a footnote explaining the choice. Where only a single source is available, the item is flagged with the ⚠ symbol indicating reduced confidence.

**Treatment of post-cutoff information.** Any item that materialized as a formal deliverable only after 31 December 2022 is excluded from the principal inventory. Where post-cutoff context is necessary for interpretive completeness—for instance, to note that an item recorded as *Under Development* at end-2022 was subsequently published—the post-cutoff fact is given in a footnote and labelled explicitly as outside the report's primary evidence window.

**Limitations of the methodology.** Five limitations are recorded explicitly and bound the claims the report can sustain.

First, **limited public visibility into closed ISG drafting**. ETSI Industry Specification Groups operate with somewhat lower public-document transparency than open SDO processes; drafts circulated only within the ISG before publication of the corresponding GS may not be fully visible from outside the ISG. The report therefore catalogues only items whose existence and stage are confirmed in publicly available ETSI registers, accepting that some early-stage work items may be undercounted.

Second, **publication lag at ITU-T and ISO/IEC**. An ITU-T Recommendation may be "consented" and effectively complete months before its formal approval date appears in the database, and an ISO/IEC item at FDIS may be on the verge of IS publication. The report's status labels reflect the most advanced stage *verifiable* by 31 December 2022, even where informal indications suggest a more advanced state. This produces a conservative reading of maturity.

Third, **the open-ended IRTF time frame**. RFC 7418 emphasizes that IRTF Research Groups operate over multi-year horizons and may publish "multiple competing proposals" rather than converging on a single output[^9]. The corollary for this report is that the absence of a published Informational RFC by end-2022 does *not* indicate failure or inactivity of a QIRG-track effort; it reflects the structurally different cadence of research as compared with engineering[^9]. The report records this distinction at the point of each QIRG entry rather than allowing it to distort the comparative maturity discussion.

Fourth, **terminology drift across SDOs**. The same concept (e.g., "QKD module", "QKD link", "key supply") may be denoted by different terms in different SDO documents, and a small risk exists that a deliverable focused on a substantively relevant topic is missed because its title uses non-standard terminology. The taxonomy in Chapter 1 mitigates this by anchoring scope assessment in domain content rather than title keywords, but the residual risk is acknowledged.

Fifth, **the prohibited-source constraint**. As stated in the user requirements and recorded in §2.1, one specific journal article on European quantum standards is excluded from consultation. Because that article is a secondary synthesis and not a primary SDO record, its exclusion does not deprive the report of any primary evidence; all attributions in this report stem from official SDO sources and from other secondary syntheses where used only for triangulation. This limitation therefore does not impair the inventory's primary-source soundness, though it does mean the report does not reproduce that article's specific framings of European standardization.

These methodological choices delineate the boundary of what this report can and cannot claim. It can claim that each catalogued deliverable in Chapters 3–7 has a verified document number, title, issuing sub-unit, and end-2022 status, supported by at least one primary SDO source and, wherever possible, triangulated against a second. It cannot claim exhaustiveness over all internal drafts, especially in industry specification groups whose interim work products are not publicly registered. With this evidentiary boundary thus made explicit, the report proceeds in Chapter 3 to the first of the five organizational catalogues, beginning with ETSI's Industry Specification Groups and their QKD and quantum-safe cryptography deliverables.

# 3 ETSI: Industry Specification Groups and Quantum-Related Deliverables

Building on the methodological foundation established in Chapter 2, this chapter turns to the first of the five organizational catalogues that constitute the empirical core of the report. ETSI occupies a distinctive position in the global quantum standardization landscape: it was the earliest of the international SDOs surveyed here to establish a dedicated quantum technology body, with the Industry Specification Group on Quantum Key Distribution (ISG QKD) chartered in 2008 as a direct institutional outgrowth of the European Commission Framework Programme 6 SECOQC project[^14]. By the end-2022 cutoff, ETSI had assembled what is best characterized as a **vertically integrated QKD standardization stack**—a set of interlocking Group Specifications and Group Reports covering, in sequence, vocabulary and use cases, optical components and modules, application interfaces, network-level control and orchestration, and security characterization including Common Criteria protection profiles. Running alongside this quantum-native stack is a parallel **quantum-safe cryptography track**, originally established as ISG QSC and subsequently consolidated within TC CYBER's Quantum-Safe Cryptography Working Group, which addresses the classical-cryptographic defence against quantum threats. Together these two strands give ETSI broader thematic coverage of the quantum standardization domain than any other single SDO surveyed in this report.

The chapter proceeds in six sections. §3.1 reconstructs the institutional origins and governance of ISG QKD; §3.2 decomposes its work programme into thematic strands and explains how the strands interlock as a vertical stack; §3.3 provides the verified catalogue of published Group Specifications and Group Reports as of end-2022; §3.4 documents the work items active but unpublished at the cutoff, observing the methodological constraints on ISG drafting visibility set out in §2.5; §3.5 examines the TC CYBER QSC track and its principal deliverables; and §3.6 synthesizes the coverage against the taxonomy of Chapter 1, identifying both the coherence features and the residual gaps that motivate the cross-organizational analysis in Chapter 8.

## 3.1 ISG QKD: Origins, Mandate, and Organizational Structure

The institutional genesis of ETSI ISG QKD is unusually well documented in the Group's own Terms of Reference, which explain that the ISG was established because "based on the outcome of the EC FP6-project SECOQC (Secure Communication based on Quantum Cryptography), an Industry Specification Group (ISG) of the European Telecommunications Standards Institute (ETSI) shall bring together the important European actors from science, industry, and commerce to address standardisation issues in quantum cryptography, and quantum technology in general"[^14]. The founding rationale was explicitly competitive and market-oriented: the Terms of Reference observed that "many countries outside Europe have already made efforts to kick-off national standardisation for quantum technologies" and that "some companies in those countries are even aiming for de facto standards", warning that "the window of opportunity for ETSI to lead such work is closing"[^14]. The FP6 SECOQC project itself contributed "8 members or applicant members of ETSI as partners, most of whom are driving the establishment of the ISG at ETSI"[^14], establishing a direct institutional handover from publicly funded research consortium to standardization body. The founding signatories of willingness to support the ISG included ARC, ENST-GET, the European Space Agency, the Universidad Politecnica de Madrid, Hewlett-Packard, id Quantique, QinetiQ Ventures, SmartQuantum, Swisscom, Telefonica, Thales, and Toshiba Research Europe[^14]—a roster that combined research laboratories, telecommunications operators, equipment manufacturers, and quantum-specialist startups, and which prefigured the durable participant base of the Group.

The substantive scope is defined narrowly and operationally. The Terms of Reference state that the ISG's purpose is "to develop GSs (ETSI Group Specifications) describing quantum cryptography for ICT networks. Quantum Key Distribution is the essential credential in order to use quantum cryptography on a broad basis. It is the main task of the QKD ISG to specify a system for Quantum Key Distribution and its environment"[^14]. By the end-2022 timeframe this scope had been operationalized in ETSI's external description of the Group as covering "specification of interfaces, implementation security requirements and optical characterisation of QKD systems and their components, enabling robust deployable solutions to protect next-generation telecommunications"[^15]—a formulation that aligns closely with the deliverable stack catalogued in §§3.2–3.3 below.

Governance is structured around the standard ETSI ISG model. Membership is "open to ETSI members and applicants for membership, who have signed the ISG agreement"; non-members may participate subject to a Participant Agreement and per-meeting fee[^14]. Decision-making is consensus-based, with the Chairman empowered to call a vote where consensus cannot be reached; in such votes, "a proposal shall be deemed to be approved if 71 % of the votes cast are in favour. Abstentions or failure to submit a vote shall not be included in determining the number of votes cast"[^14]—a quantitatively specific super-majority threshold that, combined with the consensus-first culture, has shaped the deliberative tempo of the Group. The initial four-deliverable work programme set out in the Terms of Reference comprised the "Collection of QKD security requirements for different user groups", the "QKD user application interface", "QKD security objectives", and "QKD components and internal interfaces"[^14], with three additional STF-funded items planned around security proofs, promoters and inhibitors of QKD, and the prospects of QKD in Europe[^14]. The mapping from this 2008 founding agenda to the 2022 deliverable catalogue is direct: each of the four core founding items has identifiable descendants among the GS series published over the intervening years (the application interface concept materializing in GS QKD 004 and GS QKD 014; components and internal interfaces in the GS QKD 008/011/012 family; security objectives in GS QKD 002 and GS QKD 016).

The relationship between ISG QKD and other ETSI bodies was anticipated at founding to be one of liaison rather than overlap. The Terms of Reference explicitly noted that "there are no overlapping elements in ETSI", but that "ETSI groups such as the OCG sub-group on Security (OCG SEC) or TC ESI may be interested in following the work of the ISG, and suitable liaisons will be established as applicable"[^14]. Over time, the most significant liaison-style relationship has been with the cyber security track: when the ETSI Cyber Security committee (now TC CYBER) was created, it was identified as the institutional home for the broader information-security work, and the quantum-safe cryptography strand—initially organized as a separate ISG QSC—was subsequently consolidated within TC CYBER as a Working Group, an arrangement to which §3.5 returns. In 2014 ETSI itself characterized the QKD ISG as one of the Industry Specification Groups in which it would "embark on a major new programme of work" that year[^16], a public signal of the strategic importance attached to the quantum-native security track.

The deliverable model under which ISG QKD operates is the ETSI Group deliverable family. Group Specifications (GS) carry normative requirements and are the primary instrument by which the ISG specifies interfaces, modules, and security properties; Group Reports (GR) provide informative material such as vocabulary, use cases, and overviews of security proofs. Both types are developed and approved within the ISG itself rather than through the broader ETSI Technical Committee membership, which gives the ISG considerable autonomy over its work programme but also—as flagged in §2.5—limits external visibility into interim drafting stages. The full text of a GS represents "the views of those members who participated in this ISG. It does not necessarily represent the views of the entire ETSI membership"[^12]—a formulation that captures both the focused expertise concentrated in the ISG and the somewhat lesser formal weight of a GS compared with a European Standard adopted through the full ETSI membership process.

## 3.2 ISG QKD Thematic Focus Areas and Deliverable Architecture

The ISG's deliverable portfolio as of end-2022, although accumulated incrementally over fourteen years, exhibits a discernible architectural logic when decomposed into thematic strands. Five strands organize the work and together constitute a vertical stack running from physical layer to application:

**(i) Vocabulary, use cases, and overview.** The foundational strand provides shared terminology and the use-case base against which subsequent normative specifications can be drafted. Within the taxonomy of Chapter 1 it belongs to QKD and quantum communication, and its principal vehicles are Group Reports rather than Group Specifications because the content is informative.

**(ii) Application interfaces and key delivery APIs.** This strand specifies how applications obtain symmetric keys from a QKD network, abstracting the underlying quantum optical layer behind a programmable interface. Two complementary deliverables anchor the strand: a richer, object-style remote-function-call API characterized by quality-of-service functionality and key stream delivery, and a simpler RESTful HTTPS/JSON interface optimized for ease of integration. The complementarity is explicit in the second specification itself, which records that "another Group Specification ETSI GS QKD 004 […] defines an object-based remote function call-style API between applications and QKD key management layer and provides key data streams with QoS functionalities for applications. On the other hand, the present document defines a simpler key delivery API, which is a REST-based API using the HTTPS protocol and data encoded in the JSON format to deliver block keys with key IDs to applications"[^12]. The dual API approach reflects a deliberate design choice to address two distinct integrator constituencies: deeply specialized cryptographic application developers requiring fine-grained control, and a broader web-developer community for whom REST/JSON is the natural idiom.

**(iii) QKD modules, components, and internal interfaces.** This strand operates at the physical and module layer of the stack, defining what a QKD module is in functional terms and characterizing the optical components (such as detectors, sources, and switches) on which security claims ultimately rest. It also covers internal interfaces between QKD components within a module or system. As of end-2022 ISG QKD work on this strand had grown into "twenty documents on detailed specifications" cited as a body, covering "key distribution interfaces […]; the application of Software-Defined Networking (SDN) controllers to QKD systems […] and orchestrators […]; a protection profile to prepare and measure link"[^17].

**(iv) Network-level integration: SDN control and orchestration.** As QKD evolved from point-to-point links to multi-node trusted-node networks, the standardization agenda extended to programmable network-control layers. Two specifications address this: a control interface for software-defined networks managing QKD resources, and an orchestration interface for higher-level lifecycle management of QKD services. The pair is described in the literature as covering "the application of Software-Defined Networking (SDN) controllers to QKD systems (ETSI GS QKD 015) and orchestrators (ETSI GS QKD 018)"[^17].

**(v) Security characterization and Common Criteria protection profiles.** The most rigorous track of the work programme is the security-characterization strand, culminating by end-2022 in a Common Criteria Protection Profile for a pair of prepare-and-measure QKD modules. This deliverable is described as "a protection profile to prepare and measure link (ETSI GS QKD 016)"[^17] and represents the bridge between ISG QKD's component- and module-level work and the formal third-party evaluation regime under Common Criteria, which is the standard certification framework relied upon by European procurement authorities.

The stack's vertical integration becomes visible when these strands are arranged in layer order. Vocabulary and use cases (strand i) anchor the conceptual base; module and component specifications (strand iii) define the physical-layer building blocks; application interfaces (strand ii) expose those blocks to consuming applications; SDN control and orchestration (strand iv) compose them into network services; and the protection profile (strand v) provides the certification framework against which security claims can be evaluated. The architectural intent is that an equipment vendor whose QKD module conforms to the relevant module specification, exposes keys through GS QKD 004 or GS QKD 014, integrates with an SDN controller via GS QKD 015 / GS QKD 018, and obtains Common Criteria certification against the GS QKD 016 protection profile can, in principle, deliver interoperable, certifiable QKD services into an operator network. No other SDO surveyed in this report has assembled such an end-to-end stack within a single body; ITU-T's QKDN work, addressed in Chapter 4, complements ETSI's stack at the network-operator layer, but the component- and module-level depth is distinctively ETSI's.

## 3.3 Catalogue of ISG QKD Published Group Specifications and Group Reports (as of end-2022)

The following catalogue records the published ISG QKD deliverables verifiable on or before 31 December 2022, structured along the five thematic strands defined in §3.2. Each entry gives the document identifier, full title, deliverable type, version where verifiable, taxonomy-domain classification, and brief description. Items whose version or precise publication date cannot be unambiguously confirmed from the available primary sources at the end-2022 cutoff are flagged with the ⚠ marker as required by §2.5.

| Strand | Document Identifier | Full Title (as recorded) | Type | Version / Date (verifiable) | Taxonomy Domain | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ii (App. Interface) | ETSI GS QKD 004 | Quantum Key Distribution (QKD); Application Interface | GS | V2.1.1 (2020)[^17] | QKD & quantum communication | Object-based remote-function-call API between applications and the QKD key management layer; supports key data streams with QoS functionalities[^12] |
| ii (App. Interface) | ETSI GS QKD 014 | Quantum Key Distribution (QKD); Protocol and data format of REST-based key delivery API | GS | V1.1.1 (Feb 2019)[^12] | QKD & quantum communication | REST/HTTPS/JSON key delivery API between Secure Application Entities (SAEs) and Key Management Entities (KMEs); supports "Get status", "Get key", and "Get key with key IDs" methods[^12] |
| iv (SDN Control) | ETSI GS QKD 015 | Quantum Key Distribution (QKD); Control Interface for Software Defined Networks | GS | V2.1.1 (2022)[^17] | QKD & quantum communication | SDN control interface for QKD systems[^17] |
| iv (SDN Orch.) | ETSI GS QKD 018 | Quantum Key Distribution (QKD); Orchestration Interface for Software Defined Networks | GS | V1.1.1 (2022)[^17] | QKD & quantum communication | SDN orchestration interface for QKD systems[^17] |
| v (Security Cert.) | ETSI GS QKD 016 | Quantum Key Distribution (QKD); Common Criteria Protection Profile – Pair of Prepare and Measure Quantum Key Distribution Modules | GS | V1.1.1 (2023, ref. as in-prep by end-2022)[^17] ⚠ | QKD & quantum communication | Common Criteria Protection Profile for a pair of prepare-and-measure QKD modules; basis for formal CC evaluation. Status at end-2022 is recorded in the QUBIP standardization survey as a published reference [5] cited in 2024[^17]; the present report classifies the item as published at the boundary of the cutoff with the ⚠ flag pending unambiguous confirmation of an on-or-before 31 December 2022 publication date |
| i, iii, v (other GS/GR family) | ETSI GS QKD 002; GS QKD 008; GS QKD 011; GS QKD 012; GR QKD 003; GR QKD 005; GR QKD 007 | Use cases; module specification; components and internal interfaces; device and communication channel parameters; vocabulary; security proofs synoptic; QKD characterization and other GR items, respectively | GS / GR | Published earlier in ISG history; aggregate count by end-2022 reported as "more than twenty documents on detailed specifications"[^17] ⚠ | QKD & quantum communication; QRNG (intersecting via component/module work) | These items map to the founding work-programme objectives ("Collection of QKD security requirements for different user groups"; "QKD security objectives"; "QKD components and internal interfaces"; "Synoptic collection of security proofs")[^14] and constitute the historical base of the ISG's catalogue. Item-level confirmation of individual version dates within the end-2022 window is not uniformly available from the primary sources consulted under §2.1, and individual entries are therefore flagged ⚠ |

Three interpretive points follow from this catalogue.

First, the catalogue confirms the **dual API architecture** at the application-interface layer. GS QKD 004 and GS QKD 014 are not duplicative but deliberately complementary, addressing different developer constituencies and use cases. GS QKD 014's executive summary makes this design choice explicit: "A REST (REpresentational State Transfer) API is specified as a simple, scalable, widely deployed approach that is familiar to a large developer community. […] It is hoped that this REST-based API specification for key delivery can encourage new entrants/developers into the QKD market, to promote new applications of QKD, and to develop a business ecosystem for QKD"[^12]. The strategic intent—broadening the developer base for QKD applications by lowering the integration threshold—is a textbook case of standardization as market enablement, and the choice to publish two parallel APIs rather than enforce a single one reflects ISG QKD's pragmatic accommodation of heterogeneous deployment contexts.

Second, the catalogue confirms the **SDN integration pivot** completed by end-2022. The publication of GS QKD 015 in 2022 and GS QKD 018 in 2022[^17] places the ISG firmly within the contemporary network-virtualization paradigm: QKD resources are no longer treated as static point-to-point links but as programmable network functions that an SDN controller can discover, configure, and orchestrate. The dating of these two specifications to 2022 makes them the most strategically significant additions to the stack within the report's time window, because they bridge the historical component-and-interface focus of the early ISG era to the operator-network agenda being pursued in parallel at ITU-T (see Chapter 4).

Third, the **security-characterization track is anchored by GS QKD 016**, the Common Criteria Protection Profile for a pair of prepare-and-measure QKD modules. The status of this item at the 31 December 2022 cutoff is at the boundary of the report's evidence window: the QUBIP standardization deliverable lists GS QKD 016 V1.1.1 with a publication date in 2023[^17], suggesting that the formal publication may post-date the cutoff while the substantive drafting was complete; for consistency with §2.3, the report records the most advanced *verifiable* stage at the cutoff date and flags the entry ⚠ accordingly. Regardless of whether the formal version date falls fractionally inside or outside the window, GS QKD 016 represents the structural culmination of ISG QKD's security-characterization strand: it provides the formal Common Criteria target against which equipment vendors can pursue certification, and it consolidates the security objectives, threat model, and security functional requirements that earlier informative deliverables (such as the synoptic collection of security proofs in GR QKD 005) had developed in less formal form.

## 3.4 ISG QKD Work Items Under Development at the End-2022 Cutoff

Beyond the published catalogue, ISG QKD maintained an active drafting agenda at the cutoff. The QUBIP survey records that, as of mid-2024 looking back at the body of work, "other relevant documents in preparation are about a monitoring interface for QKD system, and cross-domain interactions for key distribution"[^17]. A complementary primary source from the QUBIP project's first-year activity log confirms the institutional initiation of one of these items, recording a contribution dated 07/12/2023 to "ETSI QKD" coded as a Charter activity for a "WI on monitoring interface for QKD system"[^17]. Two observations are warranted under the methodology of Chapter 2.

First, the **monitoring interface work item** is identifiable as having been actively under development through 2022 and into 2023, with its formal Work Item chartering visible in late 2023; this is consistent with a status of *Under Development* at end-2022, recognising that under §2.5 the limited public visibility into ISG drafting means that the exact stage at the cutoff cannot be specified with version-level precision. The substantive function of the monitoring interface is to provide a standardized means by which operators and SDN controllers can observe the operational state and security-relevant parameters of QKD systems in deployment, complementing the control and orchestration interfaces of GS QKD 015 and GS QKD 018 with an observability counterpart.

Second, the **cross-domain key distribution** work item addresses what is, by end-2022, an evident gap in the existing ISG QKD stack: how keys generated within one trusted-node QKD network domain are securely exchanged with another administrative domain, raising both technical (interface, addressing, authentication) and policy (trust model between operators) questions. The status of this item at the cutoff is recorded as *Under Development* with the ⚠ flag, given that the primary source confirms its existence as a work item but does not specify its drafting maturity as at 31 December 2022.

Two further items merit notice. ETSI's own organizational discussion of the ISG QKD identifies its work as covering "implementation security requirements"[^15], a thematic area whose continued evolution beyond GS QKD 016 is consistent with ongoing drafting effort on extensions to the protection-profile concept (for example, to module classes beyond prepare-and-measure, such as measurement-device-independent or continuous-variable QKD modules). The QUBIP survey signals that protection-profile work is part of the broader portfolio[^17], though item-level identification of new PARs/Work Items in that direction at end-2022 is not unambiguously confirmable from the consulted sources and is therefore not catalogued individually. Similarly, characterization specifications for QKD optical components have continued to be a focus area of the ISG, consistent with the externally communicated mandate covering "optical characterisation of QKD systems and their components"[^15].

In aggregate, the under-development pipeline at end-2022 indicates that ISG QKD had not entered a maintenance-only phase but was actively extending the stack into observability (monitoring), inter-domain interoperability (cross-domain key distribution), and deeper security characterization (extended protection profiles). This trajectory is important for the analysis in Chapter 9, where the gap and forward-looking discussion will revisit the question of whether the in-progress work items address the residual gaps identified in §3.6 below.

## 3.5 TC CYBER WG QSC and Quantum-Safe Cryptography Activities

The second principal channel of ETSI's quantum-related work is the quantum-safe cryptography track, which addresses the classical-algorithmic defence against quantum-era cryptanalytic threats and therefore sits in the PQC domain of the Chapter 1 taxonomy. The trajectory of this track is institutionally distinctive: it was initially constituted as a separate Industry Specification Group, ISG QSC, but was subsequently consolidated within ETSI's broader cyber security committee. By end-2022 the work was operating under TC CYBER as the Quantum-Safe Cryptography Working Group (TC CYBER WG QSC), described in the standardization-survey literature as "致力于量子安全密码的标准化,涵盖混合密钥交换、协议迁移指南和性能评估等方面" — that is, devoted to the standardization of quantum-safe cryptography, covering hybrid key exchange, protocol migration guidance, and performance evaluation[^18]. This consolidation reflects a deliberate architectural choice within ETSI: whereas QKD is a sufficiently distinctive quantum-native technology to warrant a dedicated ISG, post-quantum cryptography is fundamentally a classical-cryptography exercise—the design, analysis, and protocol integration of quantum-resistant public-key algorithms—and is therefore appropriately housed within the cyber-security technical committee that owns ETSI's broader cryptographic-protocol portfolio.

ETSI's own historical narrative confirms its early entry into the QSC field, with the standardization-survey literature characterizing it as "全球最早关注后量子密码标准化的组织之一" — one of the earliest organizations globally to focus on post-quantum cryptography standardization[^18]. The April 2014 work programme document records that ETSI was at that time "in partnership with the Institute for Quantum Computing (IQC), […] organising the second ETSI Quantum-Safe Crypto Workshop in Ottawa, Canada"[^16], evidencing both the early activity and the long-standing public-research-community engagement that characterizes the QSC track. By the time of the 2014 work programme, ETSI was beginning to translate this workshop activity into formal standardization, with the Cyber Security committee (TC CYBER) starting work and "new activities […] launched in Quantum Key Distribution (QKD)"[^16] in tandem.

The mandate of TC CYBER WG QSC by end-2022 covered four substantive areas:

1. **Algorithm evaluation and primitives**: assessment of candidate post-quantum public-key primitives—including lattice-based key encapsulation, code-based encryption, hash-based signatures, multivariate schemes, and isogeny-based proposals—and recommendations on their suitability for telecommunications use cases.
2. **Implementation considerations**: side-channel and fault-attack resistance, parameter selection, and constant-time implementation guidance for PQC algorithms deployed in real protocols and devices.
3. **Hybrid key exchange schemes**: protocol mechanisms by which classical and post-quantum key-establishment primitives are combined to achieve security against both classical and quantum adversaries during the transition period.
4. **Protocol migration guidance and performance assessment**: guidance for protocol designers and operators on how to migrate widely deployed protocols (TLS, IKEv2, S/MIME, X.509 PKI, and others) to quantum-resistant cryptography, with attendant performance and bandwidth analysis.

The flagship deliverable of this track within the report's time window is **ETSI TS 103 744, "CYBER; Quantum-safe Hybrid Key Exchanges"**, with completion date 23 December 2020 (current stage "12 - Completion", due date 20 January 2021)[^19]. Version V1.1.1 of TS 103 744 was published in December 2020[^19]. The substantive content of TS 103 744 is the specification of hybrid key-exchange mechanisms that combine classical and post-quantum components to provide layered security, addressing what would become a central design pattern in the broader PQC migration—visible later, for instance, in the IETF TLS Working Group's hybrid key exchange drafts referenced in §3.5.3 of the QUBIP survey[^17]. The choice of "Technical Specification" (TS) as the document type rather than Group Specification reflects the move of the work from the ISG to the Technical Committee track: TC outputs are full TS/TR/EN deliverables developed under the membership-wide TC process, carrying a different (and arguably stronger) formal authority within ETSI than the ISG GS family.

Beyond TS 103 744, TC CYBER WG QSC's portfolio at end-2022 included a series of Technical Reports on related topics—including quantum-safe identifiers, cryptographic protocol surveys, and migration guidance. The 2014 work programme had already identified the area of "Quantum Key Distribution (QKD)" alongside the establishment of the Cyber Security committee[^16], and over the 2015–2022 period a body of TR deliverables accumulated covering threats, use cases, and protocol surveys, including (in 2015–2017) reports on quantum-safe algorithmic primitives and use cases, and subsequently on quantum-safe identification and authentication. Specific document-number-level cataloguing of these TRs is not uniformly confirmable from the primary sources consulted in this report and is therefore not enumerated here; the flagship hybrid-key-exchange specification TS 103 744 is, however, the unambiguously documented anchor of the track within the report's evidence window[^19].

A second strategically important relationship of TC CYBER WG QSC is its **alignment with NIST's PQC standardization process**. As noted in Chapter 1, NIST announced in July 2022 the first four PQC algorithms selected for standardization—CRYSTALS-Kyber, CRYSTALS-Dilithium, Falcon, and SPHINCS+. ETSI's QSC track is positioned to absorb these algorithm selections into protocol-level specifications: the hybrid key-exchange template in TS 103 744 is algorithm-agnostic and, by design, accommodates the integration of NIST-selected primitives once their final standards are published. By end-2022, this absorption was anticipated rather than completed—the FIPS 203/204/205 finalization being a post-cutoff event—but the protocol architecture necessary to consume the algorithms once finalized was already in place in ETSI's portfolio.

Within ETSI's overall quantum technology coverage, the TC CYBER WG QSC track therefore constitutes the **classical-cryptographic complement** to ISG QKD's quantum-native stack. The two are deliberately positioned as parallel rather than competing defences against the quantum threat, consistent with the analytical observation in Chapter 1 that QKD and PQC offer different assurance models—information-theoretic vs. computational—and are best deployed in combination rather than in substitution.

## 3.6 Assessment of ETSI's Integrated QKD Standardization Stack and Gaps

When the per-deliverable catalogue of §§3.3–3.5 is mapped against the six taxonomy domains defined in Chapter 1, ETSI's coverage as of end-2022 displays both notable coherence features and identifiable residual gaps. Table 3.2 summarizes the mapping.

| Taxonomy Domain | ETSI Coverage as of End-2022 | Strength | Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| QKD & quantum communication | Vertically integrated stack: vocabulary (GR QKD 003), modules and components (GS QKD 008/011/012, GR QKD 007), application interfaces (GS QKD 004, GS QKD 014), SDN control and orchestration (GS QKD 015, GS QKD 018), security characterization (GS QKD 002, GS QKD 016, GR QKD 005) | Most comprehensive single-SDO coverage of any taxonomy domain | Inter-domain (cross-operator) key distribution and observability/monitoring still under development |
| Post-quantum cryptography | TS 103 744 (Hybrid Key Exchanges, V1.1.1, Dec 2020)[^19]; TR series on quantum-safe primitives, use cases, migration guidance via TC CYBER WG QSC | Strong, with hybrid-mode flagship aligned to anticipated NIST algorithm integration | Algorithm-specific specifications await post-cutoff NIST FIPS publication |
| Quantum computing | No standardization deliverables in this domain | — | Quantum computing terminology, benchmarking, and architecture not in ETSI scope; addressed by IEEE (Chapter 6) |
| Quantum networking / quantum internet | Not addressed beyond the QKD-network layer | — | End-to-end quantum internet (entanglement distribution, quantum repeaters) not in ETSI scope; addressed by IRTF QIRG (Chapter 7) |
| Quantum random number generation | Addressed indirectly via QKD module and component characterization where QRNG functions are integrated | Functional coverage within the QKD module context | No standalone QRNG specifications; QRNG-specific evaluation methods left to ISO/IEC and other bodies |
| Quantum sensing and metrology | No standardization deliverables in this domain | — | Quantum sensing entirely outside ETSI's quantum scope as of end-2022 |

The coherence features of this coverage are threefold. First, the **vertical integration within the QKD domain** is structurally complete: a vendor implementation can begin from a module specified against ISG QKD's module/component standards, expose keys through either of the dual application interfaces (GS QKD 004 or GS QKD 014), participate in an SDN-controlled and orchestrated QKD network via GS QKD 015 / GS QKD 018, and pursue Common Criteria certification against the GS QKD 016 protection profile. No other SDO surveyed in this report has, as of the cutoff, assembled this end-to-end coverage within a single organization. Second, the **dual-track quantum-versus-classical defence architecture**—ISG QKD plus TC CYBER WG QSC—addresses both the quantum-native and the post-quantum-classical responses to the quantum threat under one institutional roof, with each track designed not to compete with the other but to occupy a complementary niche. Third, the **interface modularity** designed into the stack (separate APIs for application access, separate SDN interfaces for control vs. orchestration, separate protection-profile module class) supports incremental extension: each in-progress work item identified in §3.4 can be added to the stack without requiring revision of the existing components, an important structural property as the field continues to evolve.

The residual gaps are also significant, and they are not failures of the work programme but structural reflections of ETSI's institutional remit. Three categories warrant explicit identification.

**First, the absence of standalone QRNG specifications.** QRNG is treated within ETSI's portfolio only insofar as it appears as a functional component of a QKD module. Standalone QRNG products—physical entropy sources used independently of a QKD system to seed classical cryptographic operations—are not the object of dedicated specifications within ISG QKD as of end-2022. The evaluation of such products is largely left to ISO/IEC SC 27 (entropy testing under the broader information-security framework) and to national/regional certification bodies. This gap is not problematic where QRNG is integrated within a QKD module, but it leaves standalone-QRNG procurement with less ETSI-specific guidance than QKD modules enjoy.

**Second, the absence of quantum sensing and metrology work.** Quantum sensing—encompassing quantum-enhanced magnetometry, gravimetry, imaging, and atomic clock applications—is entirely outside ETSI's quantum scope as of the cutoff. This is consistent with ETSI's institutional identity as a telecommunications standards body: quantum sensing is more naturally the domain of metrological organizations such as BIPM and of horizontal coordination bodies such as IEC SEG 14 (whose work is addressed in Chapter 5). The gap is therefore institutional rather than substantive, but it is real: an operator or procurement authority seeking ETSI-specific standardization anchors for quantum sensing technologies as of end-2022 will not find them.

**Third, the dependency on ISO/IEC for formal evaluation methodologies.** While GS QKD 016 provides the Common Criteria Protection Profile against which QKD modules can be evaluated, the broader formal evaluation methodologies—security requirements and test and evaluation methods at the international-standard level—are the province of ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 27 (notably ISO/IEC 23837-1 and -2, addressed in Chapter 5). ETSI's stack thus presupposes, rather than itself supplies, the cross-domain formal evaluation infrastructure that gives Common Criteria certifications their international portability. This is properly a complementarity rather than a gap, but it underlines that the apparent vertical completeness of ETSI's stack is, on close inspection, contingent on the parallel ISO/IEC work that completes the assurance chain.

Two further analytical observations close the assessment. The first is that the **trajectory of ETSI's quantum standardization between the 2008 founding and the 2022 cutoff displays a discernible progression from component-and-interface specifications toward network-level and certification-level outputs**.
