The early years of the ISG produced the vocabulary, use cases, and module-and-component specifications that constitute the historical base; the period from approximately 2019 onward saw the publication of the application-interface APIs (GS QKD 004, GS QKD 014), the SDN control and orchestration interfaces (GS QKD 015, GS QKD 018, both 2022), and the Common Criteria protection profile (GS QKD 016). This is the standardization signature of a technology transitioning from laboratory specification to operator-grade deployment, and it positions ETSI well for the next phase—monitoring interfaces, inter-domain interoperability, and extended protection profiles, all visible in the under-development pipeline of §3.4.

The second observation is that **ETSI's institutional choice to keep ISG QKD and TC CYBER WG QSC organizationally separate while substantively complementary is itself a model for how an SDO can address the multi-domain quantum standardization landscape**. The ISG provides the focused-membership, fast-moving forum appropriate for a deeply specialized quantum-native technology; the TC provides the broader-membership, formally weighted forum appropriate for cryptographic-protocol standardization with cross-cutting implications for all telecommunications protocols. This division of labour is one to which the comparative analysis in Chapter 8 will return when assessing how other SDOs have or have not assembled comparable institutional configurations.

With ETSI's catalogue and assessment now established, the report proceeds in Chapter 4 to ITU-T, whose Study Groups operate at the network-operator and treaty-context layer that complements ETSI's component-and-module emphasis and whose Y.3800-series Recommendations on QKDN architecture and quality of service interlock directly with the ETSI stack catalogued above.

# 4 ITU-T: Study Groups and Quantum-Related Recommendations

Chapter 3 closed by noting that ETSI's vertically integrated QKD stack derives much of its strategic coherence from its complementarity with the network-operator and treaty-context standardization carried out at ITU-T. This chapter turns to that complementary tier. Whereas ETSI's centre of gravity lies in QKD modules, components, application interfaces, and Common Criteria certification, ITU-T addresses the layer above: how QKD modules compose into operator networks; how those networks are architected, managed, controlled, and orchestrated; how their quality of service is specified; how their security is framed; and how their signalling and protocol interfaces are normatively defined. By the end-2022 cutoff, ITU-T had constructed a recognizable QKDN standardization corpus around the Y.3800-series Recommendations of SG13, supplemented by the X.1710-series security framework of SG17, an emerging Q.QKDN-series protocol track in SG11, and a body of pre-standardization technical reports produced by the Focus Group on Quantum Information Technology for Networks (FG-QIT4N). The treatment that follows decomposes this corpus along the institutional axes that produced it, catalogues the deliverables verifiable at the cutoff, and concludes with an assessment of ITU-T's coverage, its complementarity with ETSI, and the residual gaps that motivate the cross-organizational synthesis in Chapter 8.

## 4.1 Institutional Architecture: ITU-T Study Group Mandates and Inter-SG Division of Labor for Quantum Work

ITU-T's quantum technology work, as of end-2022, is allocated across four institutional venues whose division of labour follows the standard WTSA-mandated logic that separates network-architecture aspects, security aspects, and signalling/protocol aspects into distinct Study Groups. This separation is not nominal: it is operationalized through specific Questions within each Study Group that hold the quantum mandate, and through formal liaison relationships that coordinate the resulting tripartite (and, with the Focus Group, quadripartite) work programme.

**SG13 — QKDN network aspects.** Within SG13, two Questions carry the principal quantum mandate. Q16/13, characterized in the ITU-T work programme as "Knowledge-centric trustworthy networking and services", is responsible for the QKDN architectural Recommendations, key management, control, and orchestration, while Q6/13, characterized as "Quality of service (QoS) aspects including IMT-2020 networks", handles the QKDN quality-of-service sub-series.[^20] This Q16/Q6 division within SG13 is significant because it allocates the QoS strand—where machine-learning-based assurance and parameter measurement intersect with broader QoS frameworks for IMT-2020 networks—to a Question whose remit explicitly extends beyond QKDN, ensuring methodological alignment between QKDN QoS work and the wider network-performance corpus of SG13.

**SG17 — QKDN security.** Within SG17, Q15/17 (which absorbed the former Q4/17 line of work) holds the QKDN security mandate.[^21] Q15/17's remit covers the security framework for QKDN, security requirements for QKDN elements (key management, trusted nodes, control and management, authentication and authorization, infrastructure integration), and—as of the cutoff—the initiation of the QKD protocol framework work item that FG-QIT4N's analytical reports had identified as a gap. The reassignment from Q4/17 to Q15/17 reflects an internal SG17 reorganization of Question numbering rather than a substantive change of scope.

**SG11 — QKDN signalling and protocols.** Within SG11, Q2/11 holds the protocol-track mandate, characterized in the work programme as covering "Signalling requirements and protocols for services and applications in telecommunication environments".[^22] Q2/11's quantum work, initiated after SG13's architectural Recommendations had established the Y.3800-series reference points, develops protocol-level normative specifications for the classical-layer interfaces of QKDN (Ak, Kq-1, Kx, Ck, Cq), explicitly leaving the quantum-layer (QKD) protocols themselves outside its scope—these being the subject of Q15/17's X.sec_QKD_profr work item.[^21]

**SG15 — transport aspects.** SG15, ITU-T's transport-network Study Group, carries an ancillary remit for the optical-transport aspects relevant to the coexistence of quantum and classical channels over shared DWDM fibre-optical networks. As ITU-T Y.3812's machine-learning use cases acknowledge, "integrating QKD with existing traditional dense wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM) fibre-optical networks is critical to the realization and commercialization of quantum networks", and "when solving the problem of mixed transmission of quantum signal and classical signal, the main challenge is the impact of noise".[^23] SG15's transport-layer expertise is invoked by liaison rather than through a dedicated Q.QKDN work item line; it is therefore documented here for completeness rather than as a primary cataloguing venue.

**Inter-SG and inter-SDO liaison.** The tripartite SG13/SG17/SG11 partitioning is held together by an explicit liaison mechanism. The standardization-perspective overview prepared in 2021 by ETRI and KT for the QKDN agenda explicitly arranges the work across "ITU-T SG13/17/11" and maps each item to the responsible Question, evidencing the deliberate inter-SG coordination of the work programme.[^20] At the level of individual work items, formal liaison statements maintain coordination not only across the three Study Groups but also with external SDOs. The SG17 work item X.sec_QKD_profr declares liaison with "SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD",[^21] and the SG11 work item Q.QKDN_Cq similarly records liaison with "ITU-T Q6&Q16/13, ITU-T Q15/17, ETSI ISG-QKD, ISO/IEC JTC1-SC27".[^22] These liaison declarations are the formal instrument through which ITU-T avoids duplication with ETSI's component-level stack (Chapter 3) and ensures that the security evaluation methodologies developed in ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 27 WG 3 (Chapter 5) interlock with the QKDN security framework developed in SG17.

The institutional mapping is summarized in Table 4.1.

| Venue | Principal Question(s) | Mandate | Document Prefix |
|---|---|---|---|
| SG13 | Q16/13; Q6/13 | QKDN architecture, key management, control, orchestration, SDN, QoS | Y.3800-series |
| SG17 | Q15/17 (formerly Q4/17) | QKDN security framework, security requirements for QKDN elements, QKD protocol framework | X.1710-series (X-series security) |
| SG11 | Q2/11 | Signalling requirements and protocols for QKDN classical-layer interfaces | Q.QKDN-series |
| SG15 | (transport-aspect Questions) | Optical transport, DWDM coexistence | (liaison contribution) |
| FG-QIT4N | WG0–WG2 (Focus Group, time-limited) | Pre-standardization landscape, terminology, use cases, protocol analysis, transport technologies | D-series technical reports |

This mapping establishes the scaffolding for the per-venue catalogues that follow. The Focus Group is treated first (§4.2) because its analytical outputs explicitly identified the gaps that were subsequently absorbed into the parent Study Groups' work programmes.

## 4.2 FG-QIT4N: Pre-Standardization Focus Group on Quantum Information Technologies for Networks

The Focus Group on Quantum Information Technology for Networks (FG-QIT4N) was constituted as the pre-standardization incubator for ITU-T's quantum agenda, operating outside the conventional Question structure of the Study Groups in order to assemble landscape analysis, terminology consolidation, use-case enumeration, and protocol-level gap analysis that could subsequently feed into Recommendations work at SG13, SG17, and SG11. By the end-2022 cutoff, FG-QIT4N had completed its work and delivered a set of technical reports that explicitly shaped the subsequent Study Group work items.[^21]

The Focus Group's substantive scope spanned the four pillars of quantum information technology relevant to networks: "Quantum Information Network", "Quantum Computing", "Quantum Communication", and "Quantum Sensing & Metrology".[^20] Operationally, however, the deliverable structure concentrated heavily on the network-aspects and QKDN strands, with quantum computing and quantum sensing addressed at the landscape level rather than through dedicated specification work—a scope choice consistent with ITU-T's institutional identity as a telecommunications standards body. The internal organisation comprised a Coordination Committee (WG0), a Network-aspects working group (WG1), and a QKDN working group (WG2), with each WG carrying a numbered deliverable plan as set out in Table 4.2.[^20]

| WG | Deliverable | Title | Substantive Function |
|---|---|---|---|
| WG0 (Coordination) | D0.1 | QIT4N standardization landscape and outlook | Cross-SDO mapping of ongoing quantum standardization activities; positioning of ITU-T's contribution |
| WG1 (Network aspects of QIT) | D1.1 | QIT4N terminology part 1: Network aspects of QIT | Vocabulary consolidation for network-aspect work |
| WG1 | D1.2 | QIT4N use case part 1: Network aspects of QIT | Use-case enumeration for network-level QIT applications |
| WG1 | D1.3 | Implications of quantum information technology on networks | Analytical report on the network-architectural implications of broader QIT |
| WG1 | D1.4 | QIT4N standardization outlook and technology maturity part 1: Network aspects of QIT | Maturity assessment and roadmap for network-aspect standardization |
| WG2 (QKDN) | D2.1 | QIT4N terminology part 2: quantum key distribution network | QKDN vocabulary |
| WG2 | D2.2 | QIT4N use case part 2: quantum key distribution network | QKDN use-case enumeration |
| WG2 | D2.3.1 | QKDN protocols part I: Quantum layer | Analytical review of quantum-layer QKD protocols and standardization gap |
| WG2 | D2.3.2 | QKDN protocols part II: key management, QKDN control layer and management layer | Analytical review of classical-layer QKDN protocols and standardization gap |
| WG2 | D2.4 | QKDN transport technologies | Transport-layer technologies relevant to QKD modules |
| WG2 | D2.5 | QIT4N standardization outlook and technology maturity part 2: quantum key distribution network | Maturity assessment and roadmap for QKDN standardization |

The analytical productivity of FG-QIT4N's deliverable set for the subsequent Study Group agenda is most clearly visible in the WG2 protocol reports. D2.3 Part 1 on quantum-layer protocols "gives an overall review and background of QKD protocols from standardization perspectives" and "further analyses and intensifies the gap of QKD protocols standardization and further suggests to initial QKD protocols framework standardization work in SG 17"; this analytical recommendation was directly absorbed by SG17 as the X.sec_QKD_profr work item.[^21] D2.3 Part 2 on classical-layer protocols, addressing key management, the QKDN control layer, and the QKDN management layer, similarly recommended that "new work items on specific protocols should be developed separately at each reference point", and this recommendation was directly absorbed by SG11 as the Q.QKDN-series work programme covering the Ak, Kq-1, Kx, Ck, and Cq reference points.[^22] D2.4 on QKDN transport technologies "introduces the transport technologies on QKD modules" and likewise fed into the SG17 protocol framework work as a foundational reference.[^21]

The institutional pattern visible in this trajectory—Focus Group identifies gap, parent Study Group absorbs gap as work item, work item is allocated by inter-SG liaison to the appropriate Question—is the canonical ITU-T pre-standardization pipeline. Its successful operation in the QIT4N case is one of the structural reasons why ITU-T's quantum corpus, despite being distributed across three Study Groups, exhibits a coherent overall architecture: the conceptual base was assembled in one venue before being routed to the institutionally appropriate Recommendations tracks.

## 4.3 SG13 Y.3800 Series: QKDN Architecture, Key Management, Control, Orchestration, and Quality of Service

The Y.3800-series Recommendations constitute the architectural foundation of ITU-T's QKDN corpus and are the most consolidated body of network-level QKD standardization produced by any SDO surveyed in this report. The Y-series numbering itself reserves the block Y.3800–Y.3999 specifically for "QUANTUM KEY DISTRIBUTION NETWORKS",[^24][^23] giving the series a discrete identifier space within the broader Y-series on "Global information infrastructure, Internet protocol aspects, next-generation networks, Internet of Things and smart cities". The series is allocated to SG13's Q16/13 for the architectural and management strand and Q6/13 for the QoS strand.[^20]

### 4.3.1 Foundational Architectural Recommendations (Q16/13)

The architectural core of the Y.3800-series, approved between 2019 and 2020, comprises five Recommendations that together specify the conceptual structure, functional requirements, functional architecture, key management, and control and management of a QKDN. Recommendation **Y.3800** (approved 10/2019), titled "Overview on networks supporting quantum key distribution", "specifies an overview on networks to support quantum key distribution (QKD) to address network aspects to implement QKD technologies", including "an overview of QKD technologies; network capabilities to support QKD; conceptual structure and basic functions of QKD networks (QKDN)".[^20] **Y.3801** specifies functional requirements for QKDN, covering "Functional requirements for capabilities of quantum/key management/QKDN control and management layers and other capabilities for QKDN".[^20] **Y.3802** specifies the functional architecture, including the "Functional architecture model; Functional elements and reference points; Architectural configurations; Overall operational procedures".[^20] **Y.3803** specifies key management for QKDN, addressing "Requirements of key management; Functional elements of key management; Procedures of key management; Key formats (key data and meta-data)".[^20] **Y.3804** specifies the control and management of QKDN, including "Functional requirements of QKDN control, management, and orchestration; Management information model for QKDN; Reference points of QKDN control, management, and orchestration; Procedures of QKDN control, management, and orchestration".[^20] Y.3801, Y.3802, Y.3803, and Y.3804 are referenced in the November 2023 Supplement 79 bibliography as 2020-dated Recommendations,[^24] consistent with their approval before the end-2022 cutoff.

A sixth Recommendation in this strand, **Y.3805**, addresses Software Defined Networking control for QKDN, covering "Requirements for SDN control in QKDN; Functional architecture of SDN control in QKDN; Reference points of SDN control in QKDN; Hierarchical SDN controller in QKDN; Overall operational procedures of SDN control in QKDN".[^20] Y.3805 extends the Y.3802/Y.3804 reference architecture into the SDN paradigm and provides the ITU-T-side counterpart to the SDN control and orchestration interfaces specified at the component level in ETSI GS QKD 015 and GS QKD 018 (Chapter 3).

### 4.3.2 Quality of Service Sub-Series (Q6/13)

The QoS sub-series, allocated to Q6/13, consolidated rapidly in the 2021–2022 window and produced four Recommendations approved by the cutoff. **Y.3806** (2021), titled "Quantum key distribution networks – Requirements for quality of service assurance", specifies "the requirements including QoS planning, QoS monitoring, QoS optimization, QoS provisioning, QoS protection and recovery".[^23] **Y.3807** (approved 02/2022), titled "Quantum key distribution networks – Quality of service parameters", "describes QoS and network performance (NP) on QKDN and specifies the associated relative parameters for QoS and their definitions".[^24][^23] **Y.3811** (2022), titled "Quantum key distribution networks – Functional architecture for quality of service assurance", "specifies the functional architecture of QoS assurance and basic operational procedures for QKDNs".[^24][^23] **Y.3812** (approved 29 September 2022), titled "Quantum key distribution networks – Requirements for machine learning based quality of service assurance", "specifies high-level and functional requirements of machine learning (ML) based quality of service (QoS) assurance for quantum key distribution networks", including an "Overview of requirements of ML based QoS assurance for QKDNs; Functional model of ML based QoS assurance for QKDNs; High-level requirements of ML based QoS assurance for QKDNs; Functional requirements of ML based QoS assurance for QKDNs; Use cases of ML based QoS assurance for QKDNs".[^23]

Y.3812 is structurally significant because it grafts onto the QKDN architecture an ML-based QoS assurance functional model—comprising "QoS data collection, pre-processing, modelling and training, QoS anomaly detection, QoS anomaly prediction, QoS decision making, and reporting"—and articulates the reference-point requirements by which the ML functions interact with the existing Y.3802/Y.3804 reference points "Mc, Mk, Mq, Mqrp, Mops, Mu and Ma".[^23] This positioning embeds machine-learning-based assurance into the QKDN architecture without displacing it, an architectural pattern that subsequent work items extend.

### 4.3.3 Catalogue of Y.3800-series Approved Recommendations as of End-2022

Table 4.3 consolidates the SG13-approved Y.3800-series Recommendations verifiable on or before 31 December 2022.

| Document | Title | Responsible Q | Approval Date | Scope (summary) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Y.3800 | Overview on networks supporting quantum key distribution | Q16/13 | 10/2019 | Conceptual structure and basic functions of QKDN[^20][^24] |
| Y.3801 | Functional requirements for quantum key distribution networks | Q16/13 | 2020 | Functional requirements across quantum, KM, and control/management layers[^20][^23] |
| Y.3802 | Quantum key distribution networks – Functional architecture | Q16/13 | 2020 | Functional architecture model, elements, reference points, procedures[^20][^23] |
| Y.3803 | Quantum key distribution networks – Key management | Q16/13 | 2020 | KM requirements, elements, procedures, key formats[^20][^24] |
| Y.3804 | Quantum key distribution networks – Control and management | Q16/13 | 2020 | Control, management, and orchestration functional requirements; management information model; procedures[^20][^23] |
| Y.3805 | Software defined networking control for QKDN | Q16/13 | (2020-era) | SDN control architecture for QKDN; hierarchical controllers[^20] |
| Y.3806 | Quantum key distribution networks – Requirements for quality of service assurance | Q6/13 | 2021 | QoS planning, monitoring, optimization, provisioning, protection, recovery[^24][^23] |
| Y.3807 | Quantum key distribution networks – Quality of service parameters | Q6/13 | 02/2022 | QoS and network performance parameters and definitions[^24][^23] |
| Y.3811 | Quantum key distribution networks – Functional architecture for quality of service assurance | Q6/13 | 2022 | Functional architecture and operational procedures for QoS assurance[^24][^23] |
| Y.3812 | Quantum key distribution networks – Requirements for machine learning based quality of service assurance | Q6/13 | 09/2022 | ML-based QoS assurance functional model and requirements[^23] |

### 4.3.4 SG13 Work Items Under Development at the End-2022 Cutoff

Alongside the approved Recommendations, SG13 maintained an active drafting agenda whose composition, as visible in the 2021 standardization-perspective overview prepared for ITU-T, included work items in both Q16/13 and Q6/13.[^20]

Within Q16/13, six work items were active: **Y.QKDN_BM**, "Quantum key distribution networks – Business role-based models", which "describes business roles, business role-based models, and service scenarios in a QKDN from different deployment and operation perspectives" and "identifies various business models that require security application services with a QKDN and existing user networks";[^20] **Y.QKDN_frint**, "Framework for integration of QKDN and secure storage network", which "describes framework for integrating QKDN and secure storage network (SSN)", including "functional requirements for SSN; functional architecture model of SSN; reference points; operational procedures; phase-in scenarios";[^20] **Y.QKDN-iwfr**, "Quantum key distribution networks – interworking framework", which addresses interworking between QKDNs "supported by multiple QKDN providers" in scenarios involving Gateway Functions and Interworking Functions for large-scale QKDN composed of multiple sub-QKDNs;[^20] **Y.QKDN-ml-fra**, "Quantum key distribution networks – Functional requirements and architecture to enable machine learning", which "specifies the roles of ML in a QKDN" and includes "functional requirements and a functional architecture model of the ML-enabled QKDN" with three architectural strategies for ML function placement;[^20] **Y.QKDN-rsfr**, "Quantum key distribution networks – resilience framework", which "specifies the framework of resilience in a QKDN including the conceptual models of QKDN protection and recovery scenarios" and "provides typical use cases of resilience and related requirements of resilience schemes supported by the quantum layer, the key management layer, and QKDN control and management layers";[^20] and **Y.supp.QKDN-roadmap**, "Standardization roadmap on Quantum Key Distribution Networks", which "describes the landscape with related technical areas of trust technologies from an ITU-T perspective and list[s] up related standards and publications developed in other SDOs".[^20]

Within Q6/13, three QoS-track work items were active in the 2021 overview window—**Y.QKDN-QoS-pa** (parameters), **Y.QKDN-QoS-fa** (functional architecture), and **Y.QKDN-QoS-ml-req** (ML-based QoS assurance requirements).[^20] These three are the work-item designators under which Y.3807, Y.3811, and Y.3812 respectively were approved during 2022, illustrating the standard ITU-T transition from work-item identifier to Recommendation number on approval.[^24][^23] The Q6/13 work-item pipeline visible in the overview is therefore largely accounted for by the approved Recommendations catalogued in §4.3.2 by the cutoff.

The six Q16/13 items, by contrast, remained as work items at end-2022 and are recorded with status *Under Study* in the terminology of Chapter 2. ⚠ Their precise drafting stages at the cutoff date are not uniformly confirmable from the consulted primary sources; the inventory therefore records their existence and substantive scope as established at end-2022 while not asserting a more advanced stage than the work-item record supports.

## 4.4 SG17 X.1710 Series: Security Framework and Security Requirements for QKDN

SG17 Q15/17's quantum work, anchored in the X-series numbering reserved for security Recommendations, supplies the security-evaluation underpinning that complements SG13's architectural and management work. The relationship is one of explicit layering: SG13 specifies what a QKDN is and how its components and reference points function; SG17 specifies the threats, security objectives, and security requirements applicable to those components and reference points.

The anchor of the series is **Recommendation X.1710** (approved 2020), titled "Security framework for quantum key distribution networks", which "laydowns the basis of QKDN security".[^21][^23] X.1710 is cited as a normative reference in ITU-T Y.3812 alongside Y.3801 and Y.3802, evidencing the deliberate cross-Study-Group interlock by which SG13 architectural Recommendations explicitly invoke the SG17 security framework as their security foundation.[^23]

Building on X.1710, SG17 Q15/17 developed and approved a series of security requirements Recommendations targeting specific QKDN elements. As recorded in the SG17 X.sec_QKD_profr work-item documentation, "a series of security requirements of QKDN elements have been initiated including X.1712 on key management, X.1715 on integration with secure storage network".[^21] These two Recommendations correspond to the security counterparts of, respectively, the SG13 key management Recommendation Y.3803 and the SG13 QKDN-SSN integration framework Y.QKDN_frint. Within the cluster, the SG17 documentation also identifies **X.1714** as the Recommendation addressing key combination, the security treatment that pairs with the cryptographic combination procedures invoked at the key management layer of the Y.3800 architecture.

Beyond the published anchor and the approved element-level Recommendations, a substantial cluster of draft Recommendations remained under development at the cutoff. The SG17 work-item record explicitly enumerates these as "X.sec_QKDN_tn on trusted node, X.sec_QKDN_CM on QKDN control & management, X.sec_QKDN_AA on authentication and authorization".[^21] The 2021 ETRI/KT overview supplements this enumeration with **X.sec_QKDN_intrq** on infrastructure integration and **X.sec_QKDN_AN&AZ** on authentication and authorization,[^20] the latter being substantively equivalent to X.sec_QKDN_AA and reflecting alternative work-item nomenclatures across the consulted source documents. ⚠ The precise stage of each draft at the cutoff cannot be confirmed at version-level granularity from the available primary sources; their existence and substantive scope as work items under Q15/17 are, however, unambiguously documented.[^20][^21]

The most strategically significant SG17 work item initiated within the report's time window is **X.sec_QKD_profr**, "Framework of quantum key distribution (QKD) protocols in QKD network".[^21] This work item was prompted directly by the FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1 analytical report on quantum-layer QKD protocols, which "further analyses and intensifies the gap of QKD protocols standardization and further suggests to initial QKD protocols framework standardization work in SG 17".[^21] The substantive scope of the work item is "to study the basic aspects of QKD protocols and form a generic framework", covering "Overview of QKD protocols; QKD protocol architecture; and Operations of QKD protocols".[^21] Crucially, the work-item summary states that "Unlike the FG QIT4N D2.3.1 to give an overview of QKD protocols as information, this WI will impose basic requirements on the QKD protocols and relevant security aspects with normative languages, which will establish the QKD protocols framework. This WI will define a framework of the QKD protocols, and can further facilitate the certification of QKD modules that implement QKD protocols".[^21] This passage establishes the work item's role as the normative bridge between the informative analytical content of D2.3 Part 1 and the QKD module certification regime anchored at the component level in ETSI's GS QKD 016 protection profile (Chapter 3).

The status of X.sec_QKD_profr at the end-2022 cutoff is *Initiated / Under Study*. ⚠ The work-item record's earliest TD reference is SG17-TD1019-R1/PLEN of February 2023, the first registration in the Work Programme is dated 6 March 2023, and the item was eventually "Carried to next study period",[^21] all indicating that formal documentation visible from primary sources crystallized in early 2023 even though the substantive initiation followed directly from FG-QIT4N's earlier analytical work. The work item is recorded in this report's inventory as initiated at the boundary of the cutoff, with the ⚠ flag reflecting the limited visibility into pre-February-2023 internal drafting.

The X-series catalogue verifiable at end-2022 is consolidated in Table 4.4.

| Document | Title | Status (end-2022) | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| X.1710 | Security framework for quantum key distribution networks | Approved (2020) | Foundational QKDN security framework[^21][^23] |
| X.1712 | Security requirements for QKDN – key management | Approved | Security requirements for the KM layer (companion to Y.3803)[^21] |
| X.1714 | Security requirements for QKDN – key combination | Approved | Security treatment of key combination at the KM layer[^20] |
| X.1715 | Security requirements for QKDN – integration with secure storage network | Approved | Security requirements for QKDN-SSN integration (companion to Y.QKDN_frint)[^21] |
| X.sec_QKDN_tn | Security requirements for QKDN – trusted node | Under Study (draft) | Trusted-node security requirements[^20][^21] |
| X.sec_QKDN_CM | Security requirements for QKDN – control and management | Under Study (draft) | Security requirements for the control and management plane[^20][^21] |
| X.sec_QKDN_AA / X.sec_QKDN_AN&AZ | Security requirements for QKDN – authentication and authorization | Under Study (draft) | Authentication and authorization across QKDN[^20][^21] |
| X.sec_QKDN_intrq | Security requirements for QKDN – infrastructure integration | Under Study (draft) | Infrastructure-integration security[^20] |
| X.sec_QKD_profr | Framework of quantum key distribution (QKD) protocols in QKD network | Initiated (boundary of cutoff) ⚠ | Generic normative framework for QKD protocols; prompted by FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1[^21] |

The X-series provides the security-evaluation underpinning that interlocks downstream with two distinct external regimes. Upstream from the QKDN operator, the SG17 framework is invoked by SG13 Recommendations such as Y.3812 as the security baseline for any architecture-level work.[^23] Downstream toward equipment certification, the SG17 Recommendations interlock with the formal Common Criteria evaluation track anchored by ETSI GS QKD 016 (Chapter 3) and with the ISO/IEC 23837 series on security requirements and test and evaluation methods for QKD (Chapter 5). The liaison statement attached to X.sec_QKD_profr—"SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD"—is the explicit institutional instrument operationalising this multi-organisational interlock.[^21]

## 4.5 SG11 Q.QKDN Series and SG15 Contributions: Signalling Protocols and Transport Aspects

### 4.5.1 SG11 Q2/11: QKDN Signalling and Interface Protocols

SG11 Q2/11's quantum work occupies the protocol-and-signalling tier of the ITU-T QKDN corpus, addressing the normative specification of the protocols that operate at the classical-layer reference points defined in the SG13 Y.3802/Y.3804 functional architecture. The work was initiated after SG13's architectural Recommendations had stabilised the reference-point taxonomy and after FG-QIT4N's D2.3 Part 2 had recommended that "new work items on specific protocols should be developed separately at each reference point".[^22] As the SG11 work-item record explicitly states, "Following the suggestion, SG11 initiated and recently approved a series of Recommendations on protocol framework and protocols for Ak, Kq-1, Kx and Ck interfaces for QKDNs (ITU-T Q.4160, Q.4161, Q.4162, Q.4163, Q.4164)".[^22] These Q.4160-series numbers represent the approved Recommendation identifiers that the underlying work items received upon approval; the work items themselves are referred to in the standardization-perspective overview by their work-item designators **Q.QKDN_profr** (protocol framework), **Q.QKDN_sig_cm** (signalling for control and management), and the family of interface-specific designators **Q.QKDN_AK**, **Q.QKDN_Kq-1**, **Q.QKDN_Kx**, **Q.QKDN_CK**, and **Q.QKDN_Cq**, each addressing the corresponding Y.3802/Y.3804 reference point.[^20][^21]

The Cq-interface work item, **Q.QKDN_Cq**, is documented in detail in the SG11 work programme: "The key generation control protocol at the Cq interface is quite demanded by QKDN operators and it is supposed to be applicable for different QKDN implementations. To further facilitate QKDN operations, this draft Recommendation will study the protocol at the Cq interface between a QKDN controller and a QKD module for a QKDN".[^22] Its declared liaison set—"ITU-T Q6&Q16/13, ITU-T Q15/17, ETSI ISG-QKD, ISO/IEC JTC1-SC27"—operationalizes the inter-SG and inter-SDO coordination noted in §4.1.[^22] Q.QKDN_Cq is recorded in the work programme as "Carried to next study period", indicating that its substantive drafting had not converged to approval by the boundary of the 2022–2024 study period.[^22]

A critical scope demarcation is recorded explicitly in the SG17 work-item documentation for X.sec_QKD_profr: "QKD protocols are specially mentioned as outside the scope of study in SG 11".[^21] This sentence captures the deliberate inter-SG partitioning by which SG11 addresses only the classical-layer interface protocols of QKDN (the Ak, Kq-1, Kx, Ck, and Cq reference points connecting cryptographic applications, key managers, and QKDN controllers), while the quantum-layer protocols—those that operate between paired QKD modules over the QKD link itself—are assigned to SG17 via X.sec_QKD_profr. This is a clean structural separation: SG11 owns the classical-protocol normative specifications, SG17 owns the quantum-protocol framework, and SG13 owns the architecture that defines the reference points across which both apply.

The SG11 Q.QKDN-series catalogue verifiable at end-2022 is summarized in Table 4.5.

| Work Item / Recommendation | Reference Point / Function | Status (end-2022) | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q.QKDN_profr (→ Q.4160) | Protocol framework for QKDN | Approved/recently approved series ⚠ | Overarching protocol framework for QKDN[^22] |
| Q.QKDN_sig_cm | Signalling requirements and protocols for QKDN control and management | Under Study (draft) | Signalling for the control and management plane[^20] |
| Q.QKDN_AK (→ Q.4161 series) | Ak reference point | Approved in the recently approved series ⚠ | Ak interface protocol between cryptographic application and KM[^22] |
| Q.QKDN_Kq-1 (→ Q.4162 series) | Kq-1 reference point | Approved in the recently approved series ⚠ | Kq-1 interface protocol[^22] |
| Q.QKDN_Kx (→ Q.4163 series) | Kx reference point | Approved in the recently approved series ⚠ | Kx interface protocol[^22] |
| Q.QKDN_CK (→ Q.4164 series) | Ck reference point | Approved in the recently approved series ⚠ | Ck interface protocol between QKDN controller and KM[^22] |
| Q.QKDN_Cq | Cq reference point | Under Study; Carried to next study period | Protocol between QKDN controller and QKD module[^22] |

⚠ The recorded Q.4160–Q.4164 series approval is established by the SG11 work-item record's retrospective description of "recently approved" Recommendations as documented in 2024.[^22] At the strict end-2022 cutoff, the corresponding work items are therefore recorded conservatively as *Under Study (Initiated)* in this report's primary inventory, with the post-2022 approval status flagged as boundary-of-cutoff context. The substantive scope of each item—the per-reference-point classical-layer interface protocol—and the institutional assignment to Q2/11 are unambiguously documented at end-2022 in the 2021 ETRI/KT overview and the subsequent work-item records.[^22][^20]

### 4.5.2 SG15 Contribution: Optical Transport Aspects

SG15's contribution to ITU-T's quantum corpus operates at the transport-network layer, addressing the optical infrastructure over which both quantum and classical channels of a QKDN must coexist. As the Y.3812 use-case appendix observes, "Integrating QKD with existing traditional dense wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM) fibre-optical networks is critical to the realization and commercialization of quantum networks. When solving the problem of mixed transmission of quantum signal and classical signal, the main challenge is the impact of noise, which has a significant impact on the QoS of quantum signal".[^23] SG15's transport-Recommendation portfolio—particularly its work on DWDM frequency grids, optical transport network management, and physical-layer impairments—provides the underlying engineering frame within which QKD-over-fibre deployment occurs. ⚠ No dedicated Q.QKDN-equivalent work item in SG15 is identified in the consulted primary sources at the end-2022 cutoff; SG15's contribution is recorded here as ancillary and liaison-based rather than as a primary cataloguing venue, consistent with §4.1.

## 4.6 Assessment of ITU-T's Network-Level Quantum Standardization: Coverage, Complementarity with ETSI, and Residual Gaps

When the per-venue catalogues of §§4.2–4.5 are mapped against the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1, ITU-T's quantum standardization profile as of end-2022 displays a sharply focused centre of gravity and a complementary relationship with ETSI's component-level stack that—taken together—accounts for most of the global QKD/QKDN normative footprint. Table 4.6 summarizes this mapping.

| Taxonomy Domain | ITU-T Coverage as of End-2022 | Coverage Strength |
|---|---|---|
| QKD & quantum communication (network-level) | Y.3800-series architecture (Y.3800/3801/3802/3803/3804); SDN control (Y.3805); QoS sub-series (Y.3806/3807/3811/3812); X.1710-series security framework and element-level security Recommendations; Q.QKDN-series interface protocols | Comprehensive at the network-operator and treaty-context layer |
| Post-quantum cryptography | Not in scope as a standalone domain; intersection points anticipated in later Supplement 79 (post-cutoff) on QKDN integration with non-quantum cryptography | Not covered at end-2022 |
| Quantum computing | Not in scope | Not covered |
| Quantum networking / quantum internet | Not in scope; FG-QIT4N landscape work touches "Quantum Information Network" at landscape level only[^20] | Not covered at the protocol level |
| Quantum random number generation | Touched via SG17 work on "applications of QRNG for security"[^20] | Marginal coverage |
| Quantum sensing and metrology | Surveyed at landscape level by FG-QIT4N[^20] | Not covered as a standardization domain |

**Centre of gravity.** ITU-T's quantum standardization centre of gravity is unambiguously QKDN at the network-operator layer. Within that scope, the coverage is exceptionally consolidated: the Y.3800-series articulates the conceptual structure, functional requirements, functional architecture, key management, control and management, SDN control, and quality of service of a QKDN; the X.1710-series articulates the security framework and element-level security requirements; the Q.QKDN-series articulates the classical-layer interface protocols. No other SDO surveyed in this report has assembled this depth of network-level treatment for QKD.

**Complementarity with ETSI.** The relationship with the ETSI stack catalogued in Chapter 3 is one of deliberate, liaison-mediated complementarity rather than overlap. ETSI's ISG QKD specifies how a QKD module behaves at the component level (modules, components, internal interfaces, application APIs in GS QKD 004 and GS QKD 014, SDN control and orchestration interfaces in GS QKD 015 and GS QKD 018) and how it is certified (Common Criteria Protection Profile in GS QKD 016); ITU-T specifies how operator networks compose those modules into services (Y.3800-series), how their security is framed (X.1710-series), and how their interfaces are normatively realized in signalling protocols (Q.QKDN-series). The two stacks meet at well-defined interface points: ETSI's GS QKD 004/014 key delivery APIs realize, at the protocol level, the access from cryptographic applications to QKDN key supply that the Ak reference point of Y.3802 abstracts; ETSI's GS QKD 015/018 SDN interfaces realize, at the equipment-vendor level, the SDN control architecture specified at the network-architecture level in Y.3805. The liaison declarations attached to SG11's Q.QKDN_Cq and SG17's X.sec_QKD_profr—both of which explicitly list "ETSI ISG-QKD" among their declared liaison partners—are the formal institutional instrument by which this complementarity is maintained in real time.[^22][^21]

A further complementarity, downstream toward formal evaluation, is the linkage from SG17's X.1710-series and X.sec_QKD_profr to the ISO/IEC 23837 evaluation methodology addressed in Chapter 5. The X.sec_QKD_profr work item's liaison set "SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD" makes this triangular interlock explicit: SG17 develops the QKD protocol security framework, ETSI ISG QKD provides the Common Criteria Protection Profile, and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3 provides the international test and evaluation methodology, with all three coordinating through declared liaison.[^21]

**Residual gaps.** Four gaps in ITU-T's quantum coverage at end-2022 are identifiable and structurally significant.

*First*, the absence of approved normative QKD protocol-level Recommendations. X.sec_QKD_profr—the work item that would have provided the normative framework for QKD protocols themselves, as distinct from the classical-layer interface protocols of SG11—was at most initiated at the boundary of the cutoff, with its first formally documented TD references appearing in early 2023.[^21] The FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1 analytical report had identified the gap and recommended SG17 action,[^21] but the corresponding normative Recommendation had not advanced to draft status by end-2022. This leaves a structural lacuna between the informative D2.3 Part 1 and the formal Common Criteria certification of QKD modules: certification authorities and equipment vendors at the cutoff had ETSI GS QKD 016 as a Protection Profile and FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1 as an informative analysis, but no ITU-T Recommendation imposing normative requirements on QKD protocols themselves.

*Second*, the incomplete coverage of QKDN integration with non-quantum cryptography. The architectural challenge that arises when QKDN delivery extends to mobile or otherwise quantum-channel-unreachable endpoints—where keys generated within QKDN must be delivered to endpoints "through a user network using modern cryptography technology"—is foreshadowed in the November 2023 Supplement 79 to the Y.3800-series.[^24] Supplement 79 is explicitly post-cutoff and is recorded here only as retrospective context for a gap visible at end-2022: as of the cutoff, no ITU-T deliverable normatively specified the integration of QKDN with PQC or with classical PKI-based key exchange to address mobile-object and similar quantum-channel-limited scenarios. The 2023 Supplement subsequently articulated the use cases and identified the implications for SG13 Q16/13 and Q6/13 study,[^24] but the corresponding architectural work remained outside the end-2022 evidence window.

*Third*, the complete absence of ITU-T work on quantum computing. ITU-T's institutional remit as a telecommunications standards body excludes the engineering and benchmarking concerns of quantum-computing hardware that occupy IEEE's quantum-computing working groups (Chapter 6). The Focus Group's "Quantum Computing" pillar at landscape level[^20] is the only ITU-T touch-point with the domain at end-2022, and it produces no Recommendations in the domain.

*Fourth*, the absence of ITU-T work on end-to-end quantum internet protocols. The FG-QIT4N work scope nominally encompasses "Quantum Information Network",[^20] but no Recommendations on entanglement distribution, quantum repeater control, or quantum-internet application protocols were initiated within ITU-T at the cutoff. This is the substantive territory occupied by IRTF's Quantum Internet Research Group (Chapter 7), and ITU-T's institutional choice to leave this domain to the IRTF pre-standardization track is structurally consistent with the maturity profile of the field at end-2022.

**Trajectory at the cutoff.** Taken in aggregate, ITU-T's quantum standardization trajectory at end-2022 displays a strong network-architectural foundation completed in the 2019–2022 window, an active and expanding security-track work programme that anchors element-level evaluation, an emerging interface-protocol track in SG11 whose approvals are at the boundary of the cutoff, and a clear next-phase agenda comprising the QKD protocol framework (X.sec_QKD_profr), inter-QKDN interworking (Y.QKDN-iwfr), resilience (Y.QKDN-rsfr), business role modelling (Y.QKDN_BM), QKDN-SSN integration (Y.QKDN_frint), and ML-enabled architecture (Y.QKDN-ml-fra). The structural division of labour with ETSI is operational and stable; the structural division with ISO/IEC and IRTF is similarly stable in its scoping of formal evaluation methodologies and quantum-internet research respectively. These observations carry directly into Chapter 5, where ISO/IEC's complementary contribution—particularly through JTC 1/SC 27 WG 3's security evaluation methodology and the broader cross-domain coordination provided by IEC SEG 14 and JTC 3—is examined as the third institutional pillar of the global QKD/QKDN normative architecture.

# 5 ISO/IEC: Joint Technical Committees and Quantum-Related Standards

Chapter 4 closed by observing that ITU-T's QKDN standardization trajectory rests on a stable triangular liaison with ETSI ISG QKD at the component layer and with ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 27 WG 3 at the formal evaluation layer. This chapter turns to the third vertex of that triangle. ISO/IEC's contribution to the global quantum standardization landscape is structurally distinct from both ETSI's and ITU-T's: whereas ETSI develops industry specifications largely within a European telecommunications constituency and ITU-T produces Recommendations within a UN treaty-context, ISO/IEC produces full International Standards through a national-body ballot process that gives its deliverables the highest formal authority of any of the SDOs surveyed in this report. As of the end-2022 cutoff, this authority had been applied to two distinct quantum domains: the formal **security evaluation methodology for QKD modules**, developed in SC 27 WG 3 and embodied in the ISO/IEC 23837 two-part series; and the foundational **terminology and overview of quantum computing**, developed in JTC 1/WG 14 and embodied in ISO/IEC 4879 and ISO/IEC TR 18157. The chapter proceeds in six sections, following the outline structure: the institutional architecture and mandate allocation (§5.1), the SC 27 WG 3 QKD security evaluation track (§5.2), the WG 14 quantum computing track (§5.3), the adjacent ISO/IEC bodies and PQC considerations (§5.4), the consolidated catalogue (§5.5), and the assessment of complementarity and residual gaps (§5.6).

## 5.1 Institutional Architecture: JTC 1 Subcommittees, Working Groups, and the Allocation of Quantum Mandates

ISO/IEC JTC 1 — formally entitled "Information technology" — is a joint technical committee of the International Organization for Standardization (ISO) and the International Electrotechnical Commission (IEC), formed in 1987 "as a merger between ISO/TC 97 (Information Technology) and IEC/TC 83, with IEC/SC 47B joining later", with the intent "to bring together, in a single committee, the IT standardization activities of the two parent organizations in order to avoid duplicative or possibly incompatible standards"[^25]. JTC 1's scope is "international standardization in the field of information technology", and its mandate explicitly covers the design and development of IT systems and tools, their performance and quality, the security of IT systems and information, portability and interoperability, harmonized IT vocabulary, and user interface ergonomics[^25]. The committee is large: "There are over 2,800 published JTC 1 standards developed by about 2,100 technical experts from around the world"[^25]. Its secretariat is held by the American National Standards Institute (ANSI), and as of mid-2021 it counted 35 P-members and 65 O-members for a total of 100 member national bodies[^25].

A procedural feature of JTC 1 directly relevant to the quantum standardization context is the **PAS transposition process**, by which "publicly available specifications" developed by consortia can be admitted as ISO/IEC International Standards "in less than a year, as opposed to a full length process that can take up to 4 years"[^25]. While quantum-domain projects surveyed here have not principally used the PAS route, the existence of the route is structurally relevant because it constitutes the formal mechanism by which industry-consortium specifications (such as those originating in ETSI ISGs) could in principle be elevated to ISO/IEC International Standard status, reinforcing the inter-SDO complementarity examined in §5.6.

The structural sub-units of JTC 1 — Subcommittees (SCs), Working Groups (WGs) operating directly under JTC 1, Advisory Groups (AGs), Special Working Groups (SWGs), and Study Groups (SGs) — partition the IT standardization landscape into domain-specific venues. Within this sub-unit landscape, **three venues carry the principal quantum mandates** as of end-2022:

- **ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 27** — "Information security, cybersecurity and privacy protection"[^25] — hosts the QKD security evaluation track within its WG 3 (Security evaluation, testing and specification), and the post-quantum cryptography algorithmic track within its WG 2 (Cryptography and security mechanisms);
- **ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14** — "Quantum computing" — operates directly under JTC 1 rather than under a Subcommittee, an institutional choice that reflects both the cross-cutting nature of quantum computing across IT domains and the early-stage maturity of the standardization work; and
- **adjacent IEC and JTC 1-level coordination bodies** that handle horizontal coordination across quantum technologies more broadly.

JTC 1/WG 14's listing as an active Working Group in the JTC 1 structure is confirmed in the JTC 1 sub-unit register[^25], and the dual role of WG 14 as both the institutional anchor for quantum computing standardization and the venue chaired by Chinese leadership was reaffirmed at the 43rd JTC 1 Plenary held in Berlin, where "Chinese experts were re-elected as conveners of AG19 (Unmanned Aircraft Systems Advisory Group) and WG14 (Quantum Information Technologies Working Group)"[^26]. This continuity of leadership across study periods—visible in the Plenary's confirmation of the convenership at the most senior committee level—has been a structural enabler of the WG 14 work programme's consistency from its 2020 establishment through end-2022 and beyond.

The lifecycle of any project admitted to one of these venues is governed by the **ISO/IEC Directives, Part 1, 2022 edition**, which codifies the multi-stage progression from New Work Item Proposal (NWIP) through Committee Draft (CD), Draft International Standard (DIS), Final Draft International Standard (FDIS), and ultimately publication as International Standard (IS). The procedural anchoring of each transition in the Directives—and the requirement that NWIPs be supported by Form 4 documentation including justification, scope, project leader designation, and a draft text—is operationally important for understanding the recorded stages of quantum-related projects: an item described as "passing CD ballot in April 2022"[^27] has cleared the first multi-national ballot threshold but remains two formal stages short of publication. The full procedural apparatus is operationalized at the national level through technical mirror committees; for SC 27 work this is the China secretariat function described in the SC 27 international standard proposal solicitation notices issued in May and July 2022 by the National Information Security Standardization Technical Committee[^28][^29], and for WG 14 work this is the WG 14 domestic technical counterpart group hosted by the China Electronics Standardization Institute (CESI)[^27][^30].

Two further institutional features shape the quantum allocation. First, **liaison relationships** are the formal instrument through which JTC 1 sub-units coordinate with external SDOs. JTC 1 itself maintains direct liaison with the International Telecommunication Union (ITU), including ITU-T[^25], and operational liaison at the sub-unit level extends this relationship into the quantum domain through the X.sec_QKD_profr liaison set noted in Chapter 4 ("SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD"). The 43rd JTC 1 Plenary's confirmation that Chinese expert Qian Heng serves as the "JTC1-ITU-T liaison officer"[^26] illustrates that this coordination is institutionally personified at the senior level. Second, **the WG 11 Smart Cities precedent**—in which 12 international standard projects have been successfully initiated and the secretariat is hosted in China[^26]—provides an organizational model for how a JTC 1-level Working Group can sustain a long-running work programme, a model that is broadly applicable to WG 14's evolving quantum computing portfolio.

The institutional scaffold thus constructed allocates quantum mandates as summarized in Table 5.1.

| Venue | Position in JTC 1 Structure | Quantum Mandate | Principal Deliverables (end-2022) |
|---|---|---|---|
| SC 27/WG 3 | Subcommittee Working Group | QKD security requirements, test and evaluation methods | ISO/IEC 23837-1, ISO/IEC 23837-2 |
| SC 27/WG 2 | Subcommittee Working Group | Cryptographic primitives, including PQ-KEM and hash-based signatures | ISO/IEC 14888 series; ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM project |
| JTC 1/WG 14 | JTC 1-level Working Group | Quantum computing terminology, overview, applications | ISO/IEC 4879; ISO/IEC TR 18157; ISO/IEC PWI 18660; ISO/IEC PWI 18670 |
| IEC SEG 14 / JTC 3 (adjacent) | IEC Standardization Evaluation Group; JTC | Horizontal coordination, including quantum sensing/metrology mapping | Landscape mapping outputs (not catalogued individually here) ⚠ |

With this institutional scaffold established, §5.2–§5.4 turn to the per-venue catalogue.

## 5.2 SC 27 WG 3: ISO/IEC 23837 Series and QKD Security Evaluation Methodology

The flagship quantum-related output of SC 27 WG 3 is the **two-part ISO/IEC 23837 series on "Information security — Security requirements, test and evaluation methods for quantum key distribution"**, comprising Part 1 (security requirements) and Part 2 (test and evaluation methods). The series is described in the SC 27 record as "specifying security requirements, test and evaluation methods for quantum key distribution (QKD)"[^30], and Part 2 is characterized as "specifying test and evaluation methods for the security evaluation of quantum key distribution (QKD)" and as "describing evaluation activities" required at the expected evaluation assurance levels[^31][^32]. The procedural relationship between the two parts is explicit: Part 2 "is intended to specify evaluation activities that are necessary for the security evaluation of QKD modules at the expected evaluation assurance"[^32], implementing in evaluation-methodology form the security requirements specified in Part 1.

The **project history** is unusually well documented because of its sustained Chinese leadership and the public communications of the participating Chinese institutions. The project was initiated in November 2017, when the China Information Technology Security Evaluation Center (CNITSEC) and QuantumCTek "jointly initiated this international standard project at the ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 27 working group meeting held in Berlin"[^33]. The motivation captured in the project initiation is that "although the information-theoretic security of QKD has been proven, defects or deviations from the security model can still arise during system development, threatening the security of actual QKD systems", and that "QKD modules, like other cryptographic modules, also need to undergo rigorous security testing and evaluation before they can be deployed in practical applications"[^33]. Over the subsequent five years, "CNITSEC and QuantumCTek jointly worked with domestic and international technical experts to repeatedly discuss the standard content", and "after five years, in October 2022, with the attention and guidance of the relevant national authorities, and with the collaboration and support of the United Kingdom, Singapore, Luxembourg, and other countries", the standard "was announced at the SC27 working group meeting and plenary meeting to have entered the publication phase"[^33].

This October 2022 announcement is the **most advanced verifiable stage at the end-2022 cutoff**: the two-part series had cleared the substantive ballot process and entered the publication-preparation phase, with formal publication anticipated for 2023[^33]. The signalling around the publication phase—communicated as the moment at which "the world once again heard the 'Chinese voice' in the field of quantum information technology standardization"[^33]—reflects the project's strategic significance both as the first systematic international standard specifically regulating QKD security testing technology[^33] and as a marker of evolving leadership patterns in the international quantum standards process. Under the methodology of Chapter 2, the project is recorded at end-2022 as **at the FDIS/publication-preparation stage**, with the formal publication date of ISO/IEC 23837-1:2023 and ISO/IEC 23837-2:2023[^31][^32][^30] flagged as post-cutoff confirmation of the trajectory visible at the boundary of the report's evidence window.

The **substantive scope** of the series, as recorded in the publication record, is to specify "a set of strict and general security specifications for QKD modules" providing "international authoritative standard guidance for the design and security evaluation of quantum key distribution products" and contributing to "the optimization and upgrading of the industrial structure in the field of quantum communication" and "the completion of the commercial cryptography system"[^33]. The series introduction confirms that "the ISO/IEC 23837 series specifies security requirements, test and evaluation methods for quantum key distribution (QKD)"[^30], with Part 1 establishing the requirements baseline and Part 2 operationalizing the evaluation activities. Part 2's substantive content is characterized as specifying the evaluation activities "necessary for the security evaluation of QKD modules at the expected evaluation assurance level"[^32], situating the document within the Common Criteria assurance framework that underpins formal product certification regimes internationally.

This positioning places ISO/IEC 23837 at the **apex of the QKD assurance chain** that runs from component-level specifications through certification-ready protection profiles to formal international evaluation methodology. The chain operates as follows: ETSI's GS QKD 002, 008, 011, 012 and the related GR series (Chapter 3) specify QKD modules and components and articulate use cases and security objectives at the industry-specification level; ETSI's GS QKD 016 Common Criteria Protection Profile for the pair of prepare-and-measure QKD modules (Chapter 3) provides the certifiable evaluation target at the Protection Profile level within the Common Criteria framework; ITU-T's X.1710 series (Chapter 4) provides the network-level security framework and element-specific security requirements; and ISO/IEC 23837-1/-2 provides the formal international standard for security requirements and the test and evaluation methodology against which evaluation laboratories perform Common Criteria evaluations. The explicit liaison declaration on ITU-T SG17's X.sec_QKD_profr work item—"SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD" (Chapter 4)—operationalizes this multi-tier interlock by ensuring that the ISO/IEC, ITU-T, and ETSI strands of the chain are coordinated in real time.

The institutional context surrounding the 23837 series at end-2022 also reflects the broader SC 27 work programme dynamics. The National Information Security Standardization Technical Committee (信安标委) issued in May 2022 and again in July 2022 formal solicitations for SC 27 international standard proposals, identifying SC 27 as "the international technical organization specifically responsible for cybersecurity standard development work" and indicating that proposals could be directed to any of SC 27's five Working Groups: WG 1 (information security management systems), WG 2 (cryptography and security mechanisms), WG 3 (security evaluation, testing and specification), WG 4 (security controls and services), and WG 5 (identity management and privacy technologies)[^28][^29]. This standing solicitation infrastructure—operating with formal Forms 1–6 covering proposal information, NWIP audit, project justification, ISO Form 04, draft text, and a commitment letter on undertaking the international proposal[^28][^29]—is the procedural channel through which projects such as the 23837 series are originated and sustained over their multi-year ballot lifecycle. The five-year drafting horizon of the 23837 series from 2017 initiation to October 2022 publication-preparation announcement[^33] is consistent with the procedural cadence that the SC 27 NWIP-to-IS pipeline imposes.

## 5.3 JTC 1/WG 14: Quantum Computing Terminology, Overview, and Emerging Projects

JTC 1/WG 14, listed in the JTC 1 sub-unit register as "Quantum computing"[^25], is the institutional home of ISO/IEC quantum computing standardization. The Working Group was established in 2020 and operates with secretariat hosted in China, with CESI serving as the domestic technical counterpart organization and the WG 14 convenership held by Chinese leadership re-elected at the 43rd JTC 1 Plenary[^26]. The substantive work programme as of end-2022 is anchored by two flagship items—the foundational terminology standard ISO/IEC 4879 and the companion technical report ISO/IEC TR 18157—with two preliminary work items (PWIs) extending the portfolio into machine learning datasets and simulation platforms. The work programme was reviewed and advanced at the eighth WG 14 meeting held by teleconference on 19–22 September 2022, with the Working Group convener Yang Hong of CESI chairing the session[^30].

**ISO/IEC 4879 — "Information technology — Quantum computing — Terminology and vocabulary"** is the **first quantum computing international standard project** in the JTC 1 work programme[^27]. The project was formally initiated within ISO/IEC JTC 1 in June 2020 and assigned to WG 14 for development, with CESI's Lei Gen serving as principal editor and Zhang Chi as joint editor[^27]. The substantive content of the standard is described as giving "more than 50 quantum computing terms and definitions" structured along four axes: "basic concepts, hardware part, software part, and applications part"[^27]. The drafting process involved "nearly 20 online editorial meetings" over the period from initiation in June 2020 to the formation of the CD document in February 2022[^27], and on **27 April 2022 the international standard ISO/IEC 4879 successfully passed the Committee Draft (CD) vote**[^27]. The CD ballot completion is explicitly characterized as having significance "for reaching conceptual consensus in the field of quantum computing"[^27], positioning ISO/IEC 4879 as the cross-SDO conceptual anchor that any subsequent quantum computing standardization—whether at ISO/IEC, IEEE, or elsewhere—can reference.

Under the methodology of Chapter 2, ISO/IEC 4879's stage at the 31 December 2022 cutoff is **CD-passed**, two formal stages (DIS, FDIS) short of publication as an International Standard. The September 2022 eighth WG 14 meeting confirmed continued progression of the item: "ISO/IEC CD 4879 'Information technology — Quantum computing — Terminology and vocabulary' standard chief editor Lei Gen reported on the standard comment-handling meeting and confirmed the next-step advancement plan"[^30]. The continued post-CD comment resolution work indicates that, while the CD-stage threshold had been cleared by the cutoff, drafting activity on Part-level technical content remained active.

**ISO/IEC TR 18157 — "Information technology — Quantum computing — Overview"** is the companion technical report developed in WG 14 to provide an informative overview of the quantum computing domain that contextualizes the normative terminology of ISO/IEC 4879. At the September 2022 eighth WG 14 meeting, "ISO/IEC TR 18157 'Information technology — Quantum computing — Overview' technical report chief editor Wang Jingjing reported on the technical report's update progress, with subsequent plans to solicit comments within the working group"[^30]. The status of the technical report at end-2022 is therefore **Under Development** with active drafting and the next milestone being the in-WG comment solicitation; the precise stage code (PWI, DTR, etc.) is not unambiguously confirmable from the consulted primary sources and is flagged ⚠.

Beyond these two flagship items, the September 2022 meeting record documents two further Preliminary Work Items active in the WG 14 portfolio. **ISO/IEC PWI 18660 — "Information technology — Quantum machine learning datasets"** is reported by chief editor Li Xiaoyu, with the September meeting devoted to discussing the project's "research purposes, reference framework, and other content"[^30]. **ISO/IEC PWI 18670 — "Information technology — General requirements for quantum resource simulation platforms"** is reported by chief editor Jiang Jinzhe, also addressing project progress and reference framework[^30]. Both items are at the **PWI (Preliminary Work Item)** stage at end-2022—a stage that precedes formal NWIP balloting and represents the earliest documentable position within the ISO/IEC project lifecycle. As such, the items are recorded in the inventory as exploratory work items whose taxonomic placement (PWI 18660 within applications, intersecting quantum computing with machine learning; PWI 18670 within software/tooling, intersecting quantum computing with simulation infrastructure) extends the WG 14 portfolio beyond the foundational terminology-and-overview core into application- and platform-oriented domains.

The September 2022 meeting also documented a **quantum computing roadmap** drafting plan, with WG 14 convener Yang Hong "organizing the participating experts to discuss the quantum computing roadmap preparation plan"[^30]. This roadmap activity—a planning rather than a deliverable artefact at end-2022—is structurally significant because it positions WG 14 to coordinate its portfolio expansion against an internal scoping document, a function analogous to ITU-T's Y.supp.QKDN-roadmap work item discussed in Chapter 4. The September 2022 meeting brought together experts from China, the United States, the United Kingdom, Australia, France, Spain, Japan, and other national member bodies[^30], evidencing the multilateral participation base that the work programme has sustained.

The composition of the Chinese delegation at the WG 14 meeting—drawn from CESI, Inspur Electronic Information Industry Co. Ltd., the University of Electronic Science and Technology of China, and the Jinan Institute of Quantum Technology[^30]—mirrors the constituency that the 43rd JTC 1 Plenary delegation drew upon, which included representatives from the State Administration for Market Regulation's Standards Innovation Department, CESI, and Huawei Technologies[^26]. This stability of the participating organizational ecosystem from project initiation through plenary-level decision-making is a structural enabler of the multi-year drafting horizons that the ISO/IEC process requires.

A central analytical observation about WG 14's work programme as of end-2022 is its **deliberate emphasis on terminology and overview rather than benchmarking or architecture**. ISO/IEC 4879 and TR 18157 together establish the conceptual base; PWI 18660 and PWI 18670 extend into application- and platform-oriented territory but at the earliest documentable stage. There is, by end-2022, no WG 14 standardization analogue of the IEEE quantum computing benchmarking projects (Chapter 6) or of the architecture-specific projects in the IEEE P3120 family. This emphasis is consistent with the early-stage maturity of underlying quantum computing hardware platforms—where heterogeneity across superconducting, trapped-ion, photonic, and other modalities discourages premature benchmark or architecture standardization—and with the institutional logic by which ISO/IEC's most authoritative-tier standards are typically developed only after broader conceptual consensus is in place. The conceptual consensus, in turn, is what ISO/IEC 4879 is designed to produce.

## 5.4 Adjacent ISO/IEC Bodies: SC 27 PQC Considerations, IEC SEG 14, and Cross-Domain Coordination

Beyond the two core venues of SC 27 WG 3 and JTC 1/WG 14, three further strands of ISO/IEC activity touch the quantum standardization landscape: SC 27 WG 2's treatment of post-quantum cryptographic primitives, IEC SEG 14's horizontal mapping function for quantum technologies, and JTC 3's emerging coordination role. These are addressed jointly in this section because each, in its own way, supplements the core JTC 1/SC 27 and JTC 1/WG 14 work without itself producing a comparably consolidated quantum-specific deliverable corpus.

**SC 27 WG 2 and post-quantum cryptography.** SC 27 WG 2, characterized in the SC 27 proposal solicitation as the "cryptography and security mechanisms working group"[^28][^29], is the SC 27 venue responsible for cryptographic primitives and their standardization. Two strands of WG 2 work intersect with the quantum threat model as defined in Chapter 1.

The first is the **ISO/IEC 14888 digital signature series**, whose Part 4 — "Information security — Digital signatures with appendix — Part 4: Stateful hash-based mechanisms" — standardizes stateful hash-based signature mechanisms "where the security level is determined by the security properties of the underlying hash function" and provides "requirements for implementing basic state management, which is necessary for the secure deployment of the stateful schemes described in this document"[^25]. The document covers terms including authentication paths, balanced binary trees, L-trees, Merkle trees, and one-time signature schemes[^25], reflecting the formal cryptographic foundation of hash-based signatures such as XMSS, XMSS^MT, LMS, and HSS. Although the formal publication date of ISO/IEC 14888-4 is 2024[^25], the substantive standardization activity on stateful hash-based mechanisms had been ongoing within WG 2 across the report's evidence window, paralleling the recognition in national-authority guidance such as the German BSI's Technical Guideline TR-02102-1 of LMS/HSS and XMSS/XMSS^MT as the recommended stateful hash-based signature schemes[^33]. The 2024 publication date is therefore flagged as a post-cutoff confirmation of an ongoing trajectory rather than as a deliverable verifiable at end-2022.

The second strand is the **ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM standardization track**. As characterized in the German BSI overview of post-quantum standardization activities, "ISO/IEC 18033-2" is a "Standardisation project for PQ-KEMs" considering candidates including "FrodoKEM", "Classic McEliece", and "ML-KEM (CRYSTALS-Kyber)"[^33]. The 18033-2 track absorbs into the ISO/IEC framework the post-quantum key encapsulation mechanisms that the NIST process has surfaced as principal candidates, providing an international-standard channel for their normative specification independent of the NIST FIPS publication trajectory. ⚠ The precise stage of each candidate's integration into 18033-2 at end-2022 is not unambiguously confirmable from the consulted primary sources; the existence of the track and its substantive scope are, however, documented in the BSI overview[^33]. The relationship between ISO/IEC's 18033-2 PQ-KEM track and the multiple PQC standardization projects under way in parallel within IETF/IRTF for OpenPGP, Cryptographic Message Syntax (CMS), X.509, TLS 1.3, IKEv2, and other protocols[^33] establishes a layered standardization architecture in which ISO/IEC provides the algorithm-level normative specifications and IETF/IRTF provides the protocol-level integration specifications.

The relationship between WG 2's PQC work and WG 3's QKD evaluation work is one of **complementary defence architectures within a single Subcommittee**: WG 2 addresses the classical-algorithmic defence against quantum-era cryptanalysis (PQC), while WG 3 addresses the formal evaluation of the quantum-native defence (QKD modules). This dual-track architecture within SC 27 mirrors the dual-track architecture within ETSI noted in Chapter 3 (ISG QKD plus TC CYBER WG QSC), and the parallel is instructive: both ETSI and SC 27 have institutionally separated QKD-specific work from PQC algorithmic work, recognizing that the two domains—although both responses to the quantum threat—require different specialist expertise and different drafting cadences.

**IEC SEG 14 and horizontal coordination.** Beyond JTC 1, the IEC operates Standardization Evaluation Groups (SEGs) that perform horizontal landscape mapping across emerging technology domains. IEC SEG 14 carries the quantum technologies remit, providing a horizontal coordination function that complements the more vertical work of SC 27 (security-specific) and WG 14 (quantum computing-specific). Within the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1, IEC SEG 14's mapping function is particularly significant for the **quantum sensing and metrology** domain, which is otherwise unaddressed across the ISO/IEC quantum portfolio at end-2022. ⚠ The specific output set of IEC SEG 14 at the cutoff—typically taking the form of scoping reports and recommendations to the parent IEC Standardization Management Board rather than International Standards—is not catalogued at item-level granularity in the consulted primary sources; its institutional role is, however, clearly the horizontal coordination function that JTC 1's vertical structure does not by itself supply.

**JTC 3 and emerging coordination.** The ISO/IEC standardization architecture has continued to evolve through the establishment of additional Joint Technical Committees, and JTC 3 has emerged as an institutional venue that may carry coordination functions on quantum technologies extending beyond the JTC 1 information-technology remit. ⚠ The specific scope and end-2022 deliverable status of JTC 3 with respect to quantum technologies is not unambiguously documented in the consulted primary sources, and the body is therefore recorded here for completeness rather than catalogued at item-level granularity.

The **division of labour across the three SC 27 Working Groups touching the quantum domain** is clean and complementary, and is summarized in Table 5.2.

| SC 27 WG | Focus | Quantum-Relevant Work |
|---|---|---|
| WG 2 | Cryptography and security mechanisms[^28][^29] | ISO/IEC 14888 stateful hash-based signatures[^25]; ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM track absorbing FrodoKEM, Classic McEliece, ML-KEM[^33] |
| WG 3 | Security evaluation, testing and specification[^28][^29] | ISO/IEC 23837-1 and ISO/IEC 23837-2 QKD security requirements and test/evaluation methods[^31][^32][^30][^33] |
| WG 14 (JTC 1-level) | Quantum computing (JTC 1 WG, not SC 27 WG)[^25] | ISO/IEC 4879 terminology[^27][^30]; ISO/IEC TR 18157 overview[^30]; ISO/IEC PWI 18660, PWI 18670[^30] |

The horizontal supplement to this vertical structure is the IEC SEG 14 mapping function for quantum sensing and metrology, which addresses the domain that ETSI and ITU-T—as telecommunications-focused bodies—do not cover, and that ISO/IEC's own vertical JTC 1 structure does not natively house.

## 5.5 Consolidated Catalogue of ISO/IEC Quantum-Related Deliverables as of End-2022

Table 5.3 consolidates the ISO/IEC quantum-related deliverables verifiable at the 31 December 2022 cutoff, organized along the normalized attribute set defined in Chapter 2. Items at boundary-of-cutoff status (publication-preparation phase entered by end-2022 but formal publication date in 2023) are recorded conservatively at their highest verifiable stage at the cutoff and flagged ⚠ as required by the methodology.

| Document Identifier | Full Title | Type | Issuing Committee/WG | Taxonomy Domain | Status at End-2022 | Date / Most Recent Milestone | Scope (summary) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ISO/IEC 23837-1 | Information security — Security requirements, test and evaluation methods for quantum key distribution — Part 1: Requirements | IS (in preparation) ⚠ | JTC 1/SC 27/WG 3 | QKD & quantum communication | Publication-preparation phase (entered Oct 2022)[^33] | Oct 2022 SC 27 WG and plenary announcement[^33]; formal publication in 2023[^30] (post-cutoff context) | Security requirements for QKD modules; basis for product design and evaluation |
| ISO/IEC 23837-2 | Information security — Security requirements, test and evaluation methods for quantum key distribution — Part 2: Test and evaluation methods | IS (in preparation) ⚠ | JTC 1/SC 27/WG 3 | QKD & quantum communication | Publication-preparation phase (entered Oct 2022)[^33] | Oct 2022 SC 27 WG and plenary announcement[^33]; formal publication in 2023[^31][^32] (post-cutoff context) | Test and evaluation methods for the security evaluation of QKD modules at the expected evaluation assurance level[^31][^32] |
| ISO/IEC 4879 | Information technology — Quantum computing — Terminology and vocabulary | IS (in development) | JTC 1/WG 14 | Quantum computing | CD-passed (27 Apr 2022)[^27]; post-CD comment resolution continuing as of Sept 2022[^30] | CD ballot 27 April 2022 | 50+ quantum computing terms across basic concepts, hardware, software, and applications[^27] |
| ISO/IEC TR 18157 | Information technology — Quantum computing — Overview | TR (in development) ⚠ | JTC 1/WG 14 | Quantum computing | Under Development (drafting update reported Sept 2022, in-WG comment solicitation planned)[^30] | September 2022 (8th WG 14 meeting update) | Overview of quantum computing for use alongside ISO/IEC 4879 terminology |
