| ISO/IEC PWI 18660 | Information technology — Quantum machine learning datasets | PWI | JTC 1/WG 14 | Quantum computing (applications) | Preliminary Work Item (research purposes and reference framework discussed Sept 2022)[^30] | September 2022 | Reference framework for quantum machine learning datasets |
| ISO/IEC PWI 18670 | Information technology — General requirements for quantum resource simulation platforms | PWI | JTC 1/WG 14 | Quantum computing (platforms) | Preliminary Work Item (project progress and reference framework discussed Sept 2022)[^30] | September 2022 | General requirements for quantum resource simulation platforms |
| ISO/IEC 14888-4 | Information security — Digital signatures with appendix — Part 4: Stateful hash-based mechanisms | IS (under development) ⚠ | JTC 1/SC 27/WG 2 | Post-quantum cryptography | Under Development at end-2022 (published 2024[^25], post-cutoff) | Subsequent publication 2024[^25] | Stateful hash-based signature mechanisms including Merkle-tree-based constructions[^25] |
| ISO/IEC 18033-2 (PQ-KEM track) | Information security — Encryption algorithms — Part 2: Asymmetric ciphers (PQ-KEM standardization) | IS revision track ⚠ | JTC 1/SC 27/WG 2 | Post-quantum cryptography | Standardization project absorbing FrodoKEM, Classic McEliece, ML-KEM[^33] | (stage at cutoff not specified at item-level in primary sources) ⚠ | Standardization channel for post-quantum key encapsulation mechanisms |

Six interpretive observations follow from the catalogue.

First, the **23837 series anchors ISO/IEC's QKD contribution** and stands at the boundary of the report's evidence window: the October 2022 publication-preparation announcement[^33] places the series at the highest pre-publication stage, with formal IS publication subsequently confirmed in 2023[^31][^32][^30]. The post-cutoff publication is recorded here only as retrospective context; the substantive deliverable verifiable at end-2022 is the publication-preparation status of the two parts.

Second, **ISO/IEC 4879 is the most advanced quantum computing standardization deliverable** in the ISO/IEC portfolio at end-2022, having cleared CD ballot on 27 April 2022[^27] and remaining in active post-CD comment resolution through the September 2022 WG 14 meeting[^30]. Its strategic importance—as "the first international standard project in the quantum computing field" providing "an important significance for reaching conceptual consensus in the field of quantum computing"[^27]—is independent of whether its formal IS publication occurs inside or outside the report's evidence window.

Third, **ISO/IEC TR 18157, PWI 18660, and PWI 18670 together extend WG 14's portfolio** beyond the foundational terminology into overview, machine-learning datasets, and simulation platforms[^30], establishing the platform from which a fuller WG 14 quantum computing standardization corpus can grow in the post-cutoff period.

Fourth, the **SC 27 WG 2 PQC items are at earlier stages of crystallization** in the international-standard process than the QKD-evaluation work of WG 3, reflecting the dependence of dedicated PQC algorithm standards on the upstream NIST algorithm finalization (which itself occurred post-cutoff with August 2023 draft FIPS publications[^33]). The 14888-4 stateful hash-based signature track is the most consolidated PQC strand at end-2022, but its publication remains post-cutoff[^25].

Fifth, **no ISO/IEC deliverable in the catalogue addresses QKDN network architecture, quantum networking/quantum internet protocols, standalone quantum random number generation, or quantum sensing and metrology** at item-level normative specification. The first of these gaps is structurally accounted for by the institutional division of labour with ITU-T (Chapter 4); the others reflect the boundaries of ISO/IEC's vertical JTC 1 structure and the horizontal supplementation provided by IEC SEG 14 in the sensing/metrology domain.

Sixth, the catalogue evidences a **clear stage hierarchy** across the ISO/IEC quantum portfolio at end-2022: from publication-preparation (23837-1/-2) through CD-passed (4879) to under-development TR (18157) to PWI (18660, 18670), with the PQC items at WG 2 at various intermediate stages. This stage distribution is consistent with the relative maturity of the underlying technology domains—QKD evaluation methodology, drawing on a decade of preceding ETSI ISG QKD work, being substantively mature enough to converge on a formal international standard; quantum computing terminology being the natural first standardization step in a still-heterogeneous hardware landscape; and quantum machine learning and simulation platforms being at the earliest documentable stage in the ISO/IEC process.

## 5.6 Complementarity with ETSI and ITU-T, and Residual Gaps in ISO/IEC's Coverage

When the per-deliverable catalogue of §5.5 is mapped against the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1 and read against the ETSI catalogue of Chapter 3 and the ITU-T catalogue of Chapter 4, ISO/IEC's quantum standardization profile as of end-2022 displays a structurally distinctive contribution and a coherent complementarity with the two telecommunications-focused SDOs. Table 5.4 summarizes the mapping.

| Taxonomy Domain | ISO/IEC Coverage as of End-2022 | Strength | Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| QKD & quantum communication | ISO/IEC 23837-1, 23837-2 at publication-preparation phase[^33]; formal evaluation methodology at the highest tier of normative authority | Apex of the QKD assurance chain via formal IS-tier evaluation methodology | No network-architecture standardization (structurally allocated to ITU-T); no component- or interface-level specifications (structurally allocated to ETSI) |
| Post-quantum cryptography | SC 27 WG 2 work on ISO/IEC 14888-4 stateful hash-based signatures[^25] and ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM track[^33] | Active algorithmic track with multiple candidate primitives in scope | Dedicated PQC algorithm International Standards await post-cutoff NIST FIPS finalization |
| Quantum computing | ISO/IEC 4879 (CD-passed)[^27]; TR 18157 (Under Development)[^30]; PWI 18660 and PWI 18670 (Preliminary)[^30]; quantum computing roadmap drafting initiated[^30] | First international terminology anchor for quantum computing; portfolio platform established for future expansion | No benchmarking, performance-metric, or hardware-architecture standards (structurally allocated to IEEE) |
| Quantum networking / quantum internet | Not in scope | — | Not covered (left to IRTF QIRG pre-standardization research) |
| Quantum random number generation | Functional coverage via 23837 module evaluation context | Embedded within QKD module evaluation rather than standalone | No dedicated QRNG International Standards |
| Quantum sensing and metrology | IEC SEG 14 horizontal mapping ⚠ | Horizontal scoping rather than vertical specification | No published International Standards |

**Distinctive contribution.** ISO/IEC's structurally distinctive contribution to the global quantum standardization architecture as of end-2022 lies in **two specific functions**.

The first is the supply of the **formal international evaluation methodology** for QKD modules, embodied in the 23837 series. This methodology completes the QKD assurance chain that begins with ETSI's component-and-module specifications (Chapter 3), continues through ETSI's GS QKD 016 Common Criteria Protection Profile (Chapter 3), is overlaid by ITU-T's X.1710-series security framework and X.sec_QKD_profr work (Chapter 4), and culminates in ISO/IEC 23837's formal IS-tier test and evaluation methods. The chain operates at four distinct authority tiers: industry specification (ETSI GS), treaty-context Recommendation (ITU-T), Common Criteria Protection Profile (ETSI GS QKD 016 within the CC certification framework), and formal International Standard (ISO/IEC 23837). The 23837 series at the apex provides the methodologically portable, formally balloted evaluation specification that national certification authorities can adopt as a normative reference, independently of their position vis-à-vis ETSI or ITU-T. The explicit recognition in the QKD industry communications that the 23837 series "is the first systematic international standard regulating QKD security testing technology"[^33] and "provides international authoritative standard guidance for the design and security evaluation of quantum key distribution products"[^33] captures precisely this completing-the-chain function.

The second distinctive contribution is the supply of the **cross-SDO conceptual baseline for quantum computing terminology** through ISO/IEC 4879. As an International Standard developed through national-body ballot under the JTC 1 process, ISO/IEC 4879 provides the terminology anchor that any subsequent quantum computing standardization—within ISO/IEC itself (TR 18157, PWI 18660, PWI 18670), within IEEE's quantum computing working groups (Chapter 6), or within hybrid or future cross-SDO initiatives—can reference as the authoritative cross-organizational vocabulary. The April 2022 CD-passage of 4879[^27] thus represents a structural moment in the global quantum computing standardization architecture: the first time an internationally-balloted vocabulary standard for the domain had cleared the multi-national consensus threshold of the most formal tier of international standardization.

**Complementarity with ETSI and ITU-T.** The institutional partition of labour across the three SDOs is, by end-2022, operationally settled along clean substantive lines and is summarized in Table 5.5.

| Layer of the QKD/QKDN Architecture | ETSI (Chapter 3) | ITU-T (Chapter 4) | ISO/IEC (this chapter) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Components and modules | ISG QKD GS 008/011/012; GR 003/005/007 | — | — |
| Application interfaces | ISG QKD GS 004 (object-style); GS 014 (REST) | (referenced via Y.3802 reference points) | — |
| Network architecture | — | Y.3800-series (Y.3800/3801/3802/3803/3804/3805) | — |
| QoS | — | Y.3806/3807/3811/3812 | — |
| SDN control and orchestration | ISG QKD GS 015 (control); GS 018 (orchestration) | Y.3805 | — |
| Classical-layer interface protocols | — | SG11 Q.QKDN-series | — |
| Security framework | (informative in GR 005) | SG17 X.1710-series; X.sec_QKD_profr | — |
| Common Criteria Protection Profile | ISG QKD GS 016 | — | — |
| Formal international test and evaluation methodology | — | — | SC 27 WG 3 ISO/IEC 23837-1, 23837-2 |

The table makes vivid the structural logic by which the three SDOs assemble a single, layered QKD/QKDN assurance architecture without duplication of effort: ETSI owns the component-and-interface tier, ITU-T owns the network-architecture-and-security-framework tier, and ISO/IEC owns the formal evaluation-methodology tier. The liaison declarations on the SG17 X.sec_QKD_profr work item—"SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD" (Chapter 4)—and on SG11's Q.QKDN_Cq—"ITU-T Q6&Q16/13, ITU-T Q15/17, ETSI ISG-QKD, ISO/IEC JTC1-SC27" (Chapter 4)—are the formal institutional instruments that maintain this layered architecture in real-time coordination.

In the quantum computing domain, ISO/IEC's complementarity is with IEEE rather than with ETSI or ITU-T. ISO/IEC 4879 establishes the terminology anchor; IEEE's quantum computing working groups (addressed in Chapter 6) layer the benchmarking, performance-metrics, and architecture specifications on top of that anchor. The substantive complementarity here is methodologically clean: ISO/IEC provides the formal IS-tier vocabulary that any benchmarking standard must reference for definitional consistency, and IEEE provides the engineering-society-tier benchmarking and architecture standards that the IEEE constituency is best positioned to develop. This division mirrors at the quantum-computing level the ETSI/ITU-T/ISO-IEC division at the QKD level, with each SDO occupying the niche its institutional heritage best supports.

**Residual gaps in ISO/IEC's coverage.** Four gaps in ISO/IEC's quantum coverage at end-2022 are identifiable.

*First*, the **absence of ISO/IEC standardization on QKDN network architecture**. This is properly a structural feature rather than a deficiency: the layered architecture above allocates QKDN network architecture cleanly to ITU-T, and ISO/IEC's contribution at the evaluation-methodology tier presupposes the architectural foundation that ITU-T's Y.3800-series provides. The gap is therefore one of complementarity rather than of unaddressed need, and the institutional logic by which it arises is the same logic that allocates component-and-interface work to ETSI.

*Second*, the **limited treatment of quantum sensing and metrology beyond the IEC SEG 14 mapping function**. Quantum sensing and metrology—encompassing quantum-enhanced magnetometry, gravimetry, imaging, and atomic clock applications—is not addressed within the JTC 1 vertical structure at end-2022. The horizontal supplementation provided by IEC SEG 14 ⚠ partly compensates, but no published International Standards exist in the domain. Given that ETSI and ITU-T—as telecommunications-focused bodies—also do not cover quantum sensing, this gap is the most significant cross-SDO blind spot identified in the report up to this chapter, and it is one of the substantive gaps to which Chapter 9's forward-looking discussion will return.

*Third*, the **pre-cutoff status of dedicated PQC algorithm standards within SC 27 WG 2**. The PQ-KEM track within ISO/IEC 18033-2[^33] and the stateful hash-based signature track within ISO/IEC 14888-4[^25] are both active at end-2022, but the consolidation of dedicated PQC algorithm International Standards depends on the upstream NIST algorithm finalization, which itself materialized only post-cutoff with the August 2023 draft FIPS publications and the subsequent 2024 finalization[^33]. The ISO/IEC PQC track at end-2022 is therefore in an absorption-and-integration phase rather than at a publication-output phase, and its convergence to formal IS-tier deliverables is structurally dependent on the NIST output that lies outside the report's evidence window.

*Fourth*, the **early-stage maturity of WG 14's hardware-and-applications projects**. ISO/IEC 4879 at CD-passed and TR 18157 in active drafting represent the foundational terminology-and-overview portion of the WG 14 portfolio, but the application- and platform-oriented PWI 18660 and PWI 18670[^30] are at the earliest documentable stage in the ISO/IEC process. There are, at end-2022, no WG 14 deliverables at item-level normative specification for quantum computing hardware architectures, performance benchmarks, energy efficiency metrics, or programming model standards. The structural allocation of these areas to IEEE's quantum computing working groups (Chapter 6) partly compensates, but the cross-SDO architecture for quantum computing standardization remains less consolidated at end-2022 than the QKD/QKDN architecture, and this contrast is one of the central observations carried forward into the comparative synthesis of Chapter 8.

**Trajectory at the cutoff.** The trajectory of ISO/IEC's quantum standardization at end-2022 is one of **two completing flagship items at the apex of their respective domains** (the 23837 series for QKD evaluation, ISO/IEC 4879 for quantum computing terminology), **two emerging companion items** (TR 18157, with PQC items in WG 2), and **two preliminary application/platform items** (PWI 18660, PWI 18670). The continuity of leadership—Chinese convenership of WG 14 reaffirmed at the 43rd JTC 1 Plenary[^26]; sustained CNITSEC and QuantumCTek co-leadership of the 23837 project from 2017 through 2022[^33]; the SC 27 proposal solicitation infrastructure operated through the National Information Security Standardization Technical Committee secretariat[^28][^29]—is a structural enabler of the multi-year drafting horizons that the ISO/IEC IS process requires, and positions the portfolio for continued expansion in the post-cutoff period.

With ISO/IEC's catalogue and assessment now established and the layered three-SDO architecture for QKD/QKDN assurance fully reconstructed, the report proceeds in Chapter 6 to IEEE, whose Standards Association working groups occupy a domain—quantum computing benchmarking and architecture—that is substantively orthogonal to the QKD-focused work of ETSI, ITU-T, and ISO/IEC SC 27, and that complements ISO/IEC's foundational terminology anchor with the engineering-society-tier specifications that IEEE's constituency is best positioned to develop.

# 6 IEEE: Standards Association Working Groups and Quantum-Related Projects

The previous three chapters reconstructed the layered QKD/QKDN architecture assembled jointly by ETSI, ITU-T, and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3, with ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 providing the foundational terminology anchor for quantum computing. That layered architecture, however, leaves a substantial portion of the quantum-computing standardization landscape—performance benchmarking, hardware architecture, energy-efficiency metrics, hybrid quantum-classical integration, and algorithm design methodology—addressed only at the conceptual-vocabulary tier by ISO/IEC 4879. The IEEE Standards Association fills precisely this gap. By the end of the 2022 evidence window, IEEE had assembled, predominantly under the sponsorship of the IEEE Computer Society's Standards Activities Board Standards Committee (C/SAB SC), the **most comprehensive engineering-society portfolio of quantum-computing standardization projects of any SDO surveyed in this report**, supplemented by a quantum communication project at the application-layer protocol level (P1913) and a post-quantum cryptography migration project (P3172) that together extend the portfolio into the communication and cryptographic-migration domains.

This chapter catalogues that portfolio. §6.1 reconstructs the institutional architecture under which the projects are developed, including the C/SAB SC's role as the principal sponsoring Standards Committee and the Quantum Groups Linkup (QGL) as the informal lateral coordination forum. §§6.2–6.7 catalogue the projects along thematic strands: terminology and definitions (P7130), performance metrics and benchmarking (P7131), architecture, simulators, and energy efficiency (P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3329), hybrid quantum-classical computing and algorithm design (P3185, P2995), quantum communication and networking (P1913), and post-quantum cryptography migration (P3172). §6.8 consolidates the catalogue into the normalized attribute set defined in Chapter 2. §6.9 assesses IEEE's centre of gravity, complementarity with the other surveyed SDOs, and residual gaps that motivate the cross-organizational synthesis of Chapter 8.

## 6.1 Institutional Architecture: IEEE SA, the Computer Society SAB SC, and the Quantum Groups Linkup

IEEE's quantum standardization work is developed within the institutional scaffolding of the IEEE Standards Association codified in the SA Standards Board Bylaws and discussed in Chapter 2. The procedural sequence—PAR submission to and approval by the New Standards Committee (NesCom), working-group drafting and SA balloting under the 75 % affirmative threshold, Standards Review Committee (RevCom) examination, and final approval by the SA Standards Board—channels each project from initiation to publication. For the majority of IEEE's quantum projects, the sponsoring Standards Committee is the **IEEE Computer Society's Standards Activities Board Standards Committee (C/SAB SC)**, which under its own scope and procedures is responsible for reviewing PARs originating in quantum-domain working groups, forwarding approved PAR documentation to NesCom, exercising working-group oversight, authorising SA ballots, and interfacing with RevCom on completed drafts. The 2022 organizational roster records the C/SAB SC chair as Edward Au, vice chair as Jon Rosdahl, with members drawn from Siemens EDA, Huawei, and ex-officio participants from Eduworks and self-affiliated members, supported by IEEE SA programme managers Jonathan Goldberg and Soo Kim.[^34]

The procedural cadence by which PARs progress through the C/SAB SC is illustrated by the 26 July 2022 C/SAB SC meeting, which placed two new PARs of direct relevance to this chapter on the agenda for the September 2022 NesCom meeting: **P3185 (Standard for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing)** and a separate non-quantum item P3188 (Standard for Virtual E-card Platform).[^34] At the same meeting, two extension requests for existing quantum-domain PARs were tabled: **P7131 (Standard for Quantum Computing Performance Metrics & Performance Benchmarking)** and the unrelated P2418.3 in the Ledger Technology in Agriculture series.[^34] The status snapshot of 20 July 2022 recorded that the C/SAB SC then hosted "27 Working Groups (21 with active projects)" and "36 active PARs (30 new PARs; 5 revision PARs)" with "14 published standards", a figure that places the quantum-domain working groups catalogued in §§6.2–6.7 within a substantially larger committee portfolio.[^34] Earlier in 2022, the May 24 C/SAB SC meeting had received status presentations from the Human factors for the Quality Assessment of Light Field Imaging, Human Factors for Visual Experiences, PowerKG, and **Quantum Computing Nomenclature** working groups, and the June 21 meeting from the **Quantum Computing Benchmarking** working group and Ledger Technology in Agriculture working group, evidencing the regular review cadence under which the quantum projects operated through the year.[^34]

The lateral coordination across the quantum projects—formally distributed across multiple working groups and PAR families with distinct chairs, scopes, and drafting timetables—is provided not by a Subcommittee structure analogous to ITU-T's Study Group Question allocation or ISO/IEC's SC/WG partitioning, but by the informal **Quantum Groups Linkup (QGL)**. As described on the P7131 working group's own pages, "Quantum Groups Linkup (QGL) is an informal group of IEEE-SA working groups in the 'quantum' topic area, including chairs, members, and IEEE staff. The group discusses cross-cutting technical issues and IEEE-SA issues of common interest. The group is informal and there are no instructions on how to join except to talk to somebody already in the group".[^34] This informality is itself an institutional design choice: unlike ITU-T's liaison declarations or ISO/IEC's NWIP balloting infrastructure, IEEE's lateral coordination on quantum work operates through a self-organizing community of practice that the working-group chairs and IEEE SA staff sustain across the formally separate projects.

The QGL maintains a publicly visible registry of the quantum-domain working groups whose chairs participate, providing the most consolidated single inventory of IEEE's quantum projects as of the QGL roster. The registry, reproduced in Table 6.1 below in the form documented at the P7131 working-group pages, lists ten projects spanning quantum communication, post-quantum network security (forward-looking), algorithm design, computing architecture, programmable quantum simulators, post-quantum cryptography migration, hybrid quantum-classical computing, energy efficiency, terminology, and performance benchmarking.[^34]

| Project | Title | Chair |
|---|---|---|
| P1913 | Software-Defined Quantum Communication | Stephen Bush |
| P1943 | Standard for Post-Quantum Network Security | Benjamin Smith |
| P2995 | Trial-Use Standard for a Quantum Algorithm Design and Development | Lisa Loud |
| P3120 | Standard for Quantum Computing Architecture | Blaise Vignon |
| P3120.1 | Standard for Programmable Quantum Simulator | Catherine Lefebvre |
| P3155 | Standard for Programmable Quantum Simulator | Catherine Lefebvre |
| P3172 | Recommended Practice for Post-Quantum Cryptography Migration | Benjamin Smith |
| P3185 | Standard for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing | Gilles Ceyssat |
| P3329 | Standard for Quantum Computing and Simulation Energy Efficiency | Alexia Auffèves |
| P7130 | Standard for Quantum Technologies Definitions | Andrew Vance |
| P7131 | Standard for Quantum Computing Performance Metrics & Performance Benchmarking | Erik DeBenedictis |

The institutional positioning of IEEE's quantum work within an **engineering-society constituency** is the central feature that differentiates it from the SDOs already surveyed. ETSI's ISG QKD operates within a European telecommunications standardization framework whose constituency comprises network operators, equipment vendors, and quantum-specialist firms; ITU-T's Study Groups operate within a UN treaty-context whose constituency is national administrations and recognized operating agencies; ISO/IEC SC 27 and JTC 1/WG 14 operate through national-body delegation under the most formal tier of international standardization. IEEE's constituency, by contrast, comprises individual professionals and corporate participants drawn from across the engineering and computer-science community, and its document typology—Standard, Recommended Practice, Guide, and Trial-Use Document, as catalogued under the SA Standards Board Bylaws and recounted in Chapter 2—reflects this engineering-society orientation. This positioning, combined with the QGL's self-organizing coordination, is what enables IEEE to sustain a quantum-computing portfolio whose breadth across terminology, benchmarking, architecture, energy efficiency, hybrid integration, and algorithm design exceeds that of any other single SDO at end-2022.

Two further institutional features warrant brief note. First, the PAR is "the formal instrument by which a standards project is admitted into the IEEE process" (Chapter 2); its four-year lifetime imposes a definite procedural horizon within which working-group drafting must converge to SA ballot or seek extension. The July 2022 PAR extension request for P7131 (§6.3 below) is a direct manifestation of this four-year horizon. Second, an Active PAR for which working-group drafting is ongoing and SA Standards Board approval has not yet been obtained is, in the uniform status labelling of Chapter 2, recorded as **Active PAR (Under Development)**, distinguished from an **Active (Approved Standard)** that has completed Board approval. At end-2022, the IEEE quantum portfolio comprised entirely projects in the former state; the catalogue in §§6.2–6.7 records each project's PAR status accordingly.

## 6.2 Terminology and Definitions: P7130 Quantum Technologies Definitions Working Group

The terminology-and-definitions strand of IEEE's quantum portfolio is anchored by **P7130 (Standard for Quantum Technologies Definitions)**, developed by the Quantum Computing Nomenclature Working Group under the C/SAB SC. The PAR scope, as recorded in the QGL registry, is that "this standard addresses quantum technologies specific terminology and establishes definitions necessary to facilitate clarity of understanding to enable compatibility and interoperability".[^34] The working group is chaired by Andrew Vance.[^34]

The drafting history of P7130 is unusually well documented through the C/SAB SC's status presentation cycle. The Quantum Computing Nomenclature Working Group was scheduled to present its status to the C/SAB SC at its 24 May 2022 meeting, alongside three other working groups in the broader committee portfolio.[^34] At end-2022 the project's status was that of an **Active PAR (Under Development)**: substantive drafting had advanced to the point that "an initial draft of over 60 pages was developed", but the working group had not yet brought the draft to SA ballot.[^29] The drafting difficulties that the working group had encountered—characterized in later C/SAB SC documentation as the working group "struggling for a couple of years" despite the 60-page draft—foreshadowed the way-forward deliberations subsequently considered by the C/SAB SC. Although those deliberations themselves crystallized in post-cutoff meetings (the 6 May 2025 and 22 July 2025 C/SAB SC sessions tabled three options for P7130: "work on the P7130 Draft and proceed to SA Ballot", "Withdraw the PAR", or "Major revision/addition of definitions"),[^29][^35] the underlying drafting challenges that motivated those options were already visible in the working group's end-2022 status.

The structural relationship between IEEE P7130 and the ISO/IEC 4879 terminology standard catalogued in Chapter 5 is one of **engineering-society-tier specification developed in parallel with the formal international-standard terminology anchor**. ISO/IEC 4879, having cleared its CD ballot on 27 April 2022, supplies the multilateral-ballot vocabulary baseline whose more-than-50 quantum computing terms are structured along basic concepts, hardware, software, and applications axes. IEEE P7130, by virtue of its institutional remit, can in principle reference the ISO/IEC vocabulary as a normative or informative reference while extending it with the engineering-detail definitions that IEEE's constituency requires. The scope statement's invocation of "compatibility and interoperability" objectives is consistent with this complementary positioning: P7130 is designed to enable cross-standard reference within the IEEE quantum portfolio (P7131 benchmarking, P3120 architecture, P3329 energy efficiency, and others) rather than to compete with the ISO/IEC terminology baseline. The practical drafting challenges that have constrained P7130's progression—producing a 60-page draft without yet converging to SA ballot—reflect, at the IEEE level, the genuine difficulty of stabilising terminology in a field where hardware platforms (superconducting, trapped-ion, photonic, neutral-atom, topological) remain heterogeneous and pre-commercial, the same difficulty that ISO/IEC WG 14 has addressed through its post-CD comment resolution cycle.

## 6.3 Performance Metrics and Benchmarking: P7131 Quantum Computing Benchmarking Working Group

The performance-metrics-and-benchmarking strand is anchored by **P7131 (Standard for Quantum Computing Performance Metrics & Performance Benchmarking)**, developed by the Quantum Computing Benchmark Working Group (also referred to in C/SAB SC documentation as the Quantum Computing Benchmarking Working Group). The PAR scope, as recorded by both the QGL registry and the StandICT.eu landscape catalogue, is that "the standard covers quantum computing performance metrics for standardizing performance benchmarking of quantum computing hardware and software. These metrics and performance tests include everything necessary to benchmark quantum computers (stand alone and by/for comparison) and to benchmark quantum computers against classical computers using a methodology that accounts for factors such as dedicated solvers".[^35][^34] The working group is chaired by Erik DeBenedictis.[^34]

The end-2022 status of P7131 is that of an **Active PAR (Under Development)** with an active extension request. The 26 July 2022 C/SAB SC meeting documented that "the following PAR extension requests will be considered in the next Nescom meeting on September 20, 2022: – Ledger Technology in Agriculture P2418.3 (Standard for the Framework of Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT) Use in Agriculture) – Quantum Computing Benchmark Working Group P7131 (Standard for Quantum Computing Performance Metrics & Performance Benchmarking)".[^34] The extension request itself reflects the four-year PAR lifetime convention noted in §6.1: P7131's PAR period was approaching its lifecycle threshold, and the working group sought extension to continue substantive drafting toward SA ballot. The Quantum Computing Benchmarking Working Group had presented its status to the C/SAB SC at the 21 June 2022 meeting, immediately before the July tabling of the extension request, illustrating the standard procedural sequence by which a working group's substantive progress is reviewed before the committee endorses an extension submission to NesCom.[^34]

P7131 occupies a **structurally distinctive position** within the cross-SDO quantum-computing standardization architecture. The scope's explicit emphasis on benchmarking "everything necessary to benchmark quantum computers (stand alone and by/for comparison) and to benchmark quantum computers against classical computers using a methodology that accounts for factors such as dedicated solvers"[^35] positions the project as the principal benchmarking-tier specification that is layered atop the terminology-tier baseline supplied by ISO/IEC 4879 (Chapter 5) and IEEE P7130 (§6.2). The methodological subtlety captured in the "dedicated solvers" clause—reflecting the recognition that quantum-versus-classical benchmarking must account for the asymmetry between specialised classical solvers tuned to particular problem instances and general-purpose quantum approaches—signals that the working group's substantive scope extends beyond simple gate-count or qubit-count metrics into the more sophisticated comparative methodology that operational benchmarking demands.

The three-tier cross-SDO architecture for quantum-computing standardization that emerges from §§6.2–6.3 is summarized as: ISO/IEC 4879 at the formal-IS terminology baseline, IEEE P7130 at the engineering-society terminology and definitions tier, and IEEE P7131 at the engineering-society benchmarking-methodology tier. No equivalent benchmarking project exists at ETSI, ITU-T, or ISO/IEC at end-2022; the structural allocation of the benchmarking function to IEEE—consistent with the engineering-society constituency that is best positioned to develop comparative performance methodology—is one of the cleanest examples in the report's evidence window of inter-SDO division of labour by institutional comparative advantage.

## 6.4 Architecture, Simulators, and Energy Efficiency: P3120 Series, P3155, and P3329

The architecture- and platform-oriented strand of IEEE's quantum-computing portfolio comprises four PARs that together address technical architecture, programmable quantum simulators, and energy efficiency. Their scopes, as recorded in the QGL registry, articulate a coherent hardware-platform stack.

**P3120 (Standard for Quantum Computing Architecture)**, chaired by Blaise Vignon, has scope: "This standard defines technical architectures for a quantum computers based on the technological type (e.g., fault-tolerant universal quantum computing) and one or more qubit modalities (e.g., superconducting quantum processor). The defined architectures include the hardware (e.g., signal generator) and low-level software (e.g., quantum error correction) components of a quantum computer. The standard excludes any definition of a quantum circuit or algorithm".[^34] The scope's explicit accommodation of multiple qubit modalities (superconducting being given as an illustrative example) and multiple technological types (fault-tolerant universal quantum computing being given as one option) reflects a deliberate architectural pluralism—the standard is to provide an architecture framework rather than commit to a single hardware platform—and the explicit exclusion of "quantum circuit or algorithm" definitions anchors the project firmly at the hardware-and-low-level-software tier, leaving the algorithm-design layer to the parallel P2995 project (§6.5).

**P3120.1 (Standard for Programmable Quantum Simulator)**, chaired by Catherine Lefebvre, extends the P3120 architecture work into the simulator domain: "This standard defines technical architectures for quantum simulators based on technological type (e.g., analog quantum simulator) and one or more quantum objects (e.g., neutral atoms) or quantum systems (e.g., arrays of optically trapped atoms). The standard further defines the hardware and software components (e.g., quantum operating device) of quantum simulators, and excludes any mathematical construct that can be used to describe the time evolution of a quantum system of interest known as Hamiltonian".[^34] The dotted-numbering convention (P3120.1 sub-numbered relative to P3120) signals the architectural relationship: the simulator standard is conceived as an extension of the general computing architecture rather than as an unrelated parallel project, and the exclusion of the Hamiltonian-construct definition mirrors the exclusion of algorithm definitions in P3120 itself, preserving the hardware-tier scope discipline.

**P3155 (Standard for Programmable Quantum Simulator)**, also chaired by Catherine Lefebvre, complements P3120.1 at the programming-methods tier: "This standard defines programming methods of quantum simulators according to analog (i.e., target Hamiltonian), digital (i.e., non-native Hamiltonian evolution) and hybrid (i.e., quantum-quantum or quantum-classical architectures) devices for the simulation of quantum phenomena beyond classical computing applications. This standard includes algorithms to represent the nano-scale properties of quantum simulation devices, and excludes any proposal for the architecture of quantum simulators".[^34] The careful demarcation between P3120.1 (architecture inclusive, programming exclusive) and P3155 (programming inclusive, architecture exclusive) creates a clean two-document partition of the programmable-simulator domain that allows each to be developed by the same working group leadership without overlap. The recognition of analog, digital, and hybrid simulator categories within P3155's scope—each characterized by its relationship to a target Hamiltonian—signals the substantive depth of the working group's engagement with the diversity of contemporary simulator hardware.

**P3329 (Standard for Quantum Computing and Simulation Energy Efficiency)**, chaired by Alexia Auffèves, addresses the energy-efficiency tier across the entire computing-and-simulation portfolio: "This standard defines energy efficiency metrics for quantum computing (gate-based, quantum annealing, quantum simulation). It compares the performance of the computation to its energy consumption. The performance is defined at the quantum level and at the end user level. The definition applies to all Quantum Bit (qubit) technologies, including the classical and quantum control chains, to various quantum processors, both Noisy Intermediate Scale Quantum (NISQ)-era and fault-tolerant, as well as to quantum annealers and simulators".[^34] The deliberately broad scope—covering gate-based quantum computing, quantum annealing, and quantum simulation; applying to all qubit technologies including the full classical and quantum control chains; spanning NISQ-era and fault-tolerant processors—positions P3329 as a horizontal companion to the architecture-and-platform vertical stack of P3120, P3120.1, and P3155. The dual-tier performance metric defined "at the quantum level and at the end user level" reflects the methodological sophistication that energy-efficiency benchmarking demands: a metric usable for both hardware-level engineering optimisation and end-user procurement decision-making.

At end-2022, each of P3120, P3120.1, P3155, and P3329 is recorded as an **Active PAR (Under Development)** under the C/SAB SC and within the QGL community of practice. ⚠ The QGL registry as reproduced in Table 6.1 records P3120.1 with the notation "link n/a" and P3185 and P3329 with "link not set up", indicating that working-group infrastructure (dedicated web pages, mailing lists) for some of these projects was at an earlier stage of formalization than for projects such as P7131 with established working-group web presence; the underlying PAR status as Active PAR (Under Development) at end-2022 is, however, supported by the QGL registry's enumeration of the project as part of the working IEEE quantum portfolio.[^34]

The four projects together assemble a **coherent architectural stack at the hardware/platform tier of quantum-computing standardization**: P3120 specifies the general quantum-computer architecture, P3120.1 specifies the quantum-simulator architecture as an extension of that frame, P3155 specifies the programming methods that operate on the simulator architecture, and P3329 supplies the energy-efficiency metrics applicable across the entire computing-and-simulation portfolio. The relationship to P7130's terminology work (§6.2) and P7131's benchmarking work (§6.3) is one of vertical complementarity: the terminology layer establishes shared vocabulary, the benchmarking layer establishes comparative performance methodology, and the architecture-and-platform layer establishes the hardware and platform specifications that the benchmarks measure and the energy metrics evaluate. No other SDO surveyed in this report has assembled a comparably integrated stack at the quantum-computing hardware tier as of end-2022.

## 6.5 Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing and Quantum Algorithm Design: P3185 and P2995

Two further projects extend IEEE's quantum-computing portfolio beyond the hardware/platform tier into system-integration and algorithm-design layers.

**P3185 (Standard for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing)**, chaired by Gilles Ceyssat, addresses the integration tier: "This standard defines the hardware and software architecture of hybrid quantum-classical computing environments. It specifies the interconnection between one or more quantum processor units (QPUs) and one or more central processing units (CPUs) and/or graphics processing units (GPUs) and/or tensor processing units (TPUs). This standard includes the definition of application programming interfaces (APIs) for optimal high-performance computing (HPC) and excludes any definition of classical (super) computers and quantum computers".[^34] The scope's explicit enumeration of integration targets (CPUs, GPUs, TPUs) and the inclusion of APIs "for optimal high-performance computing (HPC)" positions the project as the HPC-integration specification through which quantum processors enter the broader HPC ecosystem, with the explicit exclusion of "classical (super) computers and quantum computers" themselves preserving the integration-architecture scope discipline that the P3120 family establishes at the pure-quantum tier.

The PAR was formally placed on the NesCom agenda at the September 2022 meeting: the 26 July 2022 C/SAB SC documentation records that "the following new PARs will be considered in the next Nescom meeting on September 20, 2022: – P3185 (Standard for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing) – P3188 (Standard for Virtual E-card Platform)".[^34] At end-2022, therefore, P3185 is an **Active PAR (Under Development)** following its September 2022 NesCom-stage approval, with working-group drafting under the chairmanship of Gilles Ceyssat as recorded in the QGL registry.[^34]

The post-cutoff fate of P3185 is worth noting briefly for retrospective context. The C/SAB SC's 6 May 2025 meeting recorded that "Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing Working Group reach a consensus on 8 April 2025 to withdraw P3185 PAR (Standard for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing)",[^35] and the 22 July 2025 meeting formally approved the withdrawal under Motion 2025-005: "Approve to withdraw P3185 PAR (Standard for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing) with the following explanation for withdrawal 'The Working Group reached a consensus to withdraw the PAR and terminate the working group' and disband the Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing Working Group".[^29] These developments are explicitly outside the report's end-2022 evidence window and are recorded here only as post-cutoff retrospective context that does not bear on the end-2022 status of P3185 as an Active PAR (Under Development).

**P2995 (Trial-Use Standard for a Quantum Algorithm Design and Development)**, chaired by Lisa Loud, addresses the algorithm-design tier: "This trial-use standard defines a standardized method for the design of quantum algorithms. The defined methods apply to any type of algorithm that can be assimilated into quantum primitives and/or quantum applications. The design of the algorithms is done preceding quantum programming".[^34] The project is significant in two respects relevant to the report's normalized typology. First, its designation as a **Trial-Use Document**—one of the four IEEE document types codified in the SA Standards Board Bylaws and discussed in Chapter 2, with an effective period not exceeding three years—reflects the deliberate selection of an instrument suitable for emerging-domain standardization where the substantive content benefits from a defined trial period before transitioning to either a full Standard or expiry. Second, its scope's articulation of algorithm design as preceding quantum programming positions P2995 at the methodology tier that is upstream of both the architecture work (P3120 family) and the simulator-programming work (P3155): the algorithm is designed first in a hardware- and platform-agnostic manner, with subsequent assimilation "into quantum primitives and/or quantum applications" mapping the design onto the platform specifics.

Together, P3185 and P2995 close two important conceptual gaps in the IEEE quantum-computing portfolio. P3185 supplies the bridge between the quantum-computing standardization developed across §§6.2–6.4 and the broader high-performance computing ecosystem in which quantum processors will be deployed as accelerators alongside CPUs, GPUs, and TPUs; P2995 supplies the upstream algorithm-design methodology that the architecture and simulator work presupposes but does not itself specify. The portfolio, when these two projects are added to the terminology, benchmarking, and architecture-and-platform tiers already catalogued, exhibits a discernible end-to-end vertical structure running from algorithm design through hybrid integration to terminology baseline.

## 6.6 Quantum Communication and Networking: P1913 and Emerging Network Security Projects

IEEE's contribution to the quantum communication and networking domain at end-2022 is anchored by a single project, **P1913 (Software-Defined Quantum Communication)**, chaired by Stephen Bush. The scope, as recorded in the QGL registry, is: "This standard defines the Software-Defined Quantum Communication (SDQC) protocol that enables configuration of quantum endpoints in a communication network in order to dynamically create, modify, or remove quantum protocols or applications. This protocol resides at the application layer and communicates over Transmission Control Protocol/Internet Protocol. The protocol design facilitates future integration with Software-Defined Networking and Open Networking Foundation OpenFlow. The standard defines a set of quantum device configuration commands that control the transmission, reception, and operation of quantum states. These device commands contain parameters that describe quantum state preparation, measurement, and readout".[^34]

Three substantive features of the P1913 scope warrant analytical attention.

First, **the protocol's positioning at the application layer over TCP/IP** distinguishes it sharply from the QKDN standardization corpus catalogued in Chapters 3 and 4. Where ETSI's ISG QKD GS QKD 014 specifies a REST/HTTPS/JSON key delivery API between Secure Application Entities and Key Management Entities (Chapter 3), and ITU-T's Q.QKDN-series specifies per-reference-point classical-layer interface protocols (Chapter 4), P1913 specifies a higher-layer protocol that *configures* quantum endpoints to "dynamically create, modify, or remove quantum protocols or applications". The relationship is one of layered complementarity rather than overlap: P1913 operates as a configuration and management protocol over a substrate that may include QKD modules specified to ETSI standards and QKDN networks architected to ITU-T standards.

Second, **the explicit design intent to facilitate "future integration with Software-Defined Networking and Open Networking Foundation OpenFlow"**[^34] aligns P1913 with the SDN paradigm that ETSI ISG QKD has embraced through GS QKD 015 and GS QKD 018 (Chapter 3) and that ITU-T SG13 has embraced through Y.3805 (Chapter 4). The convergence around SDN-based control of quantum communication resources, visible simultaneously across ETSI, ITU-T, and IEEE by end-2022, is one of the cleanest examples of cross-SDO conceptual alignment in the report's evidence window—even where each SDO addresses the SDN paradigm from its own institutional position.

Third, **the explicit specification of "quantum device configuration commands that control the transmission, reception, and operation of quantum states" with parameters describing "quantum state preparation, measurement, and readout"** indicates that P1913's substantive scope extends to the engineering-level configuration of quantum devices, not merely the network-layer abstraction of QKD service delivery. This positioning gives P1913 a distinctive niche within the cross-SDO quantum-communication architecture: it is the application-layer protocol that exposes engineer-relevant configuration of the underlying quantum communication endpoints. At end-2022, P1913 is recorded as an **Active PAR (Under Development)** under the QGL registry.[^34]

A second project listed in the QGL registry, **P1943 (Standard for Post-Quantum Network Security)** chaired by Benjamin Smith, has scope: "This standard defines a post-quantum optimized version of network security protocols. It is based on a multi-layer protocols approach and allows data packets to be quantum resistant to future cryptographically relevant quantum computers (CRQCs). This standard includes hybrid modes for key exchange and authentication and specifies mechanisms for handling the larger public key sizes of post-quantum algorithms. This standard excludes any definition of a new post-quantum cryptography (PQC) protocol".[^34] ⚠ The substantive crystallization of P1943's PAR within the IEEE process and its formal end-2022 status cannot be unambiguously confirmed from the primary sources consulted in the manner that the other QGL-listed projects can be confirmed through C/SAB SC meeting records; the project's listing in the QGL registry establishes its existence as part of the IEEE quantum portfolio at the time the registry was compiled, and its substantive content—addressing post-quantum network security at the network-protocol layer—provides forward-looking context for IEEE's expanding remit at the quantum-communication and post-quantum-protocol intersection. The project is therefore recorded here for completeness of the QGL registry and as evidence of the directional expansion of IEEE's portfolio, with a corresponding ⚠ flag on the precise end-2022 PAR-status assertion.

## 6.7 Post-Quantum Cryptography Migration: P3172 Recommended Practice

The final project in IEEE's quantum-related portfolio surveyed in this chapter is **P3172 (Recommended Practice for Post-Quantum Cryptography Migration)**, chaired by Benjamin Smith. The scope, as recorded in the QGL registry, is: "This recommended practice describes multi-step processes that can be used to implement hybrid mechanisms (combinations of classical quantum-vulnerable and quantum-resistant public-key algorithms). Existing post-quantum cryptography (PQC) systems are described. Desired characteristics of the hybrid mechanisms, such as crypto agility are also described".[^34]

Two structural features of P3172 warrant analytical attention.

First, **the designation as a Recommended Practice rather than a Standard** within the IEEE SA document typology codified in the SA Standards Board Bylaws and discussed in Chapter 2 reflects a deliberate choice of instrument. Where a Standard carries fully normative requirements, a Recommended Practice "gives recommended procedures" without imposing normative compliance. For migration guidance—where the specific migration trajectory is necessarily context-dependent on the deployer's existing cryptographic infrastructure, operational constraints, and risk tolerance—the Recommended Practice instrument is methodologically appropriate, supplying procedural guidance and characterizing desired properties (such as "crypto agility") rather than imposing a uniform migration mandate. This choice mirrors at the IEEE level the analogous design choice made by ETSI in publishing the hybrid-key-exchange specification as TS 103 744 (Chapter 3) rather than as a full European Standard.

Second, **the substantive scope's emphasis on "hybrid mechanisms (combinations of classical quantum-vulnerable and quantum-resistant public-key algorithms)"**[^34] places P3172 squarely within the same hybrid-cryptography paradigm that ETSI's TS 103 744 and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2's PQ-KEM track at ISO/IEC 18033-2 also occupy. The cross-SDO alignment around hybrid mechanisms—classical and post-quantum primitives combined to provide layered security during the migration period—is one of the most coherent multi-SDO consensus patterns in the report's evidence window, and IEEE's contribution through P3172 is the engineering-society migration-guidance complement to ETSI's protocol-specification work and ISO/IEC's algorithm-standardization work. The scope's additional explicit emphasis on "crypto agility" as a desired hybrid-mechanism characteristic signals the working group's recognition that any migration recommendation must accommodate the evolving algorithmic landscape that the post-cutoff NIST FIPS finalizations would subsequently confirm.

At end-2022, P3172 is recorded as an **Active PAR (Under Development)** under the QGL registry, chaired by Benjamin Smith, who also chairs the related P1943 network-security project, evidencing the coordination of IEEE's post-quantum cryptography work under a coherent chair-level leadership.[^34]

## 6.8 Consolidated Catalogue of IEEE Quantum-Related PARs and Standards as of End-2022

Table 6.2 consolidates the per-project entries developed in §§6.2–6.7 into the normalized attribute set defined in Chapter 2. Each entry records the document identifier, full title (verbatim from the QGL registry), IEEE document type per the SA Standards Board Bylaws typology, issuing working group and its C/SAB SC sponsorship, the taxonomy domain to which the project is assigned per Chapter 1, the status as of 31 December 2022, the chair, and a scope summary. Items whose precise stage cannot be unambiguously verified from the primary sources consulted are flagged ⚠.

| Document Identifier | Full Title | Type | Issuing Working Group (Sponsoring Committee: C/SAB SC) | Taxonomy Domain | Status at End-2022 | Chair | Scope (summary) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P7130 | Standard for Quantum Technologies Definitions | Standard | Quantum Computing Nomenclature WG | Quantum computing (terminology) | Active PAR (Under Development); 60+ page draft developed | Andrew Vance | Quantum technologies specific terminology and definitions to facilitate clarity, compatibility, and interoperability[^34] |
| P7131 | Standard for Quantum Computing Performance Metrics & Performance Benchmarking | Standard | Quantum Computing Benchmark WG | Quantum computing (benchmarking) | Active PAR (Under Development); PAR extension request tabled at C/SAB SC 26 July 2022 for September 2022 NesCom consideration | Erik DeBenedictis | Performance metrics and benchmarking methodology covering quantum vs. quantum and quantum vs. classical comparisons, accounting for dedicated solvers[^35][^34] |
| P3120 | Standard for Quantum Computing Architecture | Standard | Quantum Computing Architecture WG | Quantum computing (architecture) | Active PAR (Under Development) | Blaise Vignon | Technical architectures of quantum computers across technological types and qubit modalities; includes hardware and low-level software; excludes circuits and algorithms[^34] |
| P3120.1 | Standard for Programmable Quantum Simulator (Architecture) | Standard | Programmable Quantum Simulator WG (dotted-sub of P3120) | Quantum computing (architecture/simulator) | Active PAR (Under Development) ⚠ (registry records "link n/a") | Catherine Lefebvre | Technical architectures of quantum simulators by technological type and quantum objects/systems; hardware and software components; excludes Hamiltonian definitions[^34] |
| P3155 | Standard for Programmable Quantum Simulator (Programming) | Standard | Programmable Quantum Simulator WG | Quantum computing (simulator programming) | Active PAR (Under Development) | Catherine Lefebvre | Programming methods of quantum simulators—analog, digital, hybrid—for quantum-phenomena simulation; excludes simulator architecture proposals[^34] |
| P3329 | Standard for Quantum Computing and Simulation Energy Efficiency | Standard | Quantum Computing & Simulation Energy Efficiency WG | Quantum computing (energy efficiency) | Active PAR (Under Development) ⚠ (registry records "link not set up") | Alexia Auffèves | Energy efficiency metrics across gate-based, annealing, and simulation modalities; applies to all qubit technologies, NISQ and fault-tolerant; quantum-level and end-user-level performance[^34] |
| P3185 | Standard for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing | Standard | Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing WG | Quantum computing (HPC integration) | Active PAR (Under Development); placed on NesCom agenda for September 2022 meeting at C/SAB SC 26 July 2022[^34] | Gilles Ceyssat | Hardware and software architecture of hybrid quantum-classical environments; QPU-to-CPU/GPU/TPU interconnection; APIs for HPC integration[^34] |
| P2995 | Trial-Use Standard for a Quantum Algorithm Design and Development | Trial-Use Document | Quantum Algorithm Design and Development WG | Quantum computing (algorithm design) | Active PAR (Under Development) | Lisa Loud | Standardized method for quantum algorithm design preceding quantum programming, applicable to algorithms assimilable into quantum primitives and/or applications[^34] |
| P1913 | Software-Defined Quantum Communication | Standard | Software-Defined Quantum Communication WG | QKD and quantum communication / quantum networking (application layer) | Active PAR (Under Development) | Stephen Bush | SDQC protocol at application layer over TCP/IP; configures quantum endpoints to create/modify/remove quantum protocols or applications; designed for SDN/OpenFlow integration[^34] |
| P3172 | Recommended Practice for Post-Quantum Cryptography Migration | Recommended Practice | PQC Migration WG | Post-quantum cryptography | Active PAR (Under Development) | Benjamin Smith | Multi-step migration processes for hybrid mechanisms combining classical and quantum-resistant public-key algorithms; describes existing PQC systems and crypto-agility characteristics[^34] |
| P1943 | Standard for Post-Quantum Network Security | Standard | Post-Quantum Network Security WG | Post-quantum cryptography (network protocols) | Listed in QGL registry; precise end-2022 PAR-stage status ⚠ | Benjamin Smith | Post-quantum optimized network security protocols with multi-layer approach; hybrid modes for key exchange and authentication; handles large PQC public keys; excludes new PQC protocol definitions[^34] |

Three interpretive observations follow from the consolidated catalogue.

First, the **document-type distribution** across the portfolio reveals a deliberate selection of instruments suited to substantive scope. Eight of the eleven catalogued projects are designated as Standards—the fully normative instrument under the SA Standards Board Bylaws—covering terminology, benchmarking, architecture, simulators, energy efficiency, hybrid integration, and quantum communication. P2995 is designated as a Trial-Use Document, recognising the emerging nature of quantum algorithm design methodology and the appropriateness of a defined trial period before transition to a Standard or expiry. P3172 is designated as a Recommended Practice, reflecting the necessarily context-dependent character of migration guidance. The document-type distribution itself signals the IEEE working groups' methodological maturity in matching instrument to substantive scope.

Second, the **taxonomy-domain distribution** is heavily skewed to quantum computing. Eight of the eleven projects (P7130, P7131, P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3329, P3185, P2995) are classified under quantum computing in the Chapter 1 taxonomy, with sub-axes covering terminology, benchmarking, architecture, simulator architecture, simulator programming, energy efficiency, HPC integration, and algorithm design. One project (P1913) is classified under QKD and quantum communication, with an emerging adjacency to quantum networking via its application-layer SDN-integration design intent. Two projects (P3172, P1943) are classified under post-quantum cryptography. No projects in the IEEE portfolio at end-2022 address quantum sensing, quantum metrology, or standalone quantum random number generation.

Third, the **PAR-status distribution** at end-2022 shows the portfolio uniformly at the **Active PAR (Under Development)** stage—no item in the catalogue has completed SA Standards Board approval and emerged as an Active (Approved Standard) within the report's evidence window. This is consistent with the overall maturity profile of quantum-computing standardization noted across earlier chapters: the field is in a project-definition and early-drafting phase, with foundational deliverables not yet converging to publication in any of the surveyed SDOs at the cutoff. The September 2022 NesCom-stage approval of P3185 and the September 2022 NesCom-stage extension of P7131 are the most concrete procedural milestones recorded for the portfolio within 2022;[^34] the substantive convergence of any IEEE quantum-domain PAR to an approved standard remained a post-cutoff prospect.

## 6.9 Assessment of IEEE's Quantum Standardization Profile: Centre of Gravity, Complementarity, and Residual Gaps

When the per-project catalogue of §6.8 is mapped against the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1 and read against the ETSI, ITU-T, and ISO/IEC catalogues of Chapters 3–5, IEEE's quantum standardization profile as of end-2022 displays a distinctive centre of gravity, a coherent set of complementarities with the other surveyed SDOs, and a clearly identifiable set of residual gaps. Table 6.3 summarizes the mapping.

| Taxonomy Domain | IEEE Coverage as of End-2022 | Strength | Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| Quantum computing | Vertically integrated portfolio: terminology (P7130), benchmarking (P7131), architecture (P3120, P3120.1), simulator programming (P3155), energy efficiency (P3329), HPC integration (P3185), algorithm design (P2995) | Most comprehensive engineering-society portfolio of any SDO surveyed; structurally orthogonal to QKD/QKDN focus of telecoms-oriented SDOs | All items at Active PAR (Under Development) stage; none yet at approved-standard stage by end-2022 |
| QKD and quantum communication | P1913 SDQC at application-layer protocol over TCP/IP, with SDN/OpenFlow integration design intent | Application-layer complement to QKDN operator-network work of ETSI/ITU-T | Engineering-tier scope rather than operator-network scope; no QKD module or component specifications |
| Quantum networking / quantum internet | P1913 SDQC partially adjacent to networking domain | Configuration protocol available for quantum endpoints | No end-to-end quantum-internet protocols, repeater control, or entanglement-distribution architecture (left to IRTF QIRG; Chapter 7) |
| Post-quantum cryptography | P3172 Recommended Practice for PQC migration; P1943 post-quantum network security ⚠ | Engineering-society migration guidance complement to ETSI TS 103 744 and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 algorithmic work | No dedicated PQC algorithm specifications (structurally allocated to NIST and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2) |
| Quantum random number generation | Not addressed | — | No standalone QRNG specifications |
| Quantum sensing and metrology | Not addressed | — | Entirely outside IEEE's quantum-computing-focused remit at end-2022 |

**Centre of gravity.** IEEE's centre of gravity within the global quantum standardization landscape at end-2022 is unambiguously **quantum computing**, spanning seven sub-axes (terminology, benchmarking, architecture, simulator architecture, simulator programming, energy efficiency, HPC integration, and algorithm design) across eight projects (P7130, P7131, P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3329, P3185, P2995). No other SDO surveyed in this report comes close to this breadth of quantum-computing portfolio at end-2022. ETSI has no quantum-computing portfolio. ITU-T has no quantum-computing portfolio. ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 has the foundational terminology standard (ISO/IEC 4879) and the companion overview (TR 18157) plus two preliminary work items (PWI 18660, PWI 18670), but does not approach IEEE's coverage at the benchmarking, architecture, simulator-programming, energy-efficiency, hybrid-integration, or algorithm-design tiers. The institutional explanation for this concentration is clear: IEEE's engineering-society constituency—comprising hardware engineers, software engineers, computer scientists, and applied physicists drawn from across industry and academia—is the natural community for quantum-computing engineering standardization, and the IEEE SA process is the natural channel through which that community produces standards.

**Complementarity with ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14.** The complementarity with ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 is the most architecturally explicit cross-SDO relationship in IEEE's quantum portfolio. ISO/IEC 4879, having cleared CD ballot on 27 April 2022 (Chapter 5), supplies the formal-IS-tier vocabulary baseline structured along basic concepts, hardware, software, and applications axes; IEEE P7130 supplies the engineering-society-tier definitions standard. IEEE P7131 layers benchmarking methodology atop both terminology baselines; IEEE P3120, P3120.1, P3155, and P3329 layer architecture, simulator, and energy-efficiency specifications; IEEE P3185 and P2995 layer hybrid-integration and algorithm-design specifications. The architecture is one in which **ISO/IEC supplies the cross-SDO terminology anchor and IEEE supplies the engineering-tier specifications layered atop the anchor**—an institutional division of labour that closely parallels the QKD layering observed across ETSI, ITU-T, and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3 in Chapters 3–5.

**Complementarity with ETSI and ITU-T.** In the quantum communication and networking domain, IEEE's contribution through P1913 SDQC complements rather than overlaps with the operator-network QKDN work of ETSI and ITU-T. ETSI's ISG QKD GS QKD 014 specifies a REST/HTTPS/JSON key delivery API for cryptographic application access to QKDN keys (Chapter 3); ETSI's GS QKD 015/018 specify SDN control and orchestration interfaces at the equipment-vendor level (Chapter 3); ITU-T's Y.3805 specifies SDN control architecture at the QKDN network level (Chapter 4); ITU-T's Q.QKDN-series specifies per-reference-point classical-layer interface protocols (Chapter 4). IEEE's P1913 specifies, at the application layer over TCP/IP, the SDQC configuration protocol that exposes engineer-level configuration of quantum endpoints to "dynamically create, modify, or remove quantum protocols or applications" with design intent for "future integration with Software-Defined Networking and Open Networking Foundation OpenFlow".[^34] The cross-SDO architecture in the quantum-communication domain at end-2022 thus contains application-layer (IEEE P1913), key-delivery API (ETSI GS QKD 014), SDN control/orchestration (ETSI GS QKD 015/018; ITU-T Y.3805), classical-layer interface protocols (ITU-T Q.QKDN-series), network architecture (ITU-T Y.3800-series), security framework (ITU-T X.1710-series), and formal evaluation methodology (ISO/IEC 23837 series) as distinct layers, each developed by the SDO whose institutional position best supports it.

**Complementarity with the post-quantum cryptography tracks.** In the PQC domain, IEEE's P3172 Recommended Practice supplies migration guidance that complements ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC's hybrid key-exchange specification TS 103 744 (Chapter 3) and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2's algorithmic standardization work on ISO/IEC 14888-4 and ISO/IEC 18033-2 (Chapter 5). The three SDOs partition the PQC standardization workload as follows: **NIST and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 supply the algorithm specifications, ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC supplies the protocol-level integration specifications such as hybrid key exchange, and IEEE supplies the engineering-society migration guidance**. P3172's scope emphasis on "hybrid mechanisms (combinations of classical quantum-vulnerable and quantum-resistant public-key algorithms)" and on "crypto agility" as a desired characteristic[^34] dovetails directly with the hybrid-mechanism paradigm that the ETSI and ISO/IEC tracks operationalize. The directional expansion of IEEE's PQC remit through P1943 post-quantum network security (catalogued ⚠ for end-2022 stage confirmation) signals that the network-protocol layer of PQC migration—addressing quantum-resistant variants of network security protocols with hybrid modes and larger public key sizes—is an emerging strand of IEEE's portfolio.

**Residual gaps.** Three residual gaps in IEEE's quantum portfolio at end-2022 are identifiable and structurally significant.

*First*, the **absence of quantum sensing, quantum metrology, and standalone quantum random number generation work**. Quantum sensing and metrology are entirely outside IEEE's quantum-computing-focused remit at end-2022, consistent with the institutional partition by which these domains are addressed—imperfectly—through IEC SEG 14's horizontal mapping function (Chapter 5) rather than through any of the SDOs surveyed in this report at item-level normative specification. Standalone QRNG is similarly absent from IEEE's portfolio, consistent with the broader pattern that QRNG receives dedicated standardization treatment in no surveyed SDO and is addressed only as a functional component within QKD module specifications (notably ETSI's ISG QKD work; Chapter 3) and within ISO/IEC's QKD module evaluation context (Chapter 5).

*Second*, the **uniformly Active PAR (Under Development) status of the entire portfolio at end-2022**. No IEEE quantum-domain project has completed SA Standards Board approval by the cutoff, which means that the substantive convergence of IEEE's quantum portfolio to approved standards remains a post-cutoff prospect across all eleven projects catalogued in §6.8. This is consistent with the field's overall maturity profile but does mean that the catalogue records project scopes and working-group activity rather than published normative content for the IEEE column of the cross-SDO synthesis.

*Third*, the **structural drafting challenges visible in P7130 and foreshadowing later C/SAB SC deliberations**. The Quantum Computing Nomenclature Working Group's situation at end-2022—a 60-page initial draft developed without progression to SA ballot[^29]—is a substantive challenge that the C/SAB SC's subsequent (post-cutoff) deliberations would address through the three options of proceeding to SA Ballot, withdrawing the PAR, or undertaking major revision/addition of definitions.[^29][^35] The challenge itself was already present and visible at end-2022, even though the C/SAB SC's formal deliberation of the three options is post-cutoff context. The structural issue—that stabilising terminology in a field with heterogeneous and rapidly evolving hardware platforms is genuinely difficult—is not unique to IEEE: ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14's parallel work on ISO/IEC 4879, which reached CD-passed status in April 2022, has navigated comparable challenges through its post-CD comment resolution cycle (Chapter 5). The contrast between ISO/IEC 4879's CD-passed status and IEEE P7130's pre-ballot 60-page draft at end-2022 illustrates that the two SDOs have taken different procedural paths to addressing the same substantive difficulty, with ISO/IEC reaching a more advanced procedural stage by the cutoff.

**Trajectory at the cutoff.** Taken in aggregate, IEEE's quantum standardization trajectory at end-2022 displays a broad and substantively coherent portfolio that has accumulated through the C/SAB SC's PAR-review cadence, that is held together at the technical level by the QGL informal coordination forum, that is approaching but has not yet crossed the threshold of formal SA Standards Board approval for any item, and that occupies a centre of gravity—quantum computing—that is substantively orthogonal to the QKD/QKDN-focused work of the telecommunications SDOs and complementary with the formal terminology anchor supplied by ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14. The portfolio's distinctive contribution to the global quantum standardization architecture is the engineering-society-tier specifications at benchmarking, architecture, simulator, energy-efficiency, hybrid-integration, and algorithm-design layers that no other surveyed SDO supplies; the residual gaps in sensing, metrology, and standalone QRNG, and the field-wide pre-publication stage of the portfolio, will both be revisited in Chapter 9's gap analysis.

With IEEE's catalogue and assessment now established and the engineering-society pillar of the global quantum standardization architecture documented, the report proceeds in Chapter 7 to IRTF, whose Quantum Internet Research Group operates at the pre-standardization research tier that is upstream of any of the engineering- and operator-tier standardization surveyed in Chapters 3–6, and whose research outputs feed the longer-term architectural thinking on quantum networking that may, in due course, inform formal IETF standardization.

# 7 IRTF: Research Groups and Quantum Internet Pre-Standardization

Chapter 6 closed by observing that IEEE's quantum standardization portfolio, though broad across the quantum-computing engineering domain and substantively orthogonal to the QKD/QKDN focus of the telecommunications-oriented SDOs, leaves untouched the end-to-end architectural research on quantum networks that the long-horizon vision of a "quantum internet" demands. That research territory is occupied, within the five-SDO scope of this report, by the Internet Research Task Force. The IRTF differs categorically from the four SDOs surveyed in Chapters 3–6: it is not a standards development organisation. As IRTF's own organisational description records, "the Internet Research Task Force (IRTF) focuses on longer term research issues related to the Internet while the parallel organization, the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF), focuses on the shorter term issues of engineering and standards making"[^36]. The IRTF "is comprised of a number of focused and long-term Research Groups" whose "stable long-term membership" is intended "to promote the development of research collaboration and teamwork in exploring research issues", with participation "by individual contributors, rather than by representatives of organizations"[^36]. This structural orientation positions the IRTF, and within it the Quantum Internet Research Group (QIRG), as the **pre-standardization research tier** of the global quantum standardization landscape, producing outputs whose normative force is fundamentally different from those of an ETSI Group Specification, an ITU-T Recommendation, an ISO/IEC International Standard, or an IEEE Standard.

This chapter examines that pre-standardization tier. §7.1 reconstructs IRTF's institutional position and QIRG's mandate; §7.2 catalogues QIRG's substantive research agenda; §7.3 records the verified deliverables active at the end-2022 cutoff; §7.4 examines QIRG's coordination role with other SDOs and its potential to feed future IETF standardization; §7.5 assesses IRTF's distinctive contribution to the global landscape, its complementarity with the other four SDOs, and the structural maturity limits of its outputs at the cutoff.

## 7.1 Institutional Position of IRTF and the QIRG Mandate

The institutional position of the IRTF within the broader IETF/IRTF/IAB ecosystem is defined operationally rather than nominally. The IRTF "is managed by the IRTF Chair in consultation with the Internet Research Steering Group (IRSG)", whose membership "includes the IRTF Chair, the chairs of the various Research Groups, and other individuals ('members at large') from the research community selected by the IRTF Chair"[^36]. The governance instrument that articulates the IRTF's relationship to the IETF—and that is, as noted in Chapter 2, load-bearing for any interpretation of IRTF status—is RFC 7418, which "gives an IRTF Primer for IETF Participants" and "provides a high-level description of issues for IETF participants to consider when bringing proposals for new research groups into the IRTF, and emphasizes differences in expectations between the two organizations"[^36]. The substantive distinctions codified by RFC 7418 and recounted in Chapter 2 are central to this chapter: IRTF Research Group charters are "much broader than IETF WG charters" and "often discuss different topics underneath the charter umbrella at different times"; their typical effort horizon is "at least three to five years, significantly longer than IETF WGs envision when they are chartered"; "IRTF RGs are encouraged to explore more than one alternative approach to the chartered problem area"; and "there is no expectation that the RG will 'come to consensus' on one approach", with the RG potentially publishing "multiple competing proposals as research produces results". Most consequentially for the normative-force analysis of this report, "documents approved for publication by the IRSG are not a candidate for any level of Internet Standard". This structural orientation directly underwrites the uniform status labelling for IRTF outputs introduced in Chapter 2 (*Individual Draft* / *RG Draft* / *Published as Informational RFC*) and the recognition that the absence of a published Informational RFC by end-2022 does not indicate failure or inactivity of an RG-track effort but reflects the categorically different cadence of research as compared with engineering.

Within this institutional scaffold, the QIRG is one of the IRTF Research Groups currently chartered, listed alongside fifteen others including the Crypto Forum Research Group (CFRG), the Internet Congestion Control Research Group (ICCRG), and the Thing-to-Thing Research Group (T2TRG) on IRTF's own register of active groups[^36]. The QIRG's formation trajectory is documented in the contemporaneous account of its origins at the IETF 101 Open Meeting in London: "The Internet Research Task Force (IRTF) Open Meeting at the IETF 101 in London had a rather curious point on the agenda: 'Vision for a QIRG: Quantum Internet Research Group'", and "thus, that Open Meeting has resulted in the formation of the Quantum Internet Research Group (QIRG) in the IRTF"[^37]. The Group was initially constituted as a proposed Research Group and, "following an evaluation period as a proposed Research Group", "has been approved as a full IRTF Research Group as of March of this year"—a statement made in the context of an article that records QIRG presence at IETF meetings 103, 104, 106, 107, and 108[^37]. The proposed-to-full transition through the IRSG evaluation cycle is the canonical IRTF process for new Research Group formation, and QIRG completed it within roughly two years of the IETF 101 vision presentation.

QIRG's leadership across the report's evidence window has been provided by its co-chairs, **Wojciech Kozlowski**, "a post-doctoral researcher at the Delft University of Technology (TU Delft)", and **Rodney Van Meter**, "a professor at Keio University's Shonan Fujisawa Campus, a WIDE Project board member, and one of the leaders of the Quantum Internet Task Force"[^37].
