The two co-chairs jointly delivered the chair slides at the IETF 113 Quantum Internet Research Group session held on 22 March 2022 in Vienna, evidencing continuity of leadership through the cutoff[^37]. The TU Delft and Keio affiliations of the co-chairs are themselves analytically significant: they connect QIRG directly to two of the principal academic centres of quantum networking research—the pan-European Quantum Internet Alliance led from TU Delft and the Japanese Quantum Internet Task Force in which Van Meter is a leader—giving the Group a research-community embeddedness that the engineering-society constituency of an IEEE working group or the operator-vendor constituency of an ETSI ISG does not by design seek to replicate.

The QIRG's substantive charter scope is articulated in the Group's own self-description as the venue addressing the "many things that make entanglement distribution different from packet transmission"[^37]. The classical internet "does not have to deal with entangled particles, the no-cloning theorem, and entanglement swapping", and "the differences are not limited to just the physical layer as the higher layers will also need to account for these differences. The Quantum Internet will need its own protocol stack"[^37]. The chartered work programme is therefore explicitly framed around the long-term protocol-and-architecture challenges that this categorically new substrate raises, with the Group functioning as a forum to "encourage and foster collaborations between physicists and network engineers to tackle these problems"[^37]. This scope is structurally distinct from the QKDN scope of ITU-T's Y.3800-series catalogued in Chapter 4: where QKDN, as defined in Y.3800, is a network of QKD nodes connected through QKD links that supplies symmetric keys to a classical user network, QIRG's quantum internet is an entanglement-distribution substrate whose protocol stack must accommodate the no-cloning theorem, entanglement swapping at quantum repeaters, and the coherence-time-bounded resource management that QKDN's symmetric-key supply does not require. The distinction is preserved consistently across this report: QKDN and the quantum internet "involve 'quantum networks' in some sense; they address fundamentally different architectural problems" (Chapter 1, §1.4).

The structural implication of these institutional and charter features for output maturity is summarised cleanly. IRTF outputs are positioned at the **pre-standardization research tier** by design; their status labels (Chapter 2) reflect a deliberately distinct lifecycle from the standards-track lifecycles of the other four SDOs; and their normative force—even at the highest stage of IRSG approval as an Informational RFC—remains explicitly non-Standards-Track and "might not be suitable for deployment". This is the conceptual frame within which the catalogue of §7.3 and the assessment of §7.5 must be read.

## 7.2 QIRG Research Agenda and Technical Scope

The QIRG research agenda at end-2022 is documented across two principal sources: the Group's own charter enumeration of open research problems and its co-chairs' analytical account of the substantive technical landscape. Together these sources articulate a research programme structured around three layers: the fundamental task of the quantum internet, the technical enablers required to perform that task, and the open research problems whose resolution is necessary for a deployable quantum internet to emerge.

**The fundamental task.** "The fundamental task of the Quantum Internet is to distribute entanglement"[^37]. Entanglement is characterised as "a special state of two or more quantum particles in which the individual particles cannot be described independently of the others, even if the particles are separated by arbitrary distances", and it "is the key ingredient for long-distance quantum communication because one can use entanglement, amongst other things, to teleport arbitrary quantum data"[^37]. This characterisation is foundational because it determines the protocol-level differences from the classical internet: a network that distributes entanglement, rather than packets, must accommodate the fact that quantum information cannot be copied (the no-cloning theorem), that entangled states have finite coherence times, and that quantum measurement is destructive.

**The technical enablers.** The key building block enabling entanglement distribution over arbitrary distances is the **quantum repeater**. The technical motivation arises from a fundamental constraint: "It is fundamentally impossible to copy quantum data, which is a problem given that the vast majority of photons are lost while traveling down an optical fiber. The quantum repeater solves this problem by means of entanglement swapping, whereby two short-range pairs of entangled particles can be combined to create a new entangled pair that spans a longer distance"[^37]. As of the report's evidence window, "these devices are now nearly ready for the first prototype demonstrations", with "a small-scale demo network […] currently being built in the Netherlands by the pan-European Quantum Internet Alliance (a Quantum Flagship project) and several other testbeds […] also being developed in the U.S. as part of America's Blueprint for the Quantum Internet", Japan's Quantum Internet Task Force, and China's satellite-based entanglement distribution and network programme[^37]. This pre-prototype hardware maturity is itself analytically significant: it explains why QIRG is appropriately housed at the IRTF research tier rather than at IETF as a Working Group, because the protocol questions QIRG addresses concern technology that has not yet stabilised at the engineering level.

**The application categories.** Three principal application categories motivate the quantum-internet research agenda. **Quantum cryptography** uses the fact that "quantum mechanics allows us to secure our communications at the physical level without relying on the presumed difficulty of solving certain mathematical problems", enabling "protocols that have been mathematically proven to be unbreakable"[^37]. **Cloud quantum computation** anticipates that "just like clouds on the classical Internet it is only a matter of time before their quantum counterparts start appearing", and that "being able to connect to these clouds through a quantum network will enable new kinds of cloud computing", including "blind quantum computation" that "will allow us to execute a quantum program on a server without the server ever learning the nature of said program or its input or output data"[^37]. **Quantum-enhanced sensors** combine "precision metrology and quantum networks" so that "it is possible to devise distributed protocols that can reach fundamental precision limits set by quantum theory", with "potential applications" including "geographically distributed clocks and enhanced resolution for telescopes using long-baseline interferometry"[^37]. These three categories together motivate the use-case work documented in §7.3 below, and they situate QIRG's agenda within a substantive scope that ranges across quantum communication (broader than QKD), distributed quantum computing, and quantum-enhanced metrology.

**The open research problems.** The QIRG charter enumerates a structured set of open research problems that constitute the substantive research agenda. **Resource allocation** addresses the fact that "all networks have a finite pool of resources, and quantum networks bring new resource considerations to the table, such as the coherence time of quantum memories", with the recognition that "some of the routing proposals already include a notion of dynamic traffic in the network, but it is worth making a distinction"[^38]. **Connection establishment** addresses the categorical difference that "quantum networks deliver entangled states instead of packets so the connection semantics may be different", raising the question "How does such a request look like as it propagates across the network?"[^38]. **Interoperability** addresses heterogeneous hardware: "different networks based on different hardware (ion traps, atomic ensembles, nitrogen vacancy centres) and using different protocols are currently being designed and built. How do we ensure a long-lived internetwork develops?"[^38]. **Security** addresses the recognition that although "quantum networks bring enhanced security for applications", "the question of the security of the network itself must also be addressed. Are quantum repeater networks inherently more or less vulnerable in operations than classical networks?"[^38]. **API design** addresses the inadequacy of classical sockets for entangled states: "Classical sockets are built around the concept of bits. What should an API for entangled states look like given new considerations such as fidelity, and the low coherence time of quantum memories?"[^38]. **Applications for a Quantum Internet** addresses "analyzing how to turn the low-level, abstract functions of quantum communication into services provided by the Quantum Internet, including establishing required data rates and fidelities, with specific use cases incorporating quantum services into complete information systems"[^38]. **Multi-party states and multi-party transfers such as network coding** address the recognition that "rather than simple, independent point-to-point transfers, how can we create and use more complex states?"[^38].

The seven open research problems together delineate a research agenda whose breadth—spanning resource allocation, connection semantics, interoperability, network-level security, API design, application services, and multi-party state distribution—is characteristically that of a long-horizon Research Group rather than a Working Group with a definite engineering deliverable. The charter also explicitly identifies concrete work items the Group "may produce", including "an architectural framework delineating network node roles and definitions, to build a common vocabulary and serve as the first step toward a quantum network architecture", and the use-case mapping work in which the Wehner–Elkhouss–Hanson "roadmap of technical capability milestones for quantum networks" is mapped to "concrete use cases" that "will help to determine the order and timing of classical protocols that will be needed"[^38]. These two work-item families—an architectural framework document and a use-case enumeration document—are the substantive precursors of the two flagship Internet-Drafts catalogued in §7.3.

A categorical observation closes the agenda discussion. The QIRG research scope is **not coextensive with QKD**. As the QIRG co-chairs themselves note, "single-purpose quantum key distribution (QKD) networks have been built in Boston, Geneva, Tokyo, eastern China, and elsewhere, running for well over a decade, but a more flexible network suitable for the applications above is a more challenging technical task"[^37]. The QIRG addresses that more challenging task—the flexible, multi-purpose entanglement-distribution substrate that supports cryptography, distributed quantum computing, and quantum-enhanced metrology together—not the single-purpose QKD network whose standardisation is the province of ETSI ISG QKD (Chapter 3) and ITU-T SG13/SG17/SG11 (Chapter 4). The agenda's structural distinctness from the QKD-focused work of the telecommunications SDOs is therefore not incidental but constitutive of QIRG's institutional role within the global landscape.

## 7.3 Catalogue of QIRG Internet-Drafts and Documents at End-2022

The QIRG deliverables verifiable at the 31 December 2022 cutoff comprise two flagship Internet-Drafts at advanced RG stages and a body of Research Group activity documented through periodic seminars and IETF-meeting sessions. The catalogue is presented along the normalized attribute set defined in Chapter 2.

The two flagship Internet-Drafts are the substantive precursors of the published RFCs that subsequently emerged from the QIRG process. The first, **draft-irtf-qirg-principles ("Architectural Principles for a Quantum Internet")**, supplies the architectural framework that the QIRG charter identified as a concrete work item, providing "a more detailed introduction to quantum networks, written with a classical Internet audience in mind"[^37]. Its drafting trajectory through the cutoff is documented in the IETF 113 chair slides: "draft-irtf-qirg-principles" was "reviewed by Marie-Jose Montpetit in December" 2021, with "comments […] addressed in January, currently in re-review" as of the 22 March 2022 meeting[^37]. The "re-review" status places the draft at an advanced stage within the RG-drafting lifecycle (Chapter 2's *RG Draft (active)*), one step short of the RG Last Call that would precede IRSG review. The second, **draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases ("Applications and Use Cases for the Quantum Internet")**, supplies the use-case enumeration mapping the Wehner–Elkhouss–Hanson capability milestones to concrete services. The IETF 113 chair slides record that the draft was "Active, will be discussed today"[^37]. Earlier QIRG activity at IETF 108 had similarly engaged with "two Internet-Drafts currently in progress, Applications and Use Cases for the Quantum Internet and Architectural Principles for a Quantum Internet"[^37], establishing that both drafts had been actively developed across multiple IETF sessions through the cutoff. Both flagship drafts are characterised in the contemporaneous QIRG account as having "matured significantly since their initial versions" and as being "close to their last call which will hopefully results in the first Quantum Internet RFCs"[^37], a forward-looking assessment that the post-cutoff record subsequently confirmed.

The post-cutoff publications of the two flagship drafts as IRSG-approved Informational RFCs are recorded in the IETF Datatracker QIRG document register and are flagged here as **retrospective context outside the report's evidence window**. The register records **RFC 9340, "Architectural Principles for a Quantum Internet"**, published 2023-03 as a 37-page Informational RFC, and **RFC 9583, "Application Scenarios for the Quantum Internet"**, published 2024-06 as a 26-page Informational RFC[^38]. Under the methodology of Chapter 2, RFC 9340 and RFC 9583 are not items in the report's primary inventory; their existence is recorded here only because it confirms the trajectory visible at end-2022 (the "close to their last call" status that the contemporaneous account anticipated) and because their post-cutoff IRSG approval evidences the operational soundness of the QIRG drafting process that the cutoff snapshot captures.

A third category of QIRG-adjacent activity is the **seminar programme** through which the Group sustains research-community engagement between IETF sessions. The IETF 113 chair slides record that "on 2 February 2022 Marc Kaplan from VeriQloud gave a seminar titled 'Building Quantum Networks at the Local-Area Scale'", with "recording available" online, and that the "next seminar TBD (aim for Sept/Oct)" of 2022[^37]. The seminar series is not itself a catalogued deliverable in the normalised typology, but it is recorded here as evidence that QIRG operated as an active Research Group through the cutoff, sustaining substantive engagement with adjacent research efforts in the quantum-networks community.

The IETF-meeting cadence through which QIRG progressed its work programme is similarly documented. The Group "has met regularly and has been present at most of the IETF meetings since its inception (IETF 103, 104, 106, 107, 108)"[^37], with the IETF 108 Online session having "discussed potential attacks on the Quantum Internet as well as two Internet-Drafts currently in progress"[^37] and the IETF 113 Vienna hybrid session of 22 March 2022 covering "Administrivia + RG Status + hybrid meeting issues", the use-cases draft discussion led by Kaushik Chakraborty, and "Quantum Network Explorer: application programming for the quantum internet" presented by Bart van der Vecht of QuTech, followed by an open discussion[^37]. The QIRG's meeting cadence of "2-3 meetings per year, online or in person, in accordance with current best practice"[^38] is broadly consistent with this observed record across IETF 103–113.

Table 7.1 consolidates the QIRG deliverables verifiable at end-2022 into the normalised attribute set.

| Document Identifier | Title | Type | Issuing RG | Taxonomy Domain | Status at End-2022 | Most Recent Milestone | Normative Character |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| draft-irtf-qirg-principles | Architectural Principles for a Quantum Internet | Internet-Draft | QIRG | Quantum networking / quantum internet | RG Draft (active); reviewed Dec 2021, comments addressed Jan 2022, in re-review as of Mar 2022; "close to their last call" | IETF 113 Vienna, 22 March 2022[^37] | Pre-standardization research; non-normative; not on Standards Track |
| draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases | Applications and Use Cases for the Quantum Internet | Internet-Draft | QIRG | Quantum networking / quantum internet | RG Draft (active); under continued discussion; "close to their last call" | IETF 113 Vienna, 22 March 2022[^37] | Pre-standardization research; non-normative; not on Standards Track |
| (seminar series, not a deliverable) | QIRG research seminars | Activity | QIRG | Quantum networking / quantum internet | Active; Feb 2022 VeriQloud seminar held; next seminar planned for Sept/Oct 2022 | 2 Feb 2022[^37] | n/a (activity, not deliverable) |

| Post-cutoff context (not in primary inventory) | Title | Type | Published | Page Count |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RFC 9340 | Architectural Principles for a Quantum Internet | Informational RFC (IRTF stream) | 2023-03 | 37 pages[^38] |
| RFC 9583 | Application Scenarios for the Quantum Internet | Informational RFC (IRTF stream) | 2024-06 | 26 pages[^38] |

Three interpretive observations follow from the catalogue. First, the **entire substantive QIRG portfolio at end-2022 is captured by two RG Drafts** at advanced (re-review / approaching Last Call) stage, with no Informational RFC yet published within the evidence window. This is the canonical IRTF cadence for a Research Group that completed its proposed-to-full transition in 2020 and is operating on the three-to-five-year horizon characteristic of IRTF efforts (Chapter 2); it is not a sign of inactivity or stagnation. Second, the **mapping of both flagship drafts onto the quantum networking / quantum internet domain** of the Chapter 1 taxonomy is unambiguous, and the absence of any QIRG output in other taxonomy domains—QKD specifically (left to ETSI and ITU-T), quantum computing (left to IEEE), PQC (left to ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC, ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2, NIST, and IEEE), QRNG, quantum sensing and metrology—reflects the deliberate scope discipline of the QIRG charter. Third, the **non-normative character** of both flagship deliverables—Internet-Drafts at the cutoff and Informational RFCs post-cutoff—is preserved as a uniform attribute across the QIRG entries in the catalogue, distinguishing them categorically from the normative outputs catalogued for ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, and IEEE.

## 7.4 QIRG's Role in Coordination with Other SDOs and Feeding Future IETF Standardization

A defining feature of QIRG's charter mandate is the **explicit coordination role** it occupies with respect to other standards-development organisations. The charter states that "QIRG will serve as a coordination point with other standards organizations that are working on standardization of quantum networks"[^38]. This positioning is structurally important: it acknowledges that, even as IRTF operates at the pre-standardization research tier, its outputs are intended to inform—and be informed by—the standards-development activity occurring in parallel at ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and elsewhere.

The operational evidence of this coordination role at the cutoff is twofold. First, the QIRG's IETF 113 chair slides explicitly recorded its external recognition in the broader quantum standardisation literature, noting under "Other RG Updates" that "QIRG is mentioned in a recent pre-print publication about the standardization efforts for quantum technologies"[^37]. The Group thus tracks, and is tracked by, the wider community of quantum-standardisation actors. Second, the co-chair affiliations—Kozlowski at TU Delft (a principal site of the pan-European Quantum Internet Alliance, itself a Quantum Flagship project) and Van Meter at Keio and the Japanese Quantum Internet Task Force[^37]—provide direct personal channels by which the QIRG research agenda interfaces with national and regional quantum-networking programmes that themselves engage with the SDOs catalogued in earlier chapters.

The institutional pipeline from IRTF research output to potential IETF standardisation is articulated, in general terms, by the IRTF Primer for IETF Participants and is empirically illustrated by the structural relationship between QIRG and IETF in the quantum-networking domain. Once an RG Draft achieves IRSG approval and is published as an Informational RFC, the content of that RFC—architectural principles, use-case enumeration, or other research outputs—is available for subsequent reference by any IETF Working Group that may, in the longer term, be chartered to develop standards-track protocols on the same substrate. The structural mechanism by which mature architectural research in an IRTF RG can graduate to engineering standardisation in an IETF WG is well established across the IRTF's history. For QIRG specifically, the contemporaneous co-chair account anticipated this trajectory explicitly: "the two Internet-Drafts currently being worked on the QIRG will lay down a framework for further research work to continue within the IRTF community", and "building a robust, useful quantum Internet that integrates smoothly with the classical Internet requires the expertise not just of the physicists working at the physical layer, but also of those who understand networks and distributed systems – i.e., the IETF/IRTF community"[^37]. The pipeline from QIRG research outputs to eventual IETF standardisation activity is therefore not an automatic consequence of RFC publication but a longer-term institutional possibility whose realisation depends on both the maturity of the underlying hardware (still at "the proof-of-concept stages" at the cutoff[^37]) and the demonstrated need for engineering-tier standardisation that the QIRG research outputs will, in due course, help to define.

A complementary feature of the coordination role is QIRG's function as an **upstream research venue whose outputs are watched by other SDOs for long-term architectural guidance**. The QIRG charter's explicit commitment to coordination with other quantum-networking standardisation efforts[^38], combined with the co-chairs' embeddedness in the major national and regional quantum-networking research consortia[^37], gives the Group a structural visibility within the broader landscape that the formal output catalogue alone does not capture. ETSI's ISG QKD, ITU-T's SG13/SG17/SG11 study groups, and ISO/IEC's JTC 1/SC 27—each catalogued in Chapters 3–5—maintain their own scope discipline around the QKD-focused near-term standardisation that the report has documented. None of these SDOs has, at end-2022, established a working group, study group, or industry specification group whose scope is end-to-end quantum-internet architecture. The QIRG's distinctive position is therefore not duplicative of any active standardisation venue but is the **upstream research tier** whose outputs may, in the longer term, supply the architectural baseline that future standardisation in those other SDOs (or in IETF) will require.

It is important to record what the coordination role does and does not entail. The QIRG outputs, even at the highest stage of IRSG approval as an Informational RFC, confer **no normative force** on any of the standards-development organisations they may inform. RFC 7418's articulation of the non-Standards-Track character of IRSG-approved RFCs (recounted in Chapter 2) governs the QIRG outputs as much as any other IRTF RG outputs. The relationship between QIRG and the formally normative SDOs is therefore one of informational availability rather than of binding influence: the architectural principles articulated in a future RFC 9340-style document are available for any ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, or IEEE working group to reference, but they do not constrain what those groups choose to standardise. This is precisely the relationship that the IRTF's institutional design contemplates, and it is the structural reason why the report records IRTF as a categorically distinct fifth pillar of the global quantum standardisation landscape rather than as a peer SDO to the four standards-developing organisations catalogued in Chapters 3–6.

## 7.5 Assessment of IRTF's Contribution: Distinctive Position, Complementarity, and Maturity Limits

When the per-deliverable catalogue of §7.3 is mapped against the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1 and read against the ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, and IEEE catalogues of Chapters 3–6, IRTF's quantum standardisation profile as of end-2022 displays a structurally distinctive contribution, a coherent complementarity with the other four SDOs, and a clearly identifiable set of maturity limits. Table 7.2 summarises the mapping.

| Taxonomy Domain | IRTF (QIRG) Coverage as of End-2022 | Strength | Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| Quantum networking / quantum internet | draft-irtf-qirg-principles; draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases (both RG Drafts active, approaching Last Call) | Sole venue across the surveyed SDOs addressing end-to-end quantum-internet architecture, entanglement distribution, repeaters, and application protocols on a quantum substrate | All flagship outputs at RG Draft stage; no Informational RFC published by end-2022; non-normative by design |
| QKD and quantum communication | Not in scope as a QKD standardization venue; QKD treated only as one of three application categories that motivate the broader quantum-internet agenda | — | Operator-network QKDN scope explicitly the province of ETSI and ITU-T (Chapters 3–4) |
| Quantum computing | Not in scope, except as an application substrate (blind cloud quantum computation) that the quantum internet would support | — | Quantum-computing engineering standardisation is the province of IEEE (Chapter 6) and ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 (Chapter 5) |
| Post-quantum cryptography | Not in scope | — | PQC standardisation is the province of ETSI, ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2, IEEE, and (upstream) NIST |
| Quantum random number generation | Not in scope | — | No QRNG-specific work across any surveyed SDO at end-2022 |
| Quantum sensing and metrology | Not in scope as a standardisation domain; touched only as a motivating application category (distributed clocks, telescope interferometry) | — | Quantum sensing and metrology remain the most thinly addressed domain across the surveyed SDOs (Chapter 9) |

**Distinctive position.** QIRG occupies a position within the global quantum standardisation landscape that no other surveyed SDO occupies: it is the **sole venue addressing end-to-end quantum-internet architecture** at item-level deliverable specification. The substantive content of its flagship deliverables—architectural principles for a quantum internet and application scenarios for the quantum internet—is categorically distinct from the QKDN architectural recommendations of ITU-T's Y.3800-series catalogued in Chapter 4. The structural reason for this distinctness has been recorded throughout the report: QKDN is, in the Y.3800 definition, a network of QKD nodes connected through QKD links that supplies symmetric keys to a classical user network, while the quantum internet QIRG addresses is an entanglement-distribution substrate whose protocol stack accommodates the no-cloning theorem, entanglement swapping, and coherence-time-bounded resource allocation. The cross-SDO architecture documented across Chapters 3–7 thus contains, in the QKD/quantum-communication tier, a layered division of labour among ETSI, ITU-T, and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3, and in the quantum-internet tier, a distinct upstream research function carried by QIRG alone.

**Complementarity with the other four SDOs.** The complementarity between QIRG and each of the four standards-developing SDOs is one of layered temporal cadence rather than substantive overlap. The standardisation work of ETSI ISG QKD, ITU-T SG13/SG17/SG11, ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3, and IEEE quantum-domain working groups addresses the near-term engineering and operator-network deployment of QKD and quantum-computing technologies whose hardware is at or approaching commercial readiness. The research work of QIRG addresses the longer-term protocol-stack and architectural challenges of an entanglement-distribution substrate whose hardware is "now nearly ready for the first prototype demonstrations"[^37] and whose deployment horizon is therefore measurably further out. The two cadences are not in tension: they reflect the categorically different maturity profiles of QKD as a near-deployable technology and the quantum internet as a long-horizon vision. In the cross-SDO landscape, QIRG's contribution is therefore best understood as **the upstream research tier whose outputs may, in the longer term, supply the architectural baseline for downstream standardisation** in IETF, in the four SDOs surveyed in Chapters 3–6, or in venues yet to be constituted.

**Maturity limits at the cutoff.** Three maturity limits of the QIRG portfolio at end-2022 warrant explicit recognition.

*First*, **no Informational RFC had been published within the report's evidence window**. The two flagship Internet-Drafts were at RG Draft (active) stage approaching Last Call, with their post-cutoff progression to RFC 9340 (March 2023) and RFC 9583 (June 2024) confirming the trajectory but lying outside the report's primary inventory. This is consistent with the structurally longer cadence of research relative to engineering codified in RFC 7418 and recounted in §7.1, and it is not a sign of weakness in the Group's substantive work.

*Second*, **the non-normative character of IRTF outputs is by design**. Even at the highest stage of IRSG approval, an Informational RFC is "not a candidate for any level of Internet Standard" and "might not be suitable for deployment" (Chapter 2). The QIRG outputs are therefore positioned to inform but not to bind subsequent engineering standardisation, and the report's comparative synthesis must read QIRG's contribution at the appropriate authority tier.

*Third*, the **breadth of the seven open research problems in QIRG's charter** (§7.2) means that substantial portions of the research agenda—resource allocation with coherence-time constraints, connection establishment for entangled states, interoperability across heterogeneous hardware, network-level security of quantum repeaters, API design for entangled states, applications, and multi-party states—were not yet at the stage of dedicated Internet-Drafts at the cutoff. The two flagship drafts addressed the architectural-principles and use-case sub-axes; the other sub-axes remained as open research areas within the Group's chartered remit rather than as drafted documents.

**Residual cross-SDO gaps.** Two structural observations carry from this assessment into the comparative synthesis of Chapter 8 and the gap analysis of Chapter 9.

The first is that **no other surveyed SDO covers the quantum-internet architectural research domain** at end-2022. ETSI's vertical QKD stack (Chapter 3) addresses component-and-interface specification for QKD; ITU-T's Y.3800-series (Chapter 4) addresses QKDN operator-network architecture; ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3 (Chapter 5) addresses formal QKD evaluation methodology; ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 addresses quantum-computing terminology; IEEE's portfolio (Chapter 6) addresses quantum-computing engineering and emerging quantum-communication application-layer protocols. None of these venues has, at the cutoff, a working group, study group, or industry specification group whose scope is end-to-end entanglement distribution, quantum-repeater protocols, or application protocols on a quantum substrate. QIRG is therefore the unique venue addressing this domain, and the cross-SDO landscape contains no alternative standardisation pathway for the quantum-internet architectural research that QIRG conducts.

The second is that **the structural limit of IRTF outputs as pre-standardization and non-normative by design** is a feature, not a deficiency, of the global landscape. The IRTF's institutional design contemplates exactly this role: a research tier that explores long-term architectural questions on time horizons too distant for formal standards-development processes, with outputs available to inform downstream standardisation as and when the underlying technology matures. The pre-standardization positioning of QIRG is therefore the **architecturally appropriate institutional response** to the maturity profile of quantum-internet hardware at end-2022, and it would be a category error to assess QIRG's contribution against the normative-output benchmarks appropriate to ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, or IEEE.

Taken in aggregate, IRTF's contribution to the global quantum standardisation landscape at end-2022 is the **upstream research function** that supplies architectural principles and use-case enumeration for the long-horizon quantum internet, complementary to but categorically distinct from the QKDN-focused work of the telecommunications SDOs, the formal-evaluation work of ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3, and the quantum-computing engineering work of IEEE. The two flagship Internet-Drafts active at the cutoff—draft-irtf-qirg-principles and draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases—constitute the verifiable QIRG portfolio at end-2022; their post-cutoff publication as RFC 9340 and RFC 9583 confirms the trajectory visible at the cutoff but lies outside the report's primary inventory. With IRTF's catalogue and assessment now established, the report proceeds in Chapter 8 to the cross-organisational comparative synthesis that places ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and IRTF together against the six-domain taxonomy and reads their layered division of labour as the integrated global quantum standardisation architecture as of end-2022.

# 8 Cross-Organization Comparative Analysis and Consolidated Deliverables Catalogue

The five organization-by-organization catalogues assembled in Chapters 3–7 have, individually, established what each of ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and IRTF contributes to the global quantum standardization landscape as of the end-2022 cutoff. Read in isolation, however, those per-SDO catalogues do not by themselves answer the higher-order question that motivates this report: how do the five contributions compose into an integrated global architecture, where do they reinforce one another, where do they overlap, and where do they leave residual gaps? This chapter performs the cross-organizational synthesis that answers that question. It proceeds in seven sections. §8.1 establishes the six analytical dimensions along which the comparison is conducted. §8.2 overlays the per-SDO portfolios onto the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1. §8.3 compares document typologies, normative force, and maturity stages. §8.4 examines overlaps, complementarities, and the formal coordination mechanisms that hold the multi-SDO architecture together. §8.5 compares constituencies, output pace, and institutional heritage as explanatory variables for the observed division of labour. §8.6 provides the consolidated, navigable deliverables catalogue that constitutes the report's reference synthesis. §8.7 closes with the integrated characterization of the global architecture as of end-2022 that the gap analysis of Chapter 9 will take as its analytical baseline.

## 8.1 Comparative Framework and Analytical Dimensions

The comparison conducted in this chapter operates along **six analytical dimensions**, each formally defined and operationalised so that the resulting matrices in §§8.2–8.5 are constructed on a uniform and auditable basis.

**Dimension 1 — Thematic coverage against the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1.** Each SDO's deliverable portfolio is mapped onto the six domains: QKD and quantum communication, post-quantum cryptography, quantum computing, quantum networking / quantum internet, quantum random number generation, and quantum sensing and metrology. The mapping is exhaustive against the items catalogued in Chapters 3–7 and is operationalised in §8.2 through a coverage matrix that records, for each (SDO, domain) cell, the principal deliverables anchoring the SDO's contribution. The taxonomic discipline established in Chapter 1—particularly the distinction between PQC and QKD, and between QKDN and the quantum internet—is preserved throughout the mapping.

**Dimension 2 — Document typology and normative force.** Each SDO's deliverable types carry materially different authority. ETSI Group Specifications and Group Reports are consensus-developed within an Industry Specification Group and "represent the views of those members who participated in this ISG. It [does] not necessarily represent the views of the entire ETSI membership"[^39]. ITU-T Recommendations are approved either under the Alternative Approval Process formalized in A.8-2022—where, after Study Group consent, the Director of TSB initiates a four-week Last Call and, if non-editorial comments are received, an additional three-week review[^11]—or under the Traditional Approval Process "set out in WTSA Resolution 1" applicable to Recommendations with policy or regulatory implications[^11]. ISO/IEC International Standards are developed through the multi-stage NWIP → CD → DIS → FDIS → IS progression codified in the ISO/IEC Directives, Part 1, 2022 thirteenth edition[^10]. IEEE document types are codified in the SA Standards Board Bylaws as **Standard, Recommended Practice, Guide, and Trial-Use Document**, with the last having "an effective period not exceeding three years" and with IEEE standards approved by "affirmative votes by at least 75% of the voting members present"[^8]. IRTF outputs, by contrast, are explicitly **non-Standards-Track**: RFC 7418 records that "documents approved for publication by the IRSG are not a candidate for any level of Internet Standard"[^9], with the IRTF document stream defined in RFC 5743[^9]. The comparison in §8.3 records these typology distinctions explicitly rather than smoothing them.

**Dimension 3 — Maturity stage at end-2022.** Each catalogued item is assigned the uniform status label introduced in Chapter 2: *Published* / *Approved* / *Active (Approved Standard)* at the highest tier; *Consented* / *FDIS* / *DIS* / *CD* / *Active PAR (Under Development)* / *RG Draft (active)* at intermediate tiers; and *NWIP* / *PWI* / *Individual Draft* / *Under Study* at the earliest tiers. The aggregation in §8.3 counts items by stage per SDO, producing a maturity profile that highlights structural patterns invisible in any single per-SDO catalogue.

**Dimension 4 — Output pace and cumulative volume across the evidence window.** Output pace is operationalised as the rate at which an SDO produces new deliverables across the evidence window (approximately 2008–2022 for ETSI ISG QKD; 2019–2022 for the ITU-T Y.3800-series; 2017–2022 for the ISO/IEC 23837 project; 2020–2022 for ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14; 2018–2022 for QIRG; and the rolling four-year PAR window for IEEE projects). Cumulative volume is operationalised as the number of items catalogued for each SDO at end-2022.

**Dimension 5 — Geographic and industrial constituency.** Each SDO's constituency profile shapes both the scope and the cadence of its quantum work. ETSI's constituency is "More than 800 organisations […], covering 64 countries all over the world"[^17], with the ISG QKD founding base drawn from European telecommunications operators, equipment vendors, research laboratories, and quantum-specialist firms. ITU-T's constituency is national administrations and recognized operating agencies under the UN treaty context. ISO/IEC JTC 1 operates through national-body delegation. IEEE's constituency is engineering-society individuals and corporate participants. IRTF participation is "by individual contributors, rather than by representatives of organizations"[^9]. The §8.5 analysis examines how constituency shapes scope.

**Dimension 6 — Degree of mutual coordination.** Coordination is operationalised through (i) explicit liaison declarations attached to specific work items, (ii) participation in joint coordination activities such as ITU-T's JCA-QKDN, and (iii) cross-references in published deliverables. JCA-QKDN, in particular, constitutes the most consolidated formal multi-SDO coordination instrument in the quantum domain at end-2022; its outgoing liaison statements—including JCA-QKDN-LS5 of 17 May 2024 distributed for information to IEEE, ISO/IEC JTC1/SC27, IEC TC 86, GSMA IG, ETSI ISG QKD, ETSI TC Cyber WG QSC, IRTF QIRG, ISO/IEC JTC1/WG14, ITU-T SG11, SG13, SG15, SG17, CEN/CLC/JTC 22, CCSA ST7, QED-C, GSMA PQTN, and ISO/IEC JTC3[^39]—evidence the scope of the multi-SDO coordination network at the boundary of the cutoff.

These six dimensions are not orthogonal: typology and maturity, for instance, are correlated; constituency and pace likewise. The matrices in §§8.2–8.5 do not assume orthogonality but use the dimensions as complementary lenses through which the same five-SDO portfolio is read from different analytical angles.

## 8.2 Thematic Coverage Mapping Across the Six-Domain Taxonomy

The principal cross-SDO coverage matrix, constructed from the per-organization catalogues of Chapters 3–7, is presented in Table 8.1. Each cell records the principal deliverable(s) anchoring the SDO's contribution to the domain, or indicates structural non-coverage.

| Domain ↓ / SDO → | ETSI | ITU-T | ISO/IEC | IEEE | IRTF |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **QKD & quantum communication** | Vertically integrated stack: GS QKD 002/008/011/012 (modules, components); GS QKD 004 and GS QKD 014 (application interfaces); GS QKD 015 and GS QKD 018 (SDN control and orchestration); GS QKD 016 (Common Criteria Protection Profile); GR QKD 003/005/007 (vocabulary, security proofs, characterization) | Y.3800-series operator-network architecture (Y.3800/3801/3802/3803/3804/3805); QoS sub-series (Y.3806/3807/3811/3812); X.1710-series security framework with X.1712/1714/1715 element-level security Recommendations and SG17 drafts X.sec_QKDN_tn/_CM/_AA/_intrq and X.sec_QKD_profr; SG11 Q.QKDN-series interface protocols; FG-QIT4N D-series technical reports | ISO/IEC 23837-1 and 23837-2 (formal evaluation methodology) at publication-preparation phase entered Oct 2022 | P1913 SDQC application-layer protocol (Active PAR Under Development) | Not in scope as a QKD venue |
| **Post-quantum cryptography** | TS 103 744 V1.1.1 (Dec 2020) Quantum-safe Hybrid Key Exchanges[^19]; broader TC CYBER WG QSC TR series | Not in scope at end-2022 (touched only in post-cutoff Supplement 79 on QKDN integration with non-quantum cryptography) | SC 27 WG 2: ISO/IEC 14888-4 stateful hash-based signatures (under development); ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM track absorbing FrodoKEM, Classic McEliece, ML-KEM | P3172 Recommended Practice for PQC Migration; P1943 Standard for Post-Quantum Network Security ⚠ | Not in scope as a QIRG venue (PQC is the substantive focus of the parallel IETF PQUIP/LAMPS/TLS/CFRG ecosystem[^17], outside the five-SDO scope of this report) |
| **Quantum computing** | Not in scope | Not in scope | JTC 1/WG 14: ISO/IEC 4879 (CD-passed 27 Apr 2022, terminology); ISO/IEC TR 18157 (under development, overview); PWI 18660 (quantum ML datasets); PWI 18670 (quantum resource simulation platforms) | Vertically integrated portfolio: P7130 (definitions); P7131 (benchmarking); P3120, P3120.1 (architecture, simulator architecture); P3155 (simulator programming); P3329 (energy efficiency); P3185 (hybrid quantum-classical computing); P2995 (algorithm design, Trial-Use) | Not in scope as a standardization venue |
| **Quantum networking / quantum internet** | Not in scope (ETSI's quantum networking is the QKDN substrate of ISG QKD, not entanglement distribution) | Touched at landscape level by FG-QIT4N WG1 (D1.1/D1.2/D1.3/D1.4 on network aspects of QIT) but no Recommendations on entanglement distribution or repeater protocols | Not in scope | Adjacent via P1913 SDQC (application-layer SDN-integrated configuration) | Sole venue for end-to-end quantum-internet architecture: draft-irtf-qirg-principles, draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases (both RG Drafts approaching Last Call at end-2022) |
| **Quantum random number generation** | Covered as a functional component within QKD module specifications | Touched marginally via SG17 work on QKDN security where QRNG functions intersect | Embedded within QKD module evaluation context (23837 series) rather than treated as a standalone domain | Not in scope | Not in scope |
| **Quantum sensing and metrology** | Not in scope | Touched at landscape level by FG-QIT4N (pillar 4 "Quantum Sensing & Metrology") but no Recommendations | Touched by IEC SEG 14 horizontal mapping ⚠; not addressed within JTC 1 vertical structure | Not in scope | Touched as a motivating application category but not as a standardization venue |

Reading the matrix vertically and horizontally surfaces five structural patterns of the cross-SDO architecture.

**Pattern 1 — Vertically multi-tiered coverage in QKD/quantum communication.** The QKD/quantum communication row is the only one in which **four of the five SDOs** make item-level standardization contributions, organized into a clean layered architecture that §8.4 examines as the canonical complementarity pattern.

**Pattern 2 — Concentrated coverage in quantum computing with a clean two-SDO partition.** The quantum computing row is populated by only two SDOs—ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 and IEEE—in a structurally clean partition: ISO/IEC supplies the formal-IS terminology baseline through ISO/IEC 4879, and IEEE supplies the engineering-society stack layered atop that baseline.

**Pattern 3 — PQC coverage as a triangle.** The PQC row exhibits a three-vertex pattern: ETSI's TS 103 744 supplies the protocol-level hybrid key exchange specification; ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 supplies the algorithm-standardization channel through ISO/IEC 14888-4 and the ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM track; and IEEE supplies the engineering-society migration guidance through P3172. The triangle is anchored upstream by NIST's algorithm selections (CRYSTALS-Kyber, CRYSTALS-Dilithium, Falcon, SPHINCS+ announced July 2022) and operationally complemented at the protocol-integration level by the parallel IETF/IRTF ecosystem of PQUIP, CFRG, TLS, and LAMPS work-groups[^17].

**Pattern 4 — Single-venue coverage in quantum networking / quantum internet.** The quantum networking / quantum internet row is populated, at item-level deliverable specification, by IRTF QIRG alone. ITU-T's FG-QIT4N work on "network aspects of QIT" intersects at the landscape level but did not crystallize into Recommendations within the evidence window.

**Pattern 5 — Thin coverage of QRNG and quantum sensing/metrology across all five SDOs.** The QRNG and quantum-sensing rows are the most thinly populated. These two gaps are the most structurally significant blind spots that Chapter 9 will return to.

The centres of gravity that emerge are summarised in Table 8.2.

| SDO | Centre of Gravity | Secondary Strand |
|---|---|---|
| ETSI | QKD component-to-certification stack within ISG QKD | PQC hybrid-protocol specification within TC CYBER WG QSC (TS 103 744) |
| ITU-T | QKDN operator-network architecture, security framework, and interface protocols across SG13/SG17/SG11 | FG-QIT4N pre-standardization landscape outputs (D-series technical reports) |
| ISO/IEC | Formal QKD evaluation methodology (SC 27 WG 3 / 23837 series) and quantum computing terminology anchor (JTC 1/WG 14 / 4879) | PQC algorithm absorption (SC 27 WG 2 / 14888-4 and 18033-2 tracks) |
| IEEE | Engineering-society quantum computing portfolio (8 PARs spanning terminology to algorithm design) | PQC migration guidance (P3172); emerging quantum-communication application protocol (P1913); emerging post-quantum network security (P1943 ⚠) |
| IRTF | Upstream research on quantum-internet architecture (QIRG flagship drafts) | Coordination function across the broader quantum-networking research community |

## 8.3 Document Typology, Normative Force, and Maturity Comparison

Reading the coverage matrix along Dimensions 2 and 3 surfaces a second-order structural pattern: **the cross-SDO landscape contains deliverables at four distinct authority tiers and at every maturity stage from PWI to Published, with the distribution itself characteristic of each SDO's position in the architecture**.

The four authority tiers, from highest to lowest formal weight, are:

| Tier | Document Family | Procedural Anchor | SDOs Operating at Tier |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tier 1 — Formal International Standard | ISO/IEC IS | ISO/IEC Directives, Part 1, 2022[^10] | ISO/IEC |
| Tier 2 — Treaty-context Recommendation | ITU-T Recommendation (AAP or TAP) | A.8-2022 AAP[^11]; WTSA Resolution 1 TAP[^11] | ITU-T |
| Tier 3 — Industry / Engineering-Society Standard | ETSI GS/GR/TS/TR; IEEE Standard / Recommended Practice / Guide / Trial-Use Document | ETSI ISG consensus[^17]; IEEE SA Standards Board 75% approval[^8] | ETSI, IEEE |
| Tier 4 — Pre-standardization Research | IRTF Internet-Draft / Informational RFC | IRSG approval (non-Standards-Track)[^9] | IRTF |

These four tiers are **not equivalent**. An ISO/IEC International Standard carries the highest formal authority because it is developed through national-body ballot under the ISO/IEC Directives; an ITU-T Recommendation, particularly one approved under TAP, carries treaty-context weight through Member State approval; an ETSI Group Specification or an IEEE Standard carries the consensus authority of its respective industry or engineering-society membership; an IRTF Informational RFC carries no normative force at all by design. The comparative analysis must read each deliverable at its appropriate authority tier rather than treating all "standards" as equivalent.

A second structural observation follows: **the QKD/quantum communication domain is the only domain in which deliverables exist at all four tiers**. ETSI GS QKD 014 V1.1.1 (Feb 2019)[^40] sits at Tier 3; ITU-T Y.3800-series Recommendations sit at Tier 2; ISO/IEC 23837-1 and 23837-2 (at publication-preparation phase entered October 2022) sit at Tier 1 prospectively; and IRTF QIRG's flagship drafts (concerning the quantum internet substrate above which any future QKDN integration would operate) sit at Tier 4. This four-tier coverage is the structural feature that makes the QKD assurance chain — ETSI components → ETSI GS QKD 016 Protection Profile → ITU-T X.1710-series security framework → ISO/IEC 23837 evaluation methodology — operationally complete.

The **maturity distribution** at end-2022 is summarised in Table 8.3.

| SDO | Published / Approved | Under Development / Active PAR / RG Draft | Earliest Stage (NWIP/PWI/Individual Draft) | Boundary-of-Cutoff (⚠) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ETSI | Published GS family within ISG QKD (GS QKD 002/004/008/011/012/014/015/018; GR QKD 003/005/007) plus TS 103 744 V1.1.1 (Dec 2020)[^19] within TC CYBER WG QSC | Monitoring interface for QKD system (WI in drafting end-2022)[^17]; cross-domain key distribution WI; extended protection profiles | — | GS QKD 016 V1.1.1 at the publication boundary[^17] |
| ITU-T | Y.3800/3801/3802/3803/3804/3805/3806/3807/3811/3812 in SG13; X.1710/1712/1714/1715 in SG17; FG-QIT4N D0.1/D1.1/D1.2/D1.3/D1.4/D2.1/D2.2/D2.3.1/D2.3.2/D2.4/D2.5 | Q16/13 work items (Y.QKDN_BM, Y.QKDN_frint, Y.QKDN-iwfr, Y.QKDN-ml-fra, Y.QKDN-rsfr, Y.supp.QKDN-roadmap); SG17 drafts X.sec_QKDN_tn/_CM/_AA/_intrq | X.sec_QKD_profr initiated at boundary of cutoff ⚠ | SG11 Q.QKDN-series "recently approved" Recommendations Q.4160–Q.4164 ⚠ |
| ISO/IEC | (ISO/IEC 23837-1 and 23837-2 at publication-preparation phase entered October 2022; formal IS publication post-cutoff) | ISO/IEC 4879 CD-passed 27 April 2022; ISO/IEC TR 18157 in active drafting; ISO/IEC 14888-4; ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM track | ISO/IEC PWI 18660; ISO/IEC PWI 18670 | IEC SEG 14 outputs not catalogued at item-level granularity ⚠; JTC 3 quantum scope not documented at item-level ⚠ |
| IEEE | None at end-2022 (entire quantum-domain portfolio at Active PAR stage) | Active PAR (Under Development): P7130, P7131, P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3329, P3185, P2995, P1913, P3172 | — | P1943 PAR end-2022 stage ⚠ |
| IRTF | None (no Informational RFC published by end-2022) | RG Draft (active, approaching Last Call): draft-irtf-qirg-principles; draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases | — | Post-cutoff RFC 9340 (Mar 2023) and RFC 9583 (Jun 2024) recorded only as retrospective trajectory |

Three structural observations follow.

**Observation 1.** The published anchor of the global landscape sits in **ITU-T's Y.3800-series and SG17 X.1710-series, complemented by ETSI's published GS QKD family**. ITU-T's consolidation of the Y.3800-series across 2019–2022—six architecture-and-control Recommendations approved by end-2020 followed by four QoS-track Recommendations approved between 2021 and September 2022—produces the densest single-SDO accumulation of approved network-level QKD standards in the cross-SDO landscape.

**Observation 2.** ISO/IEC's most significant quantum deliverable, the **23837 series, sits at the boundary of the cutoff in publication-preparation phase**, with formal IS publication in 2023 recorded only as post-cutoff retrospective context. ISO/IEC 4879's CD-passage of 27 April 2022 places the quantum-computing terminology anchor at an intermediate ballot stage. The structural implication is that ISO/IEC's contribution to the published-tier of the global landscape **completes**, rather than dominates, the end-2022 snapshot.

**Observation 3.** **IEEE's portfolio is uniformly at Active PAR (Under Development) stage and IRTF's at RG Draft stage at the cutoff.** No IEEE quantum-domain project has emerged as an Active (Approved Standard) by 31 December 2022, and no QIRG Internet-Draft has been published as an Informational RFC by the same date. This is consistent with the field's overall pre-publication maturity profile and with each SDO's structurally distinct lifecycle cadence, but it has substantive implications: the **engineering-society and research tiers contribute substantive scope and project-level activity rather than published normative content** at end-2022.

The combined typology-and-maturity picture is therefore one in which ITU-T anchors the published normative tier at Tier 2, ETSI complements it at Tier 3 with a vertically integrated published-GS stack, ISO/IEC supplies the Tier-1 evaluation-methodology and computing-terminology anchors at boundary-of-cutoff maturity, IEEE supplies Tier-3 engineering-society project scope uniformly at the pre-publication PAR stage, and IRTF supplies Tier-4 pre-standardization research drafts.

## 8.4 Overlaps, Complementarities, and Coordination Mechanisms

The cross-SDO portfolio examined in §§8.2–8.3 exhibits three distinct patterns of inter-SDO interaction: (i) deliberate complementarity, (ii) apparent overlap that resolves to comparative advantage, and (iii) formal coordination mechanisms. Each is examined in turn.

### 8.4.1 Deliberate Complementarity

The canonical example is the **layered QKD assurance chain**. Reading vertically from component to formal international evaluation, the chain operates as follows:

1. **Component and module specification tier** — ETSI ISG QKD GS QKD 002/008/011/012 and GR QKD 003/005/007;
2. **Application-interface tier** — ETSI ISG QKD GS QKD 004 (object-style API) and GS QKD 014 (REST/HTTPS/JSON key delivery API)[^40];
3. **Network-architecture and control tier** — ITU-T SG13 Y.3800-series (Y.3800/3801/3802/3803/3804/3805);
4. **Network-level security-framework tier** — ITU-T SG17 X.1710-series (X.1710/1712/1714/1715);
5. **Interface-protocol tier** — ITU-T SG11 Q.QKDN-series (Q.QKDN_profr, Q.QKDN_AK, Q.QKDN_Kq-1, Q.QKDN_Kx, Q.QKDN_CK, Q.QKDN_Cq);
6. **Certifiable Common Criteria target tier** — ETSI ISG QKD GS QKD 016 Protection Profile;
7. **Formal international evaluation methodology tier** — ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3 ISO/IEC 23837-1 and 23837-2.

The 23837 series is characterised as "specifying security requirements, test and evaluation methods for quantum key distribution (QKD)" and Part 2 specifically as specifying "test and evaluation methods for the security evaluation of quantum key distribution (QKD)"[^39]—an apex evaluation function requiring upstream specification of what is being evaluated (supplied by ETSI's component-and-module GS family) and what its security objectives are (supplied by ITU-T's X.1710-series security framework). The architectural intent is for an equipment vendor's QKD module, specified to ETSI standards, deployed in a QKDN architected to ITU-T standards, with security framed by ITU-T SG17 Recommendations, certified against the ETSI GS QKD 016 Protection Profile, to be evaluated using the ISO/IEC 23837 methodology. No tier duplicates another.

A second example of deliberate complementarity is the **layered quantum-computing terminology architecture**. ISO/IEC 4879 supplies the formal-IS-tier vocabulary baseline that cleared CD ballot on 27 April 2022; IEEE's P7130 supplies the engineering-society-tier definitions standard that can reference the ISO/IEC vocabulary and extend it with engineering-detail definitions[^17]. IEEE's P7131 (benchmarking), P3120 (architecture), P3120.1 (simulator architecture), P3155 (simulator programming), P3329 (energy efficiency), P3185 (hybrid integration), and P2995 (algorithm design) layer engineering-society-tier specifications atop the dual terminology anchors.

A third example, less consolidated but structurally analogous, is the **layered PQC architecture**: NIST's algorithm selections upstream of ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2's algorithm-standardization tracks[^17], operationalized at protocol level by ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC's TS 103 744[^19] and by the parallel IETF/IRTF ecosystem (PQUIP, CFRG, TLS, LAMPS)[^17], and by IEEE's P3172 Recommended Practice and P1943 Standard ⚠.

### 8.4.2 Apparent Overlaps Resolved as Comparative Advantage

Closer inspection of the matrix reveals several apparent overlaps that resolve to institutional comparative advantage.

**ETSI GS QKD 015/018 and ITU-T Y.3805 on SDN control of QKDN.** Both ETSI and ITU-T published SDN-related specifications for QKDN in the 2020–2022 window. ETSI GS QKD 015 (V2.1.1, 2022) specifies the SDN control interface[^17], GS QKD 018 (V1.1.1, 2022) specifies the SDN orchestration interface[^17], and ITU-T Y.3805 specifies the network-architecture-level requirements and functional architecture of SDN control. The two are complementary specifications operating at different tiers, coordinated through the SG11 work-item liaison declaration on Q.QKDN_Cq that lists "ITU-T Q6&Q16/13, ITU-T Q15/17, ETSI ISG-QKD, ISO/IEC JTC1-SC27"[^17].

**ETSI ISG QKD security characterization and ITU-T SG17 security framework.** ETSI's GS QKD 016 supplies a certifiable Common Criteria target for formal third-party evaluation, while ITU-T's X.1710-series supplies the network-level security-requirements framework. The two artefacts serve different stakeholders and are coordinated through SG17 work-item liaison declarations including that on X.sec_QKD_profr listing "SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD"[^17].

**IRTF QIRG and ITU-T FG-QIT4N on quantum-networking landscape.** FG-QIT4N's D1-series addresses **network aspects of QIT** with a view to ITU-T standardisation; QIRG's drafts address **the architectural principles and application scenarios of the quantum internet itself**. The two are upstream-and-downstream complementary, not duplicative.

**IEEE P7130 and ISO/IEC 4879 on quantum technologies terminology.** Both projects address quantum-domain terminology, but at distinct authority tiers (engineering-society vs. formal-IS) and with distinct intended uses. IEEE's working-group has produced "an initial draft of over 60 pages" without converging to SA ballot at end-2022[^17], while ISO/IEC 4879 cleared CD ballot in April 2022—the two are operating on different procedural cadences toward complementary deliverable types.

In each apparent overlap, what looks at first reading like duplication resolves on close inspection into a clean division of labour by **institutional comparative advantage**.

### 8.4.3 Formal Coordination Mechanisms: JCA-QKDN and Liaison Declarations

The architectural coherence of the multi-SDO landscape is maintained through two formal coordination mechanisms.

The first is **the system of liaison declarations attached to individual work items**. The SG17 work item X.sec_QKD_profr lists liaison with "SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD"[^17]; the SG11 work item Q.QKDN_Cq lists liaison with "ITU-T Q6&Q16/13, ITU-T Q15/17, ETSI ISG-QKD, ISO/IEC JTC1-SC27"[^17].

The second, and more consolidated, mechanism is the **ITU-T Joint Coordination Activity on Quantum Key Distribution Network (JCA-QKDN)**. Its outgoing liaison statements provide the most comprehensive snapshot available of the institutional network. The second-meeting incoming liaison reference (JCA-QKDN-LS2 of 8 June 2023) was distributed for information to **ISO/IEC JTC1/SC27, IEEE SA, IEC TC 86, ETSI TC CYBER, ETSI ISG QKD, IRTF QIRG, CEN/CENELEC FG QT, ISO/IEC JTC1/WG14, ITU-T SG11, SG13, SG15, SG17, CEN/CLC/JTC 22, CCSA ST7, IEC SEG 14, and QED-C**[^11]. The corresponding incoming liaison statement from ITU-T SG13 (SG13-LS136, 3 November 2023) was sent for information to **ISO/IEC JTC1/SC27, ETSI ISG QKD, ISO/IEC JTC1/AG4, IRTF QIRG, ISO/IEC JTC1/WG14, IETF opsawg, ITU-T SG2, SG11, SG15, SG17, and JCA-QKDN**[^11]. JCA-QKDN-LS5 of 17 May 2024 reporting highlights from the fourth meeting was distributed for information to **IEEE; ISO/IEC JTC1/SC27; IEC TC 86; GSMA IG; ETSI ISG QKD; ETSI TC Cyber WG QSC; IRTF QIRG; ISO/IEC JTC1/WG14; ITU-T SG11, SG13, SG15, SG17; CEN/CLC/JTC 22; CCSA ST7; QED-C; GSMA PQTN; and ISO/IEC JTC3**[^39].

Although these LS dates are formally just outside the strict end-2022 cutoff window, the JCA-QKDN coordination network they document is the institutionally settled state into which the end-2022 cross-SDO landscape was crystallising. Three observations follow.

**First, the JCA-QKDN liaison network spans all five SDOs surveyed in this report.** ETSI is represented through both ISG QKD and TC CYBER (later TC CYBER WG QSC); ITU-T through its own Study Groups SG11, SG13, SG15, and SG17; ISO/IEC through JTC1/SC27, JTC1/WG14, and JTC3; IEEE through IEEE SA; and IRTF through QIRG. JCA-QKDN is therefore the **most consolidated single multi-SDO coordination instrument** in the quantum-domain landscape at the boundary of the cutoff.

**Second, the JCA-QKDN network extends beyond the five SDOs surveyed to a wider ecosystem.** The presence of CEN/CENELEC FG QT and CEN/CLC/JTC 22, IEC TC 86, IEC SEG 14, CCSA ST7, GSMA IG and PQTN, and QED-C evidences the wider regional and industrial coordination layer. This wider ecosystem is referenced for context; cataloguing its individual deliverables is outside the report's five-SDO scope.

**Third, the JCA-QKDN operates as an information-coordination instrument rather than as a normative consolidation mechanism.** Its liaison statements report progress and surface coordination items but do not produce or approve normative deliverables. The architectural coherence of the multi-SDO landscape therefore rests on **distributed coordination through liaison statements and JCA-QKDN information exchange**, rather than on any single consolidating authority.

The effectiveness of the existing coordination infrastructure at end-2022 is **operationally effective for the QKD/QKDN domain that is its principal substantive focus**. It is less consolidated for the **quantum-computing and PQC domains**: the cross-SDO coordination for quantum computing relies more heavily on the bilateral relationship between ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 and IEEE's working groups, with IEEE's internal Quantum Groups Linkup (QGL) operating as an informal lateral coordination forum within IEEE[^17], and the coordination for PQC relies on the more distributed multi-SDO ecosystem anchored upstream by NIST.

## 8.5 Constituency, Pace, and Institutional-Heritage Comparison

Reading the cross-SDO portfolio along Dimensions 4 and 5 surfaces a third-order structural pattern: the **observed division of labour and the residual gaps are themselves explainable as direct consequences of the constituency-and-heritage profiles of the five SDOs**.

The constituency profiles are summarised in Table 8.4.

| SDO | Constituency Profile | Heritage |
|---|---|---|
| ETSI | "More than 800 organisations […], covering 64 countries"[^17] in ETSI overall; ISG QKD founding base of European telecommunications operators, equipment vendors, research laboratories, and quantum-specialist firms | European telecommunications standardization; FP6 SECOQC project handover; longest sustained quantum-standardisation continuity from 2008 |
| ITU-T | UN treaty-context: national administrations and recognized operating agencies; multi-Question structure across SG11/SG13/SG15/SG17 plus FG-QIT4N | UN-level standardization; A.8-2022 AAP/TAP procedural framework[^11] |
| ISO/IEC | National-body delegation under JTC 1; sustained Chinese leadership on the ISO/IEC 23837 project (CNITSEC + QuantumCTek co-initiators, 2017–2022) and on JTC 1/WG 14 convenership; multilateral participation across approximately ten national bodies on WG 14 at the Sept 2022 meeting | Formal international standardization through ISO/IEC Directives, Part 1, 2022[^10]; multi-year NWIP-to-IS horizon |
| IEEE | Engineering-society individuals and corporate participants; C/SAB SC sponsorship; QGL informal lateral coordination[^17] | Engineering professional standardization; SA Standards Board Bylaws governance[^8]; four-year PAR lifetime; 75% Standards Board approval threshold |
| IRTF | Academic research community individuals; "by individual contributors, rather than by representatives of organizations"[^9]; co-chair affiliations at TU Delft and Keio | Research-oriented complement to IETF; RFC 7418 governance[^9]; three-to-five-year RG horizon[^9]; non-Standards-Track Informational RFC as principal output[^9] |

The institutional-heritage analysis explains five observed features.

**Feature 1.** The long ISG QKD trajectory from 2008 reflects a **vendor-driven specification culture**. The vertical accumulation of GS QKD 002/004/008/011/012/014/015/018 and GR QKD 003/005/007 over fourteen years is the standardization signature of such a constituency: incremental, specification-driven, layered from physical components through application interfaces to SDN control and Common Criteria Protection Profile.

**Feature 2.** ITU-T's rapid Y.3800-series consolidation from 2019–2022 reflects **study-group-level architectural convergence**. The pace is enabled by ITU-T's Question-level work-item allocation, by the A.8-2022 AAP four-week Last Call procedure for approval[^11], and by the upstream conceptual base supplied by FG-QIT4N's pre-standardization landscape work.

**Feature 3.** ISO/IEC's multi-year NWIP-to-IS horizon reflects the formal IS process[^10]. The 23837 project's drafting timeline—initiated November 2017, multilateral comment resolution with UK, Singapore, Luxembourg, and other national-body participation, publication-preparation announcement October 2022—evidences the multi-year national-body ballot horizon.

**Feature 4.** IEEE's project-by-project PAR cadence reflects the **four-year PAR lifetime constraint** codified in the SA Standards Board Bylaws[^8]. The July 2022 PAR extension request for P7131[^17] is the canonical manifestation. The uniform Active-PAR status of IEEE's quantum-domain portfolio at end-2022 is the operational signature of this cadence.

**Feature 5.** IRTF's three-to-five-year research-group horizon reflects the structural difference between research and engineering codified in RFC 7418: "the effort required by a RG is likely to take at least three to five years, significantly longer than IETF WGs envision when they are chartered"[^9]. The QIRG's transition from proposed RG (IETF 101, March 2018) to full RG (March 2020) and through to flagship-draft RG-Draft (active) status at the IETF 113 Vienna meeting of March 2022 is a four-year trajectory consistent with this horizon.

The constituency-and-pace analysis therefore reveals that **the observed division of labour is not contingent but structurally rooted in the heritage and constituency profiles of the five SDOs**. The cross-SDO architecture is, viewed through this lens, a **stable institutional equilibrium** rather than a contingent allocation. The residual gaps—particularly in quantum sensing/metrology, in standalone QRNG, and in the pre-publication status of much of IEEE's portfolio—are themselves structurally rooted in the absence of an SDO whose constituency profile and institutional heritage are well matched to those domains.

## 8.6 Consolidated Deliverables Catalogue Organized by SDO and Working Group

The consolidated catalogue presented in this section is the report's reference synthesis, directly responsive to the user's enumeration requirement. It is organized hierarchically: first by SDO, and within each SDO by responsible sub-unit. Each entry records the document identifier, full title, deliverable type, end-2022 status using the uniform labels of Chapter 2, key milestone or date, taxonomy domain assignment, and a brief scope description. Items at boundary-of-cutoff status or with limited primary-source confirmation are flagged ⚠ consistent with the methodology of Chapter 2.

### 8.6.1 ETSI

**ETSI Industry Specification Group on Quantum Key Distribution (ISG QKD).** Established 2008 as direct institutional outgrowth of the EC FP6-project SECOQC; mandate covers specification of QKD systems, components, interfaces, and security characterization.

| Document | Full Title | Type | Status (end-2022) | Version / Date | Domain | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ETSI GS QKD 002 | QKD use cases and security requirements ⚠ | GS | Published (within historical ISG family) ⚠ | Published earlier in ISG history | QKD | Use cases and security requirements for different user groups |
| ETSI GS QKD 004 | Quantum Key Distribution (QKD); Application Interface | GS | Published | V2.1.1 (2020) | QKD | Object-based RFC-style API with QoS functionalities for key data streams |
| ETSI GS QKD 008 | QKD module specification ⚠ | GS | Published (within historical ISG family) ⚠ | Published earlier in ISG history | QKD | QKD module functional specification |
| ETSI GS QKD 011 | QKD components and internal interfaces ⚠ | GS | Published (within historical ISG family) ⚠ | Published earlier in ISG history | QKD | Components and internal interfaces |
