## 8.7 Synthesis: The Global Quantum Standardization Architecture as of End-2022

Reading §§8.1–8.6 together yields an integrated characterization of the global quantum standardization architecture as of 31 December 2022 that serves as the analytical baseline for Chapter 9. The architecture exhibits four structural features that together define the state of the field at the cutoff.

**Structural feature 1 — A layered, liaison-coordinated, multi-SDO system anchored in QKD/QKDN.** The most consolidated portion of the global landscape at end-2022 is the QKD/QKDN domain, in which four of the five surveyed SDOs (ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE) contribute item-level standardization at distinct authority tiers, and the fifth (IRTF) contributes upstream architectural research for the related but categorically distinct quantum-internet substrate. The architecture is layered—component and interface tier at ETSI, network architecture and security framework and interface protocols at ITU-T, formal evaluation methodology at ISO/IEC, application-layer protocol at IEEE—and the layers are operationally coordinated through liaison declarations attached to individual work items and through the multi-SDO JCA-QKDN that ITU-T maintains. The cumulative result is an end-to-end assurance architecture (component → certifiable target → network deployment → formal evaluation) operationally complete at end-2022.

**Structural feature 2 — Emerging multi-tiered architecture for quantum computing.** The quantum-computing domain is at an earlier stage of consolidation but exhibits the same multi-tier structural logic. ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14's ISO/IEC 4879 supplies the formal-IS terminology anchor (CD-passed April 2022), and IEEE's quantum-computing portfolio (P7130, P7131, P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3329, P3185, P2995) supplies the engineering-society-tier specifications across terminology, benchmarking, architecture, simulator design and programming, energy efficiency, hybrid integration, and algorithm design. The architecture is procedurally less advanced: ISO/IEC 4879's CD-passage gives the terminology anchor an intermediate ballot stage at the cutoff, and IEEE's portfolio is uniformly at Active PAR (Under Development) stage with no item yet at Active (Approved Standard).

**Structural feature 3 — Distributed PQC architecture anchored upstream by NIST.** The PQC domain exhibits a horizontal architecture rather than a vertical multi-tier architecture: NIST's algorithm selections (CRYSTALS-Kyber, CRYSTALS-Dilithium, Falcon, SPHINCS+) anchor the upstream layer; ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2's 14888-4 stateful hash-based signature and 18033-2 PQ-KEM tracks supply the algorithm-standardization channel; ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC's TS 103 744 supplies the protocol-level hybrid-key-exchange specification[^19]; the parallel IETF/IRTF ecosystem (PQUIP, CFRG, TLS, LAMPS) supplies the protocol-integration layer[^17]; and IEEE's P3172 Recommended Practice supplies the engineering-society migration guidance. The architecture is more distributed than QKD/QKDN—reflecting that PQC is fundamentally classical cryptography embedded in the broader cryptographic-protocols ecosystem rather than a quantum-network-specific technology requiring dedicated cross-SDO coordination. Its convergence to published normative outputs at the algorithm-specification tier is structurally dependent on the post-cutoff NIST FIPS finalization.

**Structural feature 4 — Single-venue pre-standardization research for the quantum internet, with thin coverage of QRNG and sensing/metrology.** The quantum-internet domain is addressed at item-level deliverable specification by IRTF QIRG alone, with two flagship Internet-Drafts at RG Draft (approaching Last Call) stage at end-2022 and no Informational RFC yet published within the evidence window. The QRNG and quantum sensing/metrology domains are the most thinly populated across the entire five-SDO landscape: QRNG is addressed only as a functional component within QKD module specifications and the 23837 evaluation context; quantum sensing/metrology is addressed only through IEC SEG 14's horizontal mapping ⚠ and as a landscape topic by FG-QIT4N pillar 4, with no surveyed SDO sustaining a dedicated specification programme.

Read together, these four features define a global quantum standardization architecture that is, **at the QKD/QKDN domain, layered and operationally complete**; **at the quantum-computing domain, multi-tiered but pre-publication**; **at the PQC domain, distributed and dependent on post-cutoff upstream algorithm finalization**; **at the quantum-internet domain, anchored in a single research venue at pre-standardization stage**; and **at the QRNG and sensing/metrology domains, structurally thin**. The structural strengths of the architecture—clean complementarity in QKD/QKDN, established coordination via JCA-QKDN, emerging terminology consensus for quantum computing—and the structural weaknesses—thin coverage of quantum sensing, QRNG, and end-to-end quantum internet protocols; pre-publication status of much of IEEE's portfolio; dependence on post-cutoff NIST PQC finalization—together form the analytical baseline against which Chapter 9 will develop the gap analysis, examine the coordination challenges, and characterize the forward trajectory of the global quantum standardization landscape.

# 9 Gaps, Challenges, Coordination Issues, and Conclusions

The preceding eight chapters have, layer by layer, reconstructed the global quantum standardization landscape as it stood on 31 December 2022: the strategic motivation and taxonomic frame (Chapter 1), the methodology by which deliverables were inventoried and normalized (Chapter 2), the per-organization catalogues for ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and IRTF (Chapters 3–7), and the cross-organizational synthesis that placed those catalogues into an integrated four-tiered architecture (Chapter 8). This concluding chapter converts that empirical and analytical accumulation into a final diagnostic and prospective synthesis. It does not introduce new evidence beyond the end-2022 cutoff; rather, it interrogates the architecture already documented in order to (i) identify where standardization coverage is structurally thin or absent, (ii) diagnose the technical and institutional challenges that constrain the pace and depth of the work, (iii) assess the effectiveness and limits of the observed coordination mechanisms, (iv) distil the comparative roles of the five SDOs into a consolidated institutional characterization, (v) describe the directional trajectory visible at the cutoff, and (vi) offer reasoned reflections on near-term priorities and on how the principal stakeholder communities can navigate the resulting multi-organization landscape. §9.7 closes the report with a final assessment of the state of the global quantum standardization landscape as of end-2022.

## 9.1 Structural Gaps in the End-2022 Quantum Standardization Coverage

Read against the six-domain taxonomy of Chapter 1, the per-organization catalogues of Chapters 3–7 and the cross-organizational coverage matrix of Chapter 8 reveal a structurally uneven distribution of standardization coverage. The unevenness is not a contingent omission—an artefact of which projects happened to be initiated by the cutoff—but a structural feature whose causes are traceable to institutional remit, technology maturity, and upstream dependencies. Four categories of gap warrant separate identification.

**Category (i) — Entirely uncovered domains.** Two of the six taxonomy domains receive **no item-level dedicated normative specification** across any of the five surveyed SDOs at end-2022. The first is **standalone quantum random number generation (QRNG)**. Across the consolidated catalogue of Chapter 8, QRNG functions appear only as functional components within QKD module specifications—addressed indirectly within ETSI ISG QKD's component-and-module work and within the QKD module evaluation context of ISO/IEC 23837. No surveyed SDO sustains a dedicated standardization programme for QRNG as a standalone cryptographic-component category, and the evaluation of standalone QRNG products used independently of QKD systems to seed classical cryptographic operations is, at end-2022, left to ISO/IEC SC 27's broader entropy-testing infrastructure and to national or regional certification bodies whose work falls outside the five-SDO scope. The second is **quantum sensing and metrology**. The Chapter 8 coverage matrix records this domain as touched only at landscape level by FG-QIT4N's pillar 4 ("Quantum Sensing & Metrology") and by IEC SEG 14's horizontal mapping function ⚠, with no published Recommendations, International Standards, Group Specifications, or PARs addressing it at item-level specification across ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC JTC 1, IEEE, or IRTF.

Both gaps are structurally rooted. The QRNG gap reflects the absence of an SDO whose institutional remit is centred on entropy-source standardization as a distinct cryptographic-component category, rather than as an embedded element of a larger system. The quantum sensing and metrology gap reflects the absence of any of the five surveyed SDOs from the metrological-traceability and physical-measurement standardization domain that is more natively the territory of BIPM and of horizontal IEC coordination bodies. ETSI and ITU-T are telecommunications standards bodies whose institutional identity does not encompass sensing and metrology; ISO/IEC JTC 1's vertical structure is information-technology focused and does not natively house the metrological work, with the IEC SEG 14 horizontal mapping function the only documented institutional touch-point at the cutoff; IEEE's quantum portfolio under C/SAB SC sponsorship is quantum-computing centred; IRTF's QIRG addresses quantum sensing only as one of three motivating application categories ("geographically distributed clocks and enhanced resolution for telescopes using long-baseline interferometry") rather than as a standardization domain in its own right.

**Category (ii) — Pre-publication domains where substantive scope exists but no normative output had emerged by the cutoff.** Three structurally significant regions of the landscape have active project scope but no published normative content at end-2022. **IEEE's quantum-computing portfolio**—P7130, P7131, P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3329, P3185, P2995—is uniformly at Active PAR (Under Development) stage, with no item having completed SA Standards Board approval by the cutoff. **IRTF QIRG's flagship Internet-Drafts** (draft-irtf-qirg-principles and draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases) are at RG Draft (active) stage approaching IRSG Last Call, with no Informational RFC yet published within the evidence window. **Dedicated PQC algorithm International Standards** within ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 await the post-cutoff NIST FIPS 203/204/205 finalization for the algorithm specifications they would absorb; ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM track and ISO/IEC 14888-4 are both active but not at published-IS stage at end-2022. Each of these pre-publication regions is structurally explainable: IEEE's uniform PAR-stage status reflects the four-year PAR lifetime cadence codified in the SA Standards Board Bylaws and the field-wide pre-commercial maturity of underlying quantum-computing hardware; IRTF's pre-publication status reflects the three-to-five-year RG horizon codified in RFC 7418 and the fundamentally non-Standards-Track character of IRTF outputs; the PQC tier 1 publication gap reflects upstream dependency on NIST's algorithm finalization, itself a post-cutoff event with August 2023 draft FIPS publications and 2024 finalization.

**Category (iii) — Partially addressed sub-domains within otherwise covered areas.** Within the QKD/QKDN domain, where Chapter 8 documented operational completeness of the layered assurance chain, four sub-domains remain incomplete at the cutoff. **QKDN integration with non-quantum cryptography** for mobile and quantum-channel-unreachable endpoints is foreshadowed in ITU-T's post-cutoff Supplement 79 to the Y.3800-series but is not normatively specified at end-2022; as Chapter 4 recorded, no ITU-T deliverable at the cutoff specifies the integration of QKDN with PQC or with classical PKI-based key exchange to address mobile-object and similar quantum-channel-limited scenarios. **Inter-domain (cross-operator) key distribution** is in scope as the Y.QKDN-iwfr work item at ITU-T Q16/13 and as a cross-domain key distribution Work Item at ETSI ISG QKD, with both at Under Development stage at the cutoff and neither yet converged to approved normative specification. **Observability and monitoring interfaces** are addressed by ETSI ISG QKD's monitoring interface Work Item, with formal Work Item chartering recorded in December 2023 and the project at active drafting status at the cutoff. The **quantum-layer QKD protocol normative framework** (X.sec_QKD_profr at ITU-T SG17) was at most initiated at the boundary of the cutoff, with its earliest TD documentation appearing in February 2023 and its drafting following directly from the FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1 analytical recommendation; this leaves a structural lacuna at the cutoff between the informative D2.3 Part 1 analytical content and the formal Common Criteria certification track anchored by ETSI GS QKD 016.

**Category (iv) — Standardization-methodology gaps.** Two methodological gaps cut horizontally across the field. The first is the **absence of approved benchmarking standards for quantum computing performance**. IEEE P7131's scope—"performance metrics for standardizing performance benchmarking of quantum computing hardware and software", covering quantum-versus-quantum and quantum-versus-classical comparisons with methodologies "that accounts for factors such as dedicated solvers"—articulates exactly the benchmarking infrastructure that the field requires, but the project is at Active PAR (Under Development) stage with a PAR extension request tabled at the 26 July 2022 C/SAB SC meeting for the September 2022 NesCom consideration. The second methodological gap is the **difficulty of defining numerical performance thresholds across heterogeneous quantum platforms**. The benchmarking literature reviewed in §9.2 below records that "the heterogeneity of quantum platforms reinforces the difficulty of defining universal numerical thresholds for performance acceptability" and that "at the present stage of quantum hardware development, a guideline-based framework provides a more robust approach, allowing benchmarks and metrics to be evaluated in context without imposing premature numerical standardization"[^41]. This insight is operationally important for the field's standardization trajectory: it explains why IEEE P7131 has not converged rapidly to a single metric, why ISO/IEC 4879 prioritizes terminology over performance metrics, and why a "roadmap towards standardizing the performance evaluation of quantum devices, ultimately leading to the creation of an organization akin to the Standard Performance Evaluation Corporation (SPEC)"[^41] remains a research-community aspiration rather than an SDO-converged deliverable at end-2022.

Table 9.1 consolidates the four categories of gap.

| Gap Category | Specific Manifestations at End-2022 | Structural Cause |
|---|---|---|
| (i) Entirely uncovered domains | Standalone QRNG; quantum sensing and metrology | Absence of an SDO whose institutional remit centres on these domains; metrological traceability natively allocated to BIPM and horizontal IEC bodies outside the five-SDO scope |
| (ii) Pre-publication domains | IEEE quantum-computing portfolio (P7130/P7131/P3120/P3120.1/P3155/P3329/P3185/P2995); IRTF QIRG flagship drafts; ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 PQC algorithm IS tracks | IEEE PAR lifecycle cadence; IRTF research-horizon cadence; upstream dependency on post-cutoff NIST FIPS finalization |
| (iii) Partially addressed sub-domains | QKDN integration with non-quantum cryptography; inter-domain (cross-operator) key distribution; observability/monitoring interfaces; quantum-layer QKD protocol normative framework (X.sec_QKD_profr) | Operational extension of mature QKD/QKDN core; pending under-development pipeline at ETSI and ITU-T |
| (iv) Standardization-methodology gaps | Approved quantum-computing benchmarking standards; numerical performance thresholds across heterogeneous platforms | Field-wide pre-commercial maturity of quantum-computing hardware; structural difficulty of cross-platform comparison documented in the benchmarking literature[^41] |

Two interpretive points close this section. First, no gap in any of the four categories is **a failure of effort**; each is a structural reflection of the institutional and technological state of the field at the cutoff. Second, the four categories impose **different priorities for resolution**: Category (i) gaps require new institutional venues or expanded scope within existing SDOs; Category (ii) gaps will resolve through the natural maturation of existing work programmes; Category (iii) gaps are within the established work programmes' pipelines and require sustained drafting effort; Category (iv) gaps require methodological consensus that no single SDO can supply unilaterally. This taxonomy of gaps directly structures the prioritization reflections of §9.6.

## 9.2 Technical and Institutional Challenges Shaping the Standardization Effort

The structural gaps documented in §9.1 are themselves shaped by a set of underlying challenges that constrain the pace and depth of quantum standardization at end-2022. Five challenge clusters can be distinguished, each of which the preceding chapters surfaced individually and which together explain why the field's standardization architecture exhibits the maturity profile recorded in Chapter 8.

**Cluster (i) — Immature underlying technology.** The most pervasive constraint is the **pre-commercial heterogeneity of the underlying quantum hardware**, particularly in the quantum-computing domain. The benchmarking literature characterizes the present stage as one in which "quantum platforms differ in several aspects, including the underlying physical technologies used to implement qubits, the set of native quantum operations they support, and the topology of the quantum hardware", with the consequence that "performance-related properties such as gate execution times, qubit connectivity, and dominant error mechanisms exhibit differences across platforms, challenging the use of numerical thresholds to define performance acceptability"[^41]. The literature further notes that "gate execution times span several orders of magnitude across platforms, ranging from microseconds to milliseconds, depending on the underlying physical technology", and that "quantum platforms differ in the native gate sets they support, ranging from architectures limited to one- and two-qubit gates to platforms enabling native multi-qubit operations"[^41]. This heterogeneity has direct standardization consequences: it precludes premature rigid numerical benchmarking standards (the Category (iv) gap of §9.1), and it complicates terminology stabilization, as illustrated empirically by the drafting trajectories of both IEEE P7130 (which reached a 60-page initial draft without converging to SA ballot by the cutoff) and ISO/IEC 4879 (which cleared CD ballot on 27 April 2022 but continued in post-CD comment resolution through the September 2022 WG 14 meeting). The same hardware-maturity constraint explains why QIRG operates at the IRTF research tier rather than at IETF as a Working Group: the protocol questions QIRG addresses concern technology that is "now nearly ready for the first prototype demonstrations" rather than at engineering-deployment maturity.

The technology-maturity constraint also shapes the quantum-internet domain's standardization profile. As reconstructed in Chapter 7, the quantum repeater—"the key ingredient for long-distance quantum communication"—is at the stage of "first prototype demonstrations" at the cutoff, with small-scale demo networks being built in the Netherlands, the United States, Japan, and China. Standardization at this stage of hardware maturity is necessarily upstream and architectural rather than engineering-protocol-specific, which is precisely the role that QIRG performs through its two flagship Internet-Drafts. The maturity profile of quantum-internet hardware therefore explains both the single-venue character of the quantum-internet domain's coverage (Chapter 8 pattern 4) and the non-normative nature of its outputs at the cutoff.

**Cluster (ii) — Vendor interoperability challenges in QKD.** The QKD/QKDN domain, despite its layered operational completeness at end-2022, faces persistent interoperability challenges that motivate substantial portions of its under-development pipeline. ETSI's dual API approach—GS QKD 004 (object-style remote-function-call API with QoS functionalities) and GS QKD 014 (REST/HTTPS/JSON key delivery API)—reflects a deliberate accommodation of two distinct integrator constituencies, as Chapter 3 recorded: "A REST (REpresentational State Transfer) API is specified as a simple, scalable, widely deployed approach that is familiar to a large developer community… It is hoped that this REST-based API specification for key delivery can encourage new entrants/developers into the QKD market". ITU-T's per-reference-point protocol work in SG11's Q.QKDN-series similarly addresses the protocol-level interoperability that operator-grade QKDN deployment requires.

Two interoperability frontiers remain incomplete at the cutoff. The **cross-domain key distribution** challenge addresses how keys generated within one trusted-node QKD network domain are securely exchanged with another administrative domain, raising both technical and policy questions that the under-development ETSI Work Item and the ITU-T Y.QKDN-iwfr Work Item are designed to address. The **inter-QKDN interworking framework** addresses interworking between QKDNs "supported by multiple QKDN providers" through Gateway Functions and Interworking Functions for large-scale QKDN composed of multiple sub-QKDNs. Both are structurally consistent with the maturation of QKD from point-to-point links through trusted-node networks toward operator-grade multi-domain deployment; both remain at Under Development stage at end-2022.

**Cluster (iii) — Security certification methodologies.** The QKD assurance chain documented in Chapter 8 is operationally effective at end-2022 but its **operational completeness is contingent on the post-cutoff publication of ISO/IEC 23837 and the post-cutoff finalization of X.sec_QKD_profr**. At the cutoff, the chain operates as follows: ETSI's component-and-module GS family specifies the artefacts being evaluated; ETSI's GS QKD 016 (at the publication boundary at the cutoff) provides the Common Criteria Protection Profile target; ITU-T's X.1710-series supplies the network-level security framework with X.1712, X.1714, and X.1715 providing element-level security requirements; and ISO/IEC 23837-1/-2 (at publication-preparation phase entered October 2022) supplies the formal international test and evaluation methodology. The chain's full operational completeness requires both ISO/IEC 23837's formal IS publication and the formalization of X.sec_QKD_profr as the normative bridge between FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1's informative analytical content and QKD module certification. At end-2022, the chain is in the final stages of crystallization rather than in fully published-and-finalized form.

A second certification challenge is the **heterogeneity of national certification regimes** under which the Common Criteria evaluation methodology must be operationalized. ISO/IEC 23837 provides the methodologically portable evaluation specification, but national certification authorities adopt and apply that methodology with national-specific procedural overlays. The cross-border portability of QKD module certifications therefore depends not only on the formal IS publication of 23837 but on the operational alignment of national certification regimes around the methodology, a coordination challenge that lies outside the scope of any of the surveyed SDOs.

**Cluster (iv) — Harmonization across SDOs with structurally distinct lifecycles.** The four authority tiers documented in Chapter 8 (formal IS at ISO/IEC, treaty-context Recommendation at ITU-T, industry/engineering-society standard at ETSI and IEEE, pre-standardization research at IRTF) are anchored in procedurally distinct lifecycles. ISO/IEC's multi-year NWIP→CD→DIS→FDIS→IS progression operates on national-body ballot cadence; ITU-T's AAP four-week Last Call (or TAP under WTSA Resolution 1) operates on Study Group consent followed by treaty-context approval; ETSI's ISG consensus model and TC process operate on consensus within Industry Specification Group or Technical Committee membership; IEEE's PAR four-year lifetime and 75% SA Standards Board approval threshold operate on engineering-society professional standardization cadence; and IRTF's three-to-five-year RG horizon with non-Standards-Track Informational RFC outputs operates on the categorically distinct research tier.

Harmonizing across these lifecycles is not impossible—Chapter 8 documented the operational effectiveness of the layered QKD/QKDN architecture and the multi-tier coordination through JCA-QKDN—but it imposes a coordination overhead that grows with the breadth of the cross-SDO work. The same item, when treated by SDOs at different tiers, may exist simultaneously at substantively different procedural stages: GS QKD 016 at the ETSI publication boundary, ISO/IEC 23837 at publication-preparation phase, and X.sec_QKD_profr at initiation, for instance, all address aspects of the same underlying QKD module evaluation question at different cadences. The harmonization challenge is therefore not a contingent inefficiency but a structural consequence of the multi-tier architecture.

**Cluster (v) — Upstream-dependency challenge in PQC.** The PQC domain at end-2022 exhibits the most acute single-source upstream dependency in the global quantum standardization architecture. As recorded in Chapter 1, NIST announced in July 2022 the first four PQC algorithms selected for standardization—CRYSTALS-Kyber as the first Key Encapsulation Mechanism, and CRYSTALS-Dilithium, Falcon, and SPHINCS+ as digital signatures—but the formal FIPS publication of the corresponding standards (FIPS 203/204/205) is a post-cutoff event with August 2023 draft FIPS publication and 2024 finalization. The downstream PQC standardization architecture—ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM track absorbing FrodoKEM, Classic McEliece, and ML-KEM; ISO/IEC 14888-4 stateful hash-based signatures; ETSI TS 103 744 hybrid key exchanges; IEEE P3172 Recommended Practice for PQC migration; IEEE P1943 post-quantum network security ⚠—is structurally positioned to absorb the NIST selections into protocol and migration deliverables but cannot complete that absorption until the upstream FIPS finalization occurs. The PQC domain's convergence to published normative outputs at the algorithm-specification tier is therefore not a question of SDO drafting effort but of post-cutoff upstream finalization.

These five challenge clusters together explain the structural unevenness of the global quantum standardization coverage documented in Chapter 8 and §9.1. The clusters are interconnected: the technology-maturity constraint (cluster i) shapes both the benchmarking methodology gap (§9.1 category iv) and the pre-publication status of much of IEEE's portfolio (§9.1 category ii); the certification-methodology challenge (cluster iii) is operationally entangled with the harmonization challenge (cluster iv) through the cross-SDO assurance chain; the PQC upstream-dependency challenge (cluster v) directly determines the pace of downstream convergence across multiple SDOs. The challenges are not amenable to resolution by any single SDO acting alone; they collectively motivate the coordination mechanisms examined in §9.3.

## 9.3 Coordination Mechanisms: Observed Practice and Limitations

The five challenge clusters of §9.2, particularly the harmonization challenge of cluster (iv), make the operational effectiveness of cross-SDO coordination mechanisms a load-bearing feature of the global quantum standardization architecture. Chapter 8 documented three coordination instruments operating at the boundary of the cutoff; this section assesses their effectiveness and limits.

**Instrument (i) — Per-item liaison declarations.** The most distributed coordination instrument is the system of liaison declarations attached to individual work items. The SG17 work item X.sec_QKD_profr's declared liaison set—"SG11, SG13, ISO/IEC JTC1 SC27 WG3, ETSI ISG-QKD"—is the canonical example, operationalizing the four-vertex coordination required for the QKD protocol framework that bridges informative FG-QIT4N analysis to formal Common Criteria certification. The SG11 work item Q.QKDN_Cq's parallel declaration—"ITU-T Q6&Q16/13, ITU-T Q15/17, ETSI ISG-QKD, ISO/IEC JTC1-SC27"—operationalizes the analogous coordination for the Cq-interface protocol between QKDN controllers and QKD modules. Per-item liaison declarations have two structural strengths: they are **fine-grained**, allowing coordination to be specified at the precision of the substantive scope of the individual work item; and they are **bidirectional**, with both the originating committee and the declared liaison partners undertaking to track each other's progress.

Per-item liaison declarations have two corresponding limits. They **produce no normative consolidation**—the liaison partners track each other's progress but do not jointly approve or constrain the deliverables that emerge. And they are **uneven in their coverage**: not every work item in any of the surveyed SDOs declares liaison with the relevant external counterparts, and the absence of a liaison declaration is not necessarily an indicator of lack of coordination but does mean that the cross-SDO awareness for that item is not formally instrumented. The per-item liaison mechanism is therefore best understood as the **distributed substrate** of cross-SDO coordination, on top of which more consolidated instruments operate.

**Instrument (ii) — ITU-T Joint Coordination Activity on QKDN (JCA-QKDN).** The most consolidated cross-SDO coordination instrument in the quantum domain at the boundary of the cutoff is JCA-QKDN. As Chapter 8 documented, JCA-QKDN's liaison-statement network spans all five surveyed SDOs plus the wider ecosystem: outgoing JCA-QKDN-LS5 of 17 May 2024 was distributed for information to IEEE, ISO/IEC JTC1/SC27, IEC TC 86, GSMA IG, ETSI ISG QKD, ETSI TC Cyber WG QSC, IRTF QIRG, ISO/IEC JTC1/WG14, ITU-T SG11/SG13/SG15/SG17, CEN/CLC/JTC 22, CCSA ST7, QED-C, GSMA PQTN, and ISO/IEC JTC3. The breadth of the LS distribution list documents that JCA-QKDN operates as the **principal information-exchange hub** for the multi-SDO quantum coordination network.

JCA-QKDN's structural limits are equally important. It operates as **an information-exchange and coordination instrument rather than as a normative consolidation body**: its outputs are liaison statements reporting progress and surfacing coordination items, not jointly approved Recommendations or Standards. It is **scope-limited to QKDN**: by its title and substantive focus, it does not extend its consolidating function to quantum computing, PQC, or quantum-internet research, leaving those domains to other coordination instruments. And its **dates of operational activity** fall partly outside the strict end-2022 cutoff window—the LS5 reference of May 2024 documents the institutionally settled state into which the end-2022 landscape was crystallizing, but the JCA-QKDN coordination network at the cutoff itself is best characterized as in active consolidation rather than at maximum operational extension.

**Instrument (iii) — Informal lateral coordination forums.** Within IEEE, the Quantum Groups Linkup (QGL) operates as the canonical example of informal lateral coordination. As Chapter 6 recorded, the QGL is "an informal group of IEEE-SA working groups in the 'quantum' topic area, including chairs, members, and IEEE staff" that "discusses cross-cutting technical issues and IEEE-SA issues of common interest", with no formal procedural status. The QGL's structural strength is its **flexibility**: it sustains intra-SDO coordination across the IEEE quantum portfolio (P1913, P1943, P2995, P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3172, P3185, P3329, P7130, P7131) without the procedural overhead of a Subcommittee structure. Its structural limit is that **it has no analogous instrument operating across SDOs**: there is no cross-SDO QGL-equivalent that sustains lateral coordination across ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and IRTF in the quantum-computing or quantum-internet domains in the manner that JCA-QKDN sustains it for QKD/QKDN.

**Aggregate effectiveness and remaining coordination gaps.** Taken together, the three coordination instruments produce an architecture that is **operationally effective for QKD/QKDN, less consolidated for quantum computing, more distributed for PQC, and largely uninstrumented for quantum sensing/metrology and standalone QRNG**. Table 9.2 summarizes the coordination coverage by domain.

| Domain | Principal Coordination Instruments at End-2022 | Operational Effectiveness | Residual Limits |
|---|---|---|---|
| QKD & quantum communication | JCA-QKDN consolidated multi-SDO hub; per-item liaison declarations on X.sec_QKD_profr, Q.QKDN_Cq, and related work items | Highly effective; layered assurance chain operationally complete | JCA-QKDN is information-exchange rather than normative; some early-stage drafting in ISG QKD remains internally visible only |
| Quantum computing | Bilateral ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 – IEEE relationship; IEEE-internal QGL lateral coordination | Effective at the bilateral and intra-IEEE level | No analogous JCA-equivalent operating across the two SDOs at multi-tier scale |
| Post-quantum cryptography | Distributed coordination across NIST (upstream), ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2, ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC, IEEE PQC working groups, and the parallel IETF ecosystem (PQUIP, CFRG, TLS, LAMPS) | Effective as a distributed ecosystem | No single consolidating instrument; convergence dependent on NIST FIPS finalization |
| Quantum networking / quantum internet | QIRG's chartered coordination role with other SDOs; co-chair embeddedness in TU Delft and Keio quantum-networking research consortia | Effective as upstream research coordination | No standards-tier coordination instrument because the field is at pre-standardization stage |
| QRNG | Embedded within QKD module coordination context; no domain-specific instrument | Marginal | Domain-specific coordination instrument absent |
| Quantum sensing and metrology | IEC SEG 14 horizontal mapping ⚠; FG-QIT4N pillar 4 landscape work | Marginal | No SDO-specific coordination instrument; coordination natively the territory of metrological organizations outside the five-SDO scope |

The coordination architecture at end-2022 thus exhibits a **focused-effectiveness profile**: where the coordination need is most acute (QKD/QKDN, where four SDOs contribute item-level standardization at distinct authority tiers), the most consolidated coordination instrument (JCA-QKDN) operates; where the coordination need is bilateral (quantum computing, between ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 and IEEE), bilateral and intra-IEEE instruments operate; where the coordination need is broadest but most distributed (PQC), distributed ecosystem coordination operates anchored upstream by NIST; and where standardization itself is at pre-standardization stage (quantum internet), the coordination role is itself a pre-standardization research function. This focused-effectiveness profile is structurally appropriate to the state of the field at the cutoff, but it leaves the QRNG and quantum-sensing domains with no domain-specific coordination instrument, consistent with the Category (i) entirely-uncovered gap of §9.1.

## 9.4 Synthesis: Distinct yet Complementary Roles of the Five SDOs and the Integrated Architecture

The gap analysis of §9.1, the challenge diagnosis of §9.2, and the coordination assessment of §9.3 together support a consolidated characterization of each SDO's distinctive role in the global quantum standardization architecture as of end-2022. Chapter 8 reconstructed the architecture at the deliverable-and-tier level; this section distils that reconstruction into a final institutional characterization.

**ETSI** occupies the **vertically integrated industry-specification position** within the architecture. Its ISG QKD has produced, since its 2008 founding as a direct institutional outgrowth of the EC FP6-project SECOQC, the most complete end-to-end QKD specification stack of any single SDO surveyed: component and module specifications (GS QKD 002/008/011/012), vocabulary and informative reports (GR QKD 003/005/007), dual application interfaces (GS QKD 004 object-style API and GS QKD 014 REST/HTTPS/JSON key delivery API), SDN control and orchestration interfaces (GS QKD 015 and GS QKD 018, both 2022), and the Common Criteria Protection Profile for prepare-and-measure modules (GS QKD 016, at the publication boundary). In parallel, ETSI's TC CYBER Working Group on Quantum-Safe Cryptography supplies the classical-cryptographic complement to the QKD stack through TS 103 744 V1.1.1 (December 2020) on hybrid key exchanges and the accumulated TR series on quantum-safe primitives, use cases, and migration guidance. ETSI's role in the integrated architecture is therefore the supply of the **vendor-and-operator-oriented industry specification tier** at Tier 3 of the four-tier authority hierarchy, with its specifications providing both the components-and-interfaces base for the QKD assurance chain and the protocol-level template (hybrid key exchanges) that the broader PQC migration architecture absorbs.

**ITU-T** occupies the **treaty-context network-level Recommendation position**. Its SG13 has produced the most consolidated body of network-level QKD standardization of any SDO surveyed, organized as the Y.3800-series under the dedicated Y.3800–Y.3999 numbering block: the architecture-and-control core (Y.3800/3801/3802/3803/3804/3805, approved 2019–2020) and the QoS sub-series (Y.3806/3807/3811/3812, approved 2021–2022, with the most recent Y.3812 on ML-based QoS assurance approved September 2022). Its SG17 has produced the X.1710-series security framework with element-level Recommendations X.1712, X.1714, and X.1715, and has initiated the X.sec_QKD_profr work item that will provide the normative bridge between FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1's informative analytical content and the formal QKD module certification regime. Its SG11 has produced the Q.QKDN-series interface protocols for the per-reference-point classical-layer protocols (Q.QKDN_profr, Q.QKDN_AK, Q.QKDN_Kq-1, Q.QKDN_Kx, Q.QKDN_CK, Q.QKDN_Cq). Its Focus Group on Quantum Information Technology for Networks produced eleven D-series technical reports (D0.1, D1.1–D1.4, D2.1–D2.5) that established the pre-standardization conceptual base from which the SG-level Recommendations work programmes have grown. ITU-T's role in the integrated architecture is therefore the supply of the **treaty-context Recommendation tier** at Tier 2 of the authority hierarchy, with its Recommendations providing the operator-network architectural foundation upon which ETSI's component-and-interface specifications operationally rest.

**ISO/IEC** occupies the **formal International Standard tier**. Its JTC 1/SC 27/WG 3 has produced the two-part ISO/IEC 23837 series ("Information security — Security requirements, test and evaluation methods for quantum key distribution"), which entered the publication-preparation phase in October 2022 and formally publishes in 2023 as the apex of the QKD assurance chain. Its JTC 1/WG 14 has produced the foundational ISO/IEC 4879 quantum-computing terminology standard, which cleared CD ballot on 27 April 2022 and continued in post-CD comment resolution through the September 2022 WG 14 meeting, supplemented by ISO/IEC TR 18157 (under development) and PWIs 18660 and 18670 (preliminary work items on quantum machine learning datasets and quantum resource simulation platforms). Its JTC 1/SC 27/WG 2 maintains the algorithm-standardization tracks for PQC (ISO/IEC 14888-4 stateful hash-based signatures and ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM absorbing FrodoKEM, Classic McEliece, and ML-KEM). ISO/IEC's role in the integrated architecture is the supply of the **formal IS-tier evaluation methodology, terminology anchor, and algorithm standardization channel** at Tier 1, structurally complementing the lower-tier specifications of the other SDOs by providing the most formally authoritative evaluation, vocabulary, and algorithm specifications.

**IEEE** occupies the **engineering-society quantum-computing position**. Its C/SAB SC sponsorship and informal QGL coordination support the most comprehensive engineering-society quantum-computing portfolio of any SDO surveyed: terminology (P7130), benchmarking (P7131, with extension request in September 2022 NesCom cycle), architecture and simulator (P3120, P3120.1, P3155), energy efficiency (P3329), hybrid quantum-classical integration (P3185, placed on September 2022 NesCom agenda), and algorithm design (P2995 Trial-Use). Beyond quantum computing, IEEE supplies the application-layer Software-Defined Quantum Communication protocol (P1913), the PQC migration Recommended Practice (P3172), and the Post-Quantum Network Security Standard (P1943 ⚠). All projects in the IEEE portfolio are at Active PAR (Under Development) stage at the cutoff. IEEE's role in the integrated architecture is the supply of the **engineering-society-tier specifications across the quantum-computing engineering domain plus emerging application-layer quantum-communication and PQC-migration deliverables**, structurally layered atop ISO/IEC 4879's formal-IS terminology anchor and operating at Tier 3 of the authority hierarchy in complementary positioning to ETSI's industry-specification work.

**IRTF** occupies the **pre-standardization research position**. Its Quantum Internet Research Group, transitioned from proposed RG to full RG in March 2020 under co-chairs Wojciech Kozlowski (TU Delft) and Rodney Van Meter (Keio), addresses the long-term protocol-and-architecture challenges of end-to-end entanglement distribution that are categorically distinct from the QKDN focus of the telecommunications SDOs. Its two flagship Internet-Drafts (draft-irtf-qirg-principles and draft-irtf-qirg-quantum-internet-use-cases) were at RG Draft (active) stage approaching IRSG Last Call at the IETF 113 Vienna meeting of 22 March 2022 and are positioned to inform downstream IETF standardization should the underlying quantum-repeater hardware mature to engineering-deployment readiness. IRTF's role in the integrated architecture is the supply of the **pre-standardization research tier** at Tier 4, with its outputs available for downstream reference by any SDO that subsequently chooses to address the entanglement-distribution substrate at engineering-standardization tier.

Table 9.3 consolidates these institutional roles.

| SDO | Principal Position in the Architecture | Tier in Four-Tier Hierarchy | Centre-of-Gravity Domain |
|---|---|---|---|
| ETSI | Vertically integrated industry-specification stack: components → interfaces → SDN control → Protection Profile; plus PQC hybrid-protocol template | Tier 3 (Industry / Engineering-Society Standard) | QKD; secondary in PQC |
| ITU-T | Treaty-context network-level Recommendations: operator-network architecture, security framework, interface protocols, QoS | Tier 2 (Treaty-context Recommendation) | QKDN |
| ISO/IEC | Formal IS-tier evaluation methodology, terminology anchor, and PQC algorithm absorption channel | Tier 1 (Formal International Standard) | QKD evaluation + quantum-computing terminology + PQC algorithms |
| IEEE | Engineering-society quantum-computing portfolio plus PQC migration and emerging quantum-communication protocols | Tier 3 (Engineering-Society Standard) | Quantum computing |
| IRTF | Pre-standardization research on quantum-internet architecture | Tier 4 (Pre-standardization Research) | Quantum networking / quantum internet |

The **integrated architecture** that these five institutional positions jointly assemble is **four-tiered, layered, and structured around four substantive patterns**. The four substantive patterns, as Chapter 8 reconstructed and as this section now distils:

1. **Layered and operationally complete QKD/QKDN coverage**, in which four of the five SDOs (ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE) contribute item-level standardization at distinct authority tiers and the layered assurance chain from components through certification target to formal evaluation methodology is operationally complete at end-2022.

2. **Multi-tiered but pre-publication quantum-computing coverage**, in which ISO/IEC supplies the formal-IS terminology anchor (4879, CD-passed) and IEEE supplies the engineering-society stack (8 PARs at Active PAR Under Development stage) layered atop it.

3. **Distributed PQC coverage dependent on upstream NIST finalization**, in which NIST anchors the algorithm selections, ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 supplies the algorithm-standardization channel, ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC supplies the protocol-level hybrid template, and IEEE supplies the migration guidance, with full convergence dependent on post-cutoff FIPS finalization.

4. **Single-venue pre-standardization quantum-internet coverage**, in which IRTF QIRG alone addresses the end-to-end entanglement-distribution architecture at item-level deliverable specification.

The institutional equilibrium reflected in these four patterns is, as Chapter 8 observed, **structurally rooted in the constituency and heritage profiles of the five SDOs** rather than contingent on which projects happened to be initiated. ETSI's vendor-and-operator constituency naturally supports a long-running, incremental industry-specification programme. ITU-T's treaty-context constituency naturally supports network-level Recommendations with treaty weight. ISO/IEC's national-body delegation naturally supports the formal IS-tier evaluation methodology and terminology anchor that cross-border recognition requires. IEEE's engineering-society constituency naturally supports the quantum-computing engineering portfolio that the broader engineering community requires. IRTF's research constituency naturally supports the pre-standardization research function that the long-horizon quantum-internet vision demands. The architecture is, in this sense, a stable institutional equilibrium whose structural features can be expected to persist as the field's standardization continues to mature.

## 9.5 Standardization Trajectory and Forward Outlook from the End-2022 Vantage

The institutional equilibrium documented in §9.4 is not static; it exhibits four directional trajectories visible at the cutoff that signal the next phase of the global quantum standardization landscape. This section characterizes each trajectory strictly on the basis of evidence verifiable at end-2022, with post-cutoff developments referenced only as retrospective context where they confirm a trajectory already visible at the cutoff.

**Trajectory (i) — Shift toward end-to-end QKDN.** The most consolidated directional trajectory at the cutoff is the maturation of QKD standardization from component-and-link specification toward end-to-end QKDN with cross-domain interworking, observability, and resilience. The empirical evidence supporting this trajectory is multi-stranded. ITU-T's Y.3800-series consolidated rapidly across 2019–2022, with the architecture-and-control core completed by end-2020 (Y.3800/3801/3802/3803/3804/3805) and the QoS sub-series completed across 2021–2022 (Y.3806/3807/3811/3812). ETSI's SDN integration was completed in 2022 with the publication of GS QKD 015 (control, V2.1.1) and GS QKD 018 (orchestration, V1.1.1). The convergence around SDN-based control across ETSI GS QKD 015/018 and ITU-T Y.3805 represents the cleanest example of cross-SDO conceptual alignment in the report's evidence window. The under-development pipeline at the cutoff—ETSI's monitoring interface Work Item and cross-domain key distribution Work Item; ITU-T's Y.QKDN-iwfr (interworking framework), Y.QKDN-rsfr (resilience framework), Y.QKDN_BM (business role-based models), Y.QKDN_frint (QKDN-SSN integration), and Y.QKDN-ml-fra (ML-enabled functional architecture)—signals that the trajectory will continue into observability, inter-operator interworking, resilience, business modelling, and ML-based QoS through the post-cutoff period.

**Trajectory (ii) — Growing quantum-computing standardization centre of gravity.** The second consolidated trajectory is the emergence of quantum-computing standardization from project-definition to terminology baseline and engineering portfolio. The empirical evidence at the cutoff comprises: ISO/IEC 4879's CD-passage on 27 April 2022—"the first international standard project in the quantum computing field" providing the formal-IS terminology baseline; the continued post-CD comment resolution through the September 2022 WG 14 meeting; IEEE's expanding eight-project quantum-computing portfolio under C/SAB SC sponsorship with QGL coordination, spanning terminology, benchmarking, architecture, simulator design and programming, energy efficiency, hybrid integration, and algorithm design; and the September 2022 NesCom-stage placement of P3185 (Hybrid Quantum-Classical Computing) and PAR-extension submission for P7131 (benchmarking). The trajectory at end-2022 is the **progression from terminology baseline to engineering portfolio**, with the two-SDO partition between ISO/IEC's formal-IS-tier terminology anchor and IEEE's engineering-society-tier specifications consolidating as a stable institutional architecture for the quantum-computing domain.

**Trajectory (iii) — Emergence of quantum-internet protocol research.** The third trajectory is the emergence of quantum-internet protocol research as a distinct standardization-adjacent domain. The empirical evidence at the cutoff comprises: QIRG's transition from proposed RG to full RG in March 2020; sustained meeting cadence across IETF 103, 104, 106, 107, 108, and 113 (March 2022 in Vienna); the two flagship Internet-Drafts at RG Draft (active) stage approaching IRSG Last Call, with the contemporaneous chair-level assessment that they were "close to their last call which will hopefully results in the first Quantum Internet RFCs"; and QIRG's chartered coordination role with other SDOs, formally documented in its mention in the standardization-perspective literature and in the JCA-QKDN LS distribution list. The trajectory at end-2022 is the **maturation of the QIRG research output from RG Draft to forthcoming IRSG-approved Informational RFC**, positioning the quantum-internet domain for downstream engineering standardization should the underlying quantum-repeater hardware reach engineering-deployment readiness.

**Trajectory (iv) — Imminent PQC convergence anticipated downstream of NIST FIPS finalization.** The fourth trajectory is the imminent convergence of the distributed PQC architecture toward published normative outputs at the algorithm-specification tier. The empirical evidence at the cutoff comprises: NIST's July 2022 announcement of the first four selected PQC algorithms (CRYSTALS-Kyber, CRYSTALS-Dilithium, Falcon, SPHINCS+); the active ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 tracks (ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM absorbing FrodoKEM, Classic McEliece, and ML-KEM; ISO/IEC 14888-4 stateful hash-based signatures); ETSI TS 103 744 V1.1.1 (December 2020) providing the algorithm-agnostic hybrid-key-exchange template positioned to absorb NIST selections once finalized; IEEE P3172 Recommended Practice for PQC Migration and P1943 ⚠ Standard for Post-Quantum Network Security positioning the engineering-society migration tier; and the parallel IETF/IRTF ecosystem (PQUIP, CFRG, TLS, LAMPS) positioning the protocol-integration tier. The trajectory at end-2022 is the **anticipated post-cutoff convergence of the distributed PQC architecture once NIST FIPS 203/204/205 are finalized**, with each downstream SDO positioned to absorb the algorithm specifications into protocol and migration deliverables.

The four trajectories together describe a forward outlook in which **the QKD/QKDN architecture moves into operator-grade deployment maturity**, **the quantum-computing architecture progresses from terminology baseline to engineering portfolio**, **the quantum-internet architecture transitions from RG Draft to Informational RFC**, and **the PQC architecture converges to published algorithm and protocol standards downstream of NIST finalization**. None of these trajectories addresses the entirely-uncovered Category (i) gaps of §9.1 (standalone QRNG and quantum sensing/metrology) directly; closing those gaps requires either expansion of scope within existing SDOs or the establishment of new institutional venues, neither of which is yet visible as a directional trend at end-2022.

## 9.6 Priorities and Stakeholder Navigation of the Multi-Organization Landscape

The combined diagnosis of §§9.1–9.5 supports reasoned reflections on which gaps merit prioritization in the post-cutoff period and on how the principal stakeholder communities can navigate the resulting multi-SDO landscape. The reflections offered in this section are derived from the architecture documented in Chapter 8 and the gap analysis of §9.1; they do not introduce new evidence beyond the cutoff.

### 9.6.1 Prioritization Reflections

Four prioritization observations follow from the gap analysis.

**First, the quantum sensing and metrology gap** is the most structurally significant of the entirely-uncovered Category (i) gaps. Quantum-enhanced magnetometry, gravimetry, imaging, and atomic clock applications are advancing in research laboratories and increasingly in commercial settings, and their absence from any of the five surveyed SDOs' work programmes at the cutoff means that procurement, calibration, and cross-border traceability rely on metrological-organization standards (such as those developed under BIPM) and on national infrastructure rather than on the international standardization architecture surveyed in this report. IEC SEG 14's horizontal mapping function ⚠ provides the institutional touch-point closest to addressing this gap, and the post-cutoff trajectory of SEG 14's recommendations to the parent IEC Standardization Management Board will be the structural indicator of whether the gap is being addressed.

**Second, standalone QRNG specifications** represent a Category (i) gap whose practical significance is rising with the commercialization of QRNG products independent of QKD systems. Standalone QRNG products are used to seed classical cryptographic operations in a wide range of applications, and their evaluation at end-2022 relies on entropy-testing infrastructure that is not domain-specific. The structural question is whether the QRNG gap is best addressed through a dedicated programme within ISO/IEC SC 27 (where entropy testing is the natural disciplinary home) or through scope expansion within an existing ETSI ISG QKD adjacency. Neither path is yet visible as a directional trend at the cutoff.

**Third, end-to-end quantum-internet protocol work** is a Category (ii) pre-publication gap whose resolution is structurally on QIRG's chartered path. The completion of QIRG's flagship drafts as Informational RFCs (the post-cutoff RFC 9340 and RFC 9583 confirm this trajectory) supplies the architectural baseline for any downstream engineering standardization. The structural question for the post-cutoff period is whether and when the broader IETF community charters a Working Group for engineering-tier protocol specification of the quantum internet, conditional on hardware maturity, and whether ITU-T's FG-QIT4N WG1 outputs evolve into ITU-T Recommendations addressing quantum-internet network aspects beyond QKDN. Neither development is visible as a Working Group charter at the cutoff.

**Fourth, the QKD protocol normative framework (X.sec_QKD_profr)** is the most structurally significant Category (iii) partially-addressed sub-domain. Its progression from boundary-of-cutoff initiation to formal Recommendation completes the normative bridge between FG-QIT4N D2.3 Part 1's informative analytical content and QKD module certification under the assurance chain anchored by ETSI GS QKD 016 and ISO/IEC 23837. Its resolution in the post-cutoff period—along with the formal publication of ISO/IEC 23837-1 and 23837-2—will operationally complete the QKD assurance chain that Chapter 8 documented as in final crystallization at the cutoff.

The completion of the multi-SDO PQC architecture, once NIST FIPS finalization is complete in the post-cutoff period, is a Category (ii) gap whose resolution path is structurally determined and whose timing is set by upstream events outside the surveyed SDOs' control.

### 9.6.2 Stakeholder Navigation Guidance

The multi-tier, multi-SDO character of the global quantum standardization landscape imposes navigation challenges on four principal stakeholder constituencies. Each constituency benefits from a different reading of the architecture.

**Network operators** are advised to **interpret the layered QKD/QKDN assurance chain as a coordinated whole rather than as competing standards**. The chain operates as a coordinated four-tier architecture: ETSI's GS QKD family specifies components, modules, and interfaces; ITU-T's Y.3800-series specifies the network architecture and the Y.3806/3807/3811/3812 QoS sub-series specifies the QoS framework; ITU-T's X.1710-series specifies the security framework; ETSI's GS QKD 016 provides the Common Criteria Protection Profile target; and ISO/IEC 23837-1/-2 supplies the formal international evaluation methodology. An operator procuring QKDN equipment can rely on the layered architecture by requiring that procured equipment specifies conformance to the relevant ETSI GS family, deploys within a network architecture conformant to ITU-T Y.3800-series, satisfies the security framework of ITU-T X.1710-series, holds Common Criteria certification against the ETSI GS QKD 016 Protection Profile, and is evaluated using the ISO/IEC 23837 methodology. The four documents are not competitors; they are tiers of a single coordinated assurance architecture.

**Equipment vendors** are advised to **leverage the dual API and SDN integration architecture for cross-SDO interoperability** while tracking the under-development monitoring and cross-domain key distribution work items. ETSI's dual API approach—GS QKD 004 object-style API for cryptographic-application developers requiring fine-grained QoS-aware key streams, and GS QKD 014 REST/HTTPS/JSON for broader web-developer integration—allows vendors to expose QKD capability to multiple integrator constituencies through a single underlying module. ETSI's GS QKD 015/018 SDN control and orchestration interfaces, complementary to ITU-T's Y.3805 SDN control architecture, position vendors to integrate with operator SDN platforms through a coordinated cross-SDO interface set. Vendors anticipating multi-domain and multi-operator deployment are advised to track the under-development monitoring interface and cross-domain key distribution Work Items at ETSI ISG QKD and the Y.QKDN-iwfr interworking framework at ITU-T SG13 Q16/13, since these items will define the inter-domain interoperability that operator-grade deployment requires.

**Regulators and procurement authorities** are advised to **recognize the distinct authority tiers and the role of formal evaluation methodologies in cross-border procurement**. The four-tier authority hierarchy documented in Chapter 8 is not nominal but operationally significant: an ISO/IEC International Standard carries the highest formal authority through national-body ballot under the ISO/IEC Directives; an ITU-T Recommendation carries treaty-context weight; an ETSI Group Specification or IEEE Standard carries the consensus authority of its respective constituency; and an IRTF Informational RFC is explicitly non-Standards-Track. For cross-border procurement under formal certification regimes (such as Common Criteria), the structural anchor is the formal IS-tier evaluation methodology of ISO/IEC 23837, which provides the methodologically portable specification that national certification authorities can adopt independently of their bilateral relationships with ETSI or ITU-T. Regulators framing national procurement requirements for QKD modules can specify conformance to the ETSI GS family for technical content, the ITU-T X.1710-series security framework for security objectives, the ETSI GS QKD 016 Protection Profile as the certifiable target, and the ISO/IEC 23837 methodology as the evaluation basis, with confidence that the four are coordinated rather than competing.

**Researchers** are advised to **engage with IRTF QIRG's pre-standardization research tier and with ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14 and IEEE working groups for engineering-tier quantum-computing standardization**. The pre-standardization research tier represented by QIRG operates on a fundamentally different cadence and authority basis from the standards-developing tiers, and research-community engagement with QIRG is the natural channel for influencing the longer-term architectural research that will, in due course, inform downstream engineering standardization. For quantum-computing standardization, the bilateral ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14–IEEE relationship is the principal venue, with the WG 14 portfolio (ISO/IEC 4879 terminology, ISO/IEC TR 18157 overview, PWI 18660 quantum ML datasets, PWI 18670 quantum simulation platforms) and the IEEE quantum portfolio (P7130 definitions, P7131 benchmarking, P3120 series architecture, P3155 simulator programming, P3329 energy efficiency, P3185 hybrid integration, P2995 algorithm design) jointly defining the engineering-society space. Researchers in the QKD/QKDN and PQC domains will navigate principally through ETSI ISG QKD, ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC, the relevant ITU-T Study Groups, and ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 and WG 3, respectively.

The four constituencies' navigation guidance is summarized in Table 9.4.

| Constituency | Primary SDOs / Venues | Principal Navigation Insight |
|---|---|---|
| Network operators | ETSI ISG QKD; ITU-T SG13/SG17/SG11; ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3 | Layered assurance chain operates as coordinated whole; procurement should reference all four tiers |
| Equipment vendors | ETSI ISG QKD; ITU-T SG13 (Y.3805) | Dual API and SDN integration architecture supports cross-SDO interoperability; track under-development monitoring and cross-domain work items |
| Regulators and procurement authorities | ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 3; ETSI ISG QKD (GS QKD 016); ITU-T SG17 (X.1710-series) | Formal IS-tier evaluation methodology is the cross-border-portable anchor; the four tiers are coordinated |
| Researchers | IRTF QIRG; ISO/IEC JTC 1/WG 14; IEEE quantum-computing working groups; ETSI TC CYBER WG QSC; ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2 | Pre-standardization research tier (QIRG) operates on distinct cadence; quantum-computing engineering standardization is bilateral ISO/IEC–IEEE; PQC is distributed across multiple SDOs anchored upstream by NIST |

## 9.7 Concluding Observations on the State of the Global Quantum Standardization Landscape

The preceding eight chapters and the six preceding sections of this chapter have together reconstructed the global quantum standardization landscape as it stood on 31 December 2022 and have analyzed its structural features, gaps, challenges, coordination mechanisms, institutional roles, directional trajectories, and stakeholder navigation pathways. This concluding section consolidates the principal observations of the report into a final assessment.

The first concluding observation is that **as of end-2022, the global quantum standardization landscape exhibits a stable institutional equilibrium in which ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and IRTF occupy structurally distinct yet complementary niches rooted in their constituency and heritage profiles**. The equilibrium is not contingent: each SDO's centre of gravity—ETSI's QKD component-to-certification stack within ISG QKD plus PQC hybrid-protocol specification within TC CYBER WG QSC; ITU-T's QKDN operator-network architecture and security framework across SG13/SG17/SG11; ISO/IEC's formal-IS-tier evaluation methodology, terminology anchor, and PQC algorithm absorption channel; IEEE's engineering-society quantum-computing portfolio; and IRTF's upstream research on quantum-internet architecture—reflects the institutional comparative advantage that each SDO's constituency and heritage profile naturally supports. The four-tier authority architecture (formal IS, treaty-context Recommendation, industry/engineering-society standard, pre-standardization research) and the layered domain-by-domain architecture (QKD/QKDN, quantum computing, PQC, quantum internet, QRNG, quantum sensing/metrology) together describe a global system that is more coordinated than its institutional diversity might suggest and whose coordination is achieved through distributed liaison mechanisms, the consolidated JCA-QKDN hub, and informal lateral coordination forums such as IEEE's QGL.

The second concluding observation is that **the QKD/QKDN domain has achieved layered operational completeness across four authority tiers at end-2022**. Four of the five surveyed SDOs (ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE) contribute item-level standardization at distinct tiers, with the layered assurance chain from components through Common Criteria Protection Profile to network architecture, security framework, and formal evaluation methodology operationally complete at the cutoff in the sense that each tier has produced or is on the verge of producing its principal anchor deliverable. The 2019–2022 consolidation of the ITU-T Y.3800-series, the 2022 publication of ETSI's SDN control and orchestration specifications (GS QKD 015 and GS QKD 018), the October 2022 entry of ISO/IEC 23837 into the publication-preparation phase, and the boundary-of-cutoff status of ETSI GS QKD 016 together represent the structural moment at which the QKD/QKDN domain transitioned from emerging-technology specification to operator-grade standardization infrastructure.

The third concluding observation is that **the quantum-computing domain has established its foundational terminology anchor and engineering-society project scaffolding at end-2022, even as no engineering-tier project has emerged as a published standard**. ISO/IEC 4879's CD-passage on 27 April 2022 provides the cross-SDO conceptual baseline; ISO/IEC TR 18157, PWI 18660, and PWI 18670 extend the JTC 1/WG 14 portfolio; and IEEE's eight quantum-computing PARs (P7130, P7131, P3120, P3120.1, P3155, P3329, P3185, P2995) under C/SAB SC sponsorship and QGL coordination span the engineering-society scope from terminology to algorithm design. The uniformly Active PAR (Under Development) status of the IEEE portfolio at the cutoff is consistent with the field-wide pre-commercial maturity of underlying quantum-computing hardware and with the IEEE four-year PAR lifecycle cadence; the substantive scope and the institutional scaffolding are in place to support the post-cutoff progression from project definition to published standards.

The fourth concluding observation is that **the PQC domain awaits post-cutoff NIST FIPS finalization for full convergence**. The distributed PQC architecture—anchored upstream by NIST's July 2022 algorithm selections; absorbed at the algorithm-standardization tier by ISO/IEC SC 27 WG 2's ISO/IEC 18033-2 PQ-KEM and ISO/IEC 14888-4 stateful-hash-based-signature tracks; templated at the protocol-integration tier by ETSI TS 103 744's hybrid key exchanges; complemented at the migration tier by IEEE P3172 and at the network-security tier by IEEE P1943 ⚠; and operationalized at the protocol-integration ecosystem by the parallel IETF/IRTF activities—is structurally positioned to converge to published algorithm and protocol standards downstream of post-cutoff FIPS finalization. The convergence trajectory is structurally determined; its timing is set by upstream events outside the surveyed SDOs' control.

The fifth concluding observation is that **the quantum-internet, QRNG, and quantum-sensing domains remain structurally thin at end-2022**. IRTF QIRG's two flagship Internet-Drafts at RG Draft (active) stage represent the entirety of item-level deliverable specification in the quantum-internet domain across the five-SDO scope of this report. Standalone QRNG specifications are entirely absent across the five SDOs, addressed only as functional components within QKD module specifications and within the QKD module evaluation context. Quantum sensing and metrology is touched only at landscape level by FG-QIT4N's pillar 4 and by IEC SEG 14's horizontal mapping function ⚠, with no published Recommendations, International Standards, Group Specifications, or PARs at item-level specification. These three thin-coverage domains are the most structurally significant features of the residual gap landscape at the cutoff, and their resolution requires either expansion of scope within existing SDOs, establishment of new institutional venues, or, in the case of quantum sensing and metrology, engagement with metrological organizations outside the five-SDO scope of this report.

The sixth and final concluding observation reaffirms the report's methodological discipline. The comparative survey delivered in Chapters 3–8 has been conducted in strict adherence to the 31 December 2022 cutoff established in Chapter 1 and operationalized in Chapter 2. Items at the boundary of the cutoff—including ETSI GS QKD 016 with V1.1.1 dated 2023, ISO/IEC 23837-1 and 23837-2 entering publication-preparation phase in October 2022 with formal IS publication in 2023, ITU-T X.sec_QKD_profr with its earliest TD documentation in February 2023, ITU-T SG11's Q.QKDN-series "recently approved" Recommendations Q.4160–Q.4164, and IRTF QIRG's flagship drafts subsequently published as RFC 9340 (March 2023) and RFC 9583 (June 2024)—have been recorded conservatively at their highest verifiable stage at the cutoff and flagged ⚠ as required by the methodology, with post-cutoff confirmations of trajectory referenced only as retrospective context outside the primary inventory. Items whose existence and substantive scope are confirmed at the cutoff but whose precise procedural stage cannot be unambiguously verified from the consulted primary sources have been similarly flagged ⚠.

Within these methodological boundaries, the report has delivered a verified inventory of working groups, focus areas, and deliverables that satisfies the user's three core research questions for each of ETSI, ITU-T, ISO/IEC, IEEE, and IRTF: which specific working group, technical committee, study group, industry specification group, or research group is responsible for quantum technology-related work; what their thematic focus areas are; and which key standards, technical reports, recommendations, group specifications, or draft documents they have published or have under development as of the cutoff, including document numbers, titles, types, and status. The per-organization catalogues of Chapters 3–7, the cross-organizational comparative analysis and consolidated deliverables catalogue of Chapter 8, and the diagnostic and prospective synthesis of this concluding chapter together constitute the analytical baseline against which any subsequent assessment of the field's post-cutoff evolution can be conducted. The global quantum standardization landscape as of end-2022 is a system in active and accelerating maturation, with its QKD/QKDN architecture operationally complete in layered form, its quantum-computing architecture scaffolding established, its PQC architecture poised for convergence, its quantum-internet architecture at upstream research stage, and its QRNG and quantum-sensing domains awaiting future institutional treatment. The report ends where it began: with the recognition that quantum technology standardization is no longer an academic afterthought but a precondition for the orderly transition of the global digital infrastructure into the quantum era.
